PREFACE 

Just what should one include in a history of this kind? 

If one were to ask that question of each church member there would be many different answers, for many are inclined toward history while others definitely are not. Naturally, what interests some does not interest others. So we cannot and do not expect our ideas on the subject to be generally accepted as the final answer to the question. 

We believe that anything that throws light on the development and progress of the church is of historical value. But there is more to it than that. Many of the members, through no fault of their own, are not even acquainted with the names of the early preachers and lay members of the church, who they were and when and how they served. Neither do they know about many of the outstanding events that took place and when certain important changes were made, and many who knew about some of them have forgotten them. 

In view of this it seems to us that such information should be made available for everyone who is interested, so it is with this idea in mind that we have assembled the material here presented. We do not expect everyone to be interested in all the contents, but we do hope that all the contents will be of interest to some one.

The task was more difficult than we realized at first. In the first place there are no church records at all for the early years, and the first that are available are so meagre that one cannot even "read between the lines." Then, too, we know of several instances where dates were recorded incorrectly. By the aid of certain family records we were able to correct them. 

But as for the rest-well-there was nothing we could do but accept them as they are. We hope they are not too far wrong. 

Furthermore we have had to depend in great part upon personal memory, and as every one knows, memory sometimes plays strange tricks. At times we were sure we had 

VII

VIII PREFACE 

certain incidents well in mind, only to have some one contribute information that put another slant to the whole affair and we had to begin all over again. We have tried to check and recheck incidents as related by the old people and wherever it was not possible to check sufficiently we have had to forego the pleasure of relating some and dating others. 

Taking all these things into consideration it is impossible to say that no errors have crept in, but we can say that we have tried to avoid them.

We do not marvel that people do not remember more, but rather that they remember so much of the early days. It is not easy to go back into the dim past, say sixty to eighty years ago and remember incidents and remember them in detail. 

Some people have a flair for history. Such a one was the late 

C. A. Neuenschwander. If he were here today he could relate much that would be of interest to many, as could also many others who have passed on. 

It has not been possible to compile and write an all inclusive history, but we have tried to present a word picture that will better enable us to understand how the church has grown and what it has accomplished. 

In case some should think that we have been personal in certain matters, we wish to say that the contrary is true; nothing personal has been intended. We have tried to maintain an impersonal attitude throughout the book and if we have failed in this we are sorry. 

Perhaps we should have exercised modesty in telling about the work of Father. But in a work of this kind we believe there is more virtue in writing an honest record than in exercising modesty, so there was no alternative except to throw modesty to the winds. As we have gone into this history we have learned to know as never before the power of the man, the power of his indomitable spirit, his sacrificial and unselfish spirit, and we have come to the conclusion that it is almost impossible to overstate what he meant to the Mennonite Church in this community and the community at large. If it is thought that too much space has been given him, perhaps it will be

PREFACE IX 

excused upon the basis that he was the leader of the church for more than a third of its history. 

We hope, as you peruse the book, that your joy will be commensurable with ours in preparing it for you. It has been a real pleasure to get acquainted with some of the old characters that we often have heard mentioned by the older people. 

Some of them have become so real to us, it seems--almostthat we should meet them on going down the street. When we first read about some of them we naturally wondered who they were. By having Sam Lehman's chronology of early 

Mennonite families before us we were able to place them. 

But you will not have that privilege, so we have prepared a WHO'S WHO to enable you to link them up with people you know. We believe this will help you to better understand the picture we have tried to paint for you.

Too much praise and gratitude cannot be given to those who from personal memory gave freely and willingly of their time and experiences. The same must be said of the following who spent much time in research and in writing their findings. 

Without their splendid help it would have been impossible to produce this in so short a time.

INTRODUCTION 

"They rehearsed all the things God had done with them." 

Acts 14:17. This is the report of a gathering in the Early 

Church, and points to experiences of those who had been out witnessing and laboring. The following pages contain rehearsings also of things that have transpired, and of the Lord's dealings with His servants in the Mennonite Church of this community, not during one campaign of work only, but during the long stretch of one hundred years. It would take many volumes to record all that took place, even if the material were available, which doubtless it is not. Only snatches of material have been available. These have proved to be intensely interesting, and the reader will find them so. The record is a real "Ebenezer" and should stimulate to definite praise and thanksgiving. 

This book, "The First Hundred Years," is the result of only one phase of the activities of the Centenary Committee, which consists of the church Board of Deacons together with the executive heads of the organizations within the church. Other phases of activities have been the securing of special speakers and the arranging for a week of special services, extending from Sunday, September 18 to Sunday, September 25. Provision has been made for from one to four meetings daily except Saturday. The guest speakers that are to have prominent places on the program are Dr. P. R. 

Schroeder of Freeman, South Dakota, and Dr. A. Warkentin of Newton, Kansas. Beside the Bible Study Hours and the inspirational messages by these brethren, programs are to be given by the different church auxiliaries, each organization to be responsible for one evening or afternoon service. Under 

God, the Centenary Week promises to be one of rich blessing, a time of rejoicing, and of bringing praise to our God. As such we believe it will fill the desires and be a direct answer XIX

XX 

INTRODUCTION 

to the fervent prayers of the ones who were responsible for the planning for these services. 

The book is an attempt-a successful attempt, we trustto keep alive in the hearts and minds of the Mennonite people of this community the things that have transpired, and the memory of the people whom God has used to make the church what it is today; as well as to encourage them to greater faithfulness in the things of the Kingdom. Until recently there have been those within the church who have lived for a long span of years and have been able to reach back in memory to the early church leaders and to the momentous activities of the early years in the church's history. A traditional connection was thus maintained even though there were comparatively few records to which to refer. This class, however, is being thinned out very rapidly, in fact has almost disappeared. It has become necessary to put down on record in permanent, compiled form those facts that are worth preserving. Many among us, the youth especially, will warmly welcome a history of the first one hundred years of the existence of the Mennonite church in this community. 

In looking about for someone to undertake the task of gathering and compiling the material, the committee was fortunate in securing the services of Miss Eva F Sprunger.

She, perhaps better than anyone else, had access to valuable records, for her father was none other than the late 

Rev. S. F. Sprunger who had shepherded the flock for more than thirty-three years. Apart from this advantage she is possessed of an indefatigable will and determination to see things through, and has been untiring in her efforts to secure facts and general information that would be of interest in compiling the history. The fact that she has spent a number of years in active missionary service in a foreign land-Chinaundoubtedly has whetted her appreciation for the things received in the church in which she grew up. Who, more than a foreign missionary, knowing what it means to be cut off from the sacred, visible home-church ties, can feel the warm

INTRODUCTION XXI 

tugging at the heartstrings at the thought of the "Holy Days and Sabbath Bells" of the dear church in the homeland? This phase, together with the fact of the warm missionary atmosphere which the church has long maintained, all of which the undersigned deeply appreciates also, has undoubtedly had a large part in giving to Miss Sprunger a special urge to try to make the book a source of real blessing. Much credit is due her for her untiring work and her interest in seeing it come to a prompt completion. 

This book and the services connected with the Centenary festivities will cause us to realize anew what great things the Lord has done for us as a believing congregation, and what advantages are ours at present through His grace. May they be the means, also, of causing the congregation as a body as well as individuals separately to look forward and realize that "there remaineth yet very much land to be possessed," and then to undertake and do in His strength greater things, "until He come."

FRIEDA SPRUNGER BOEHR, 

China Missionary on furlough.

DOCTRINAL STATEMENT 

The Triune God-The Father, the Son, and the Holy 

Ghost. 

A divinely inspired Bible-Old and New Testamentsauthoritative and without error in the original. 

Jesus Christ, pre-existent God, one with the Father, who was made incarnate through the Virgin Birth, Who was crucified, dead, and buried, Whose body on the third day was raised from the dead, and Who through His incarnation, life, death, and resurrection, made a full and complete Atonement and satisfaction for the sins of all men, and Who ascended into Heaven, from whence He "shall so come (again) in like manner as ye have seen Him go into Heaven" (Acts 1:11). The return of Jesus Christ will be premillennial and bodily.

God made man from the beginning of Creation male and female in His own image; any theory of the evolutionary origin of the human race is false and destructive.

Through sin Adam fell, and with him all the race. (Rom. 3:23).

By nature, therefore, man is "dead in trespasses and sins" (Eph. 2:1) and his only hope for redemption and salvation is in Jesus Christ through the New Birth (John 3:3-5). 

VII. The bodily resurrection of the just and the unjust; the former shall have part in the First Resurrection (Rev. 20:5-6.)

VIII. Heaven is a prepared place for a prepared people (John 

14:1-3)-a place of everlasting bliss for those who be- XXIII

XXIV 

DOCTRINAL STATEMENT 

lieve and accept Jesus as Saviour. Hell is a place "prepared for the devil and his angels" (Matt. 25:41) to which will go into everlasting punishment all unbelievers or such as reject God's offer of salvation.

REV. C. H. SUCKAU, Chairman of Board D. J. SPRUNGER, Secretary BEN SPRUNGER, Member JERRY LIECHTY, Member E. A. LUGINBILL, Member E. W. BAUMGARTNER, Member.

In the life of any great man we begin with his birthday. 

That is his beginning. But in the life of this church our thoughts must go far away and back into the dim past. We must find out how it came to be here and why. So, for the benefit of those who do not know much about the Mennonites, and why they came to America and settled here, it seems wise at this point to give a little history of them. 

In Switzerland, in the early days of the Zwinglian and Lutheran reformation there emerged a sect called Anabaptists. 

They were a God-fearing, peaceable, earnest and industrious people. They had certain religious convictions. They did not believe in infant baptism, in taking up arms in time of war, and in taking oaths and holding state offices. They also disbelieved in the infallibility of the Pope and agreed with others in their protests against the falsities of the Roman Catholic Church. To these convictions they adhered most scrupulously and they rather suffered violence to their bodies than do violence to their consciences and their understanding of the 

Word of God.

But such convictions brought persecution by both the State Church and the Catholics. Efforts were made in places to compel them to unite with the Reformed church, but they failed. Attempts were made to exterminate them by perpetrating indescribable oppressions upon them. Some were rent into pieces by red hot thongs, others were locked up in their own houses and with everything in them burned up. 

Some were fried, hanging to pillars, many were imprisoned and allowed to starve and rot, others were drowned; many were sold as galley slaves, and their property confiscated. 

Books on Bible knowledge were denied them, and if found were burned. In fact, they suffered other tortures so numerous and indescribably cruel that they cannot be enumerated 1 1

here. Those who escaped were hunted like wild beasts and were pursued from place to place where they hid by day among rocks and in wild woods. 

It is asserted that many sang praises while they were led forth to execution. Some seized the opportunity to preach to their enemies before they were executed. The persecutors were astonished at such calmness and firmness, but pronounced it the "work of the hellish dragon."

The persecutions spread over a large territory, extending into Germany and the Netherlands, and even into Hungary, and in some sections lasted for more than two centuries. What the people endured for religious convictions' sake cannot be imagined. Surely their lot was a hard one, yet they continued to multiply, and too, they maintained their identity and their individuality though they did split into many factional organizations because of internal and doctrinal differences. 

There was one young man who was deeply impressed by the happenings about him. He was a Catholic priest by the name of Menno Simons, who was born in 1492 in the village of Witmarsum in West Friesland. He was educated for the priesthood and in 1520 when he was twenty-eight years old began the duties of his office. At that time he had very little knowledge of the Bible and no religious convictions, but the religious persecutions of the Anabaptists impressed him greatly. Gradually he came to question the correctness of some of the traditional ceremonies of the Catholic church and this led him to study the Bible with renewed vigor. He also began to study the teachings which had found their way into Lower Germany and the Netherlands. He came to realize that some of his church views were contrary to the teachings of the Bible, and later, in 1536, openly renounced the Roman Catholic Church. A year later he cast his lot with a small group of peaceful and non-resistant Anabaptists whose leaders had all been driven out of the land or put to death. He was then ordained to the ministry and became their most influential leader, and entered upon an active campaign in behalf of the new faith. His influence extended throughout the Anabaptist

communities and was such that his followers began to be known by his name. From 1544 on when the term "Menist" was first used by Countess Anne in West Friesland, the Anabaptists of the Netherlands, Germany and Switzerland were frequently called Menists, which has since become Mennonites. 

However, in Switzerland where Menno Simons' influence was not so direct and potent as in the Netherlands, the term Menist was not used so generally. There, the Swiss Anabaptists were usually called Täufer, or Wiedertäufer, or Taufgesinnte, but they recognized themselves as a part of the Mennonite body. 

During the seasons of oppression when a large number of them were driven into exile, many found refuge in the Jura mountains in Switzerland, but they were not allowed to settle in or near the cities, nor were they allowed to own their own land. All they could do was to rent the uplands which were stony and rough. Naturally, farming such land did not enable them to earn a sufficient income and many had to learn various trades.

Though these simple, harmless, cruelly-treated people had found new homes in the Jura districts, yet they had not acquired complete religious freedom. Needless to say, neither had they acquired economic freedom. And too, as years passed by, they resented the idea of doing military duty. So it is not strange that early in the nineteenth century when they heard rather glowing reports from other Mennonites who had come to America that some of them decided also to come to the land of freedom and opportunity. Among those who were forced into exile was one Ulrich 

Baumgartner, a teacher, who fled from the Pfalz in Germany into Switzerland. He was taken prisoner in 1629, but was freed later on. A descendant of his was David Baumgartner, a deacon and teacher. Deacon David Baumgartner had a son 

David, born in 1765, and it is with this younger David that this history continues. When he was twenty-three years old he became a preacher, by lot, no doubt, according to the Mennonite custom in the early days. Rev. David Baumgartner was mar-

ried three times. His eldest son by his third marriage was 

Christian who also plays an important part in this history. 

In the year 1837 Rev. David Baumgartner and several members of his family came to America and settled in Wayne 

County, Ohio. Rev. Baumgartner was then seventy-two years old and still able to preach. The children wanted to farm but land was quite expensive there so they decided to move to Indiana where government land was still available at low figures. Christian was married August 4, 1838 and he with his bride came to Indiana the 15th of the following month and settled about one mile northwest of Vera Cruz. Peter, his brother, settled two miles southeast of Vera Cruz. 

Rev. David Baumgartner, his wife and younger children followed in August 1839. Several other families came soon after and settled in French Township, in the western part of Adams County. This was the beginning of the Mennonite congregation in this vicinity and David Baumgartner was the first minister and missionary. There was no preacher that first year after Christian and Peter came, but we can well believe that they met occasionally for worship for they came from a very religious family. 

At first the people met in the homes of various members for services, but the families grew and gradually more people joined the church. The homes became too small to accommodate the growing congregation so they met in a schoolhouse where they had services once in three weeks. 

On March 18, 1845, Christian Baumgartner was chosen to assist his father. It is not known who or how many were in the lot besides him. His father was then eighty years old and after that father and son took turns preaching. The church did not believe in a paid ministry so the preachers had to do other work to earn their living. Christian was a farmer, as we have seen, his father was a bookbinder by trade. 

The little congregation grew and flourished and by 1849 sixteen families belonged to the church. However, there were other families that attended their services and some had to walk seven to eight miles to do so. Among the early Men-

nonite families besides the David, Christian, John, Peter and Sam Baumgartner families were those of John Augsburger, Peter Bixler, Christian Bierie, Isaac Falb, Abraham Gilliom, Abraham Habegger, John Hirschy, John and Peter Luginbill, Peter Merillat, John and Peter Moser, John Soldner, Christian and John Stauffer, Christian Steiner and John Suter.

The congregation again cast lot for a minister on October 20, 1850. There were five young men on the list, namely, Isaac Moser, Christian Augsburger, John and Sam Baumgartner and Peter C. Steiner. Isaac Moser was elected and installed by Rev. Ulrich Steiner of Bluffton, Ohio, but he never preached.

In March 1852 a number of Swiss Emmenthalers arrived. 

They settled in the vicinity near Vera Cruz and affiliated themselves with the Baumgartner congregation. Among them were two ministers, Ulrich Kipfer and Matthias Strahm. 

In March of that same year a colony of about eighty people left the region of the Jura Alps in Switzerland and came to 

America. Most of them were members of the Sprunger family and its relatives by marriage; nearly all were brothers, sisters, cousins, brothers-in-law and sisters-in-law. They arrived in 

Wayne County, Ohio in May, but in June and July most of them came on to Indiana and settled in the neighborhood where the town of Berne now stands. Among their number was Peter S. Lehman who had been chosen into the ministry by lot in 1848. So instead of joining the Baumgartner congregation they organized one of their own. 

In July of the following year lot was held by the Baumgartner congregation to elect an elder. The lot was among 

Christian Baumgartner, Matthias Strahm and Peter S. Lehman.

The latter was chosen and after he was ordained by 

Rev. David Baumgartner, continued as leader of the Berne congregation. Rev. Ulrich Steiner of Putnam County, Ohio and Rev. Ulrich Kipfer helped in the ordination services. It seems Rev. Kipfer had been elected elder before coming to 

America, for there is no record of his election to that office. 

A few months later, in November, Rev. David Baumgart-

ner passed away. Shortly after his death, his son Christian asked to be relieved of his obligations as minister and Ulrich Kipfer and Matthias Strahm took up the duties as leaders of the Baumgartner congregation.

In the death of Rev. Baumgartner the church lost a valuable leader. He was a man of strong Christian characteristics and clear convictions. He was a minister of the gospel for about sixty-five years, a remarkable record indeed. He lies buried in the small cemetery east of Vera Cruz.

About that time and for several years following a great storm of religious confusion passed over the section, and many of the settlers became discouraged. Rev. David Baumgartner was no longer present to hold his congregation together in a steady faith of their church doctrines. The Mennonites had a certain standard and were most conscientious about it; they were also content with it, and perhaps afraid of the idea of aiming at another. Their standard was far below the generally accepted Christian level of today. 

On the other hand, the Evangelicals who had come to the vicinity in 1853 were stressing points more important. So some of the members severed their relations with the Mennonite congregation and joined the Evangelical association.

If this religious unrest had occurred ten to twelve years later, they would have had no occasion to leave their mother church for then they would have had a leader who could have led them into satisfactory experiences. 

But there was another agitation in the church. This was due to lack of discreet leadership on the one part, or obstinacy on the other, perhaps both. Anyhow Matthias Strahm and a number of families left the congregation and organized the New Manist church. Thus there was a three-way split in the church. This happened about the year 1858. 

Rev. Kipfer then served alone until 1865 when Christian 

Augsburger was elected to the ministry. The lot was held on 

June 25 in the Baumgartner church, and was among Christian 

Augsburger, John and Peter Bixler, John Stauffer and Peter 

C. Steiner. Mr. Augsburger was ordained by Rev. Kipfer.

These two then worked together until March 12, 1866 when 

Rev. Kipfer passed away.

The congregation now had to elect an elder to take Rev. 

Kipfer's place. The lot was cast on October 21 of that same year and there were only two on the list, Christian Augsburger and Christian Baumgartner. The lot fell on the latter, much to the disappointment of Mr. Augsburger for he sought the eldership. Rev. Baumgartner was ordained by Rev. John

Moser of Bluffton, Ohio. Three years later, in 1869, the Christian Augsburger, John Stauffer, Peter Steiner and George

Fox families withdrew from the church and organized the so-called Old Mennonite congregation which met in the Schlatter schoolhouse for worship. 

The congregation had grown steadily and had built a house of worship in 1860 or thereabouts, on a plot of ground donated by Peter Baumgartner, a prominent and influential member of the congregation. There was no architect to advise them how to build but they followed the age long custom of selecting the best material when building a house of 

God; they went into the forest and picked the nicest trees they could find. To build on a pretentious scale was out of the question, but they tried to build well and when one thinks on the history of that building we know they succeeded, for it has been moved from place to place at least two times since it was sold in 1887 and is still in good condition. 

Mr. and Mrs. Peter Baumgartner

The Baumgartner Church as it appears today.

لرسولير Interior View of the Baumgartner Church

The first church in Berne built in 1856 -East 

Drawing by Arthur L. Sprunger. 

Interior view of the first church in Berne

The church was situated on the northeast corner of the Peter Baumgartner farm (located north of the Jonas Neuenschwander farm). It was a very simple structure with one door facing the east and three windows on each side. The pulpit was at the west end. There was one aisle through the center of the church, the men sitting on one side and the women on the other. The benches were not made for comfort, we are sure, but they had backs, which is more than the Berne benches had. Evidently the roof was not leak proof for whenever it snowed Peter Baumgartner and some of his boys would crawl up into the small attic and carry down bushels and bushels of snow so that it would not leak down into the church proper. Peter Baumgartner, by the way, served as janitor of the church for many years, and without pay. Jacob Steiner father of Sam-was also one of the janitors later on. 

But before pursuing the history of this congregation any further, let us go back and see what had been going on in Berne since the organization of the congregation there in 1852. 

Peter S. Lehman, as we have seen, was the preacher there. 

At first they also met in the homes of various members for worship, but as the families grew larger and as more immigrants came from Switzerland the houses became entirely too small. One old lady told the writer that her parents had to move the bedstead outside when they entertained the church services. So in 1855 the congregation decided to build a church. 

It was not a large one, only twenty-eight by thirty feet but it took quite some time to build it. The church members did the work and in all probability worked only when they had leisure from their own farm work. However, one must take into consideration the fact that they did not have machinery to do the heavy work, that it had to be done by hand. But the long delay was not due entirely to lack of time on the part of the church members. It was customary in those days to set up a building and let it stand "to settle," and it was not at all unusual to begin a house one year and complete it the next.

It was not until 1856 that the posts were ready and set

up and the building covered. The following spring the work took the form of a frolic but what they did is not easy for a woman who is not acquainted with carpenter terminology to describe. How is this for an attempt?

The eight-inch posts had been set about two and one-half feet apart and V-shaped grooves cut out. They split re-inforcements (spranzle) to fit the grooves and had the oxen knead the mud, water and straw together to make it stiff. Then the men packed this combination, with their hands, between and around the reinforcements. This they called "wickle," a cheap kind of insulation, but very effective. It dried out during the hot summer months and in the fall the church was weatherboarded, the windows were set in, and the floor and doors made. In 1858 it was ready for use and in 1860 it was plastered and considered finished. Peter and John Luginbill were experts in splitting clapboards and they did much of that work. 

In the northwest corner was a small room, attached as an afterthought, so to speak, where the women hung their shawls or bonnets (according to the season) on nails. It was not closed all around, the east side being open, and if the rain came from the northeast, it was just too bad for their wraps. 

There was only an outside entrance to this room and in winter was not easily accessible.

There were two windows on each of the north, west and south sides and one on the east. There were three doors, one on the west side for the women, one on the east side for the men, and it has been said that neither ever used the others' entrance. The third door was also on the east side, it opened to a stairway which led northward, up to the attic where the preachers met to discuss church matters. The stairway was boarded from ceiling to floor, but the outside door was not exactly in the corner, so the corner sometimes offered opportunity to young boys for a little fun. 

There was an aisle running full length east and west, and another one crossing it, north and south. There was no pulpit, but the north aisle, where the preacher stood (as near the center as possible), had railings on each side to indicate one.

At the west side of this aisle was a bench where the preachers sat, the place nearest the center aisle reserved for the senior or guest preacher. A big clock hung on the north wall directly over the aisle. The benches were crudely built, backless, and uncomfortable.

The older men sat in the northeast section facing west, the older women in the northwest section facing east. The younger men and boys occupied the southeast section facing west and the younger women and girls the southwest section facing east, in just the right place so that the young men could look right across at them. True, there was a table in the south aisle at which the song leaders sat, but we are certain they could not hide the pretty girls altogether. For the benefit of those sitting in the northwest section "log foot stools" were provided. These were mere blocks cut off of logs. 

That first church stood on what is now a parking lot, north, across the street from the Mennonite parsonage, on land (one-half acre) donated by Abraham B. 

Sprunger who then lived on what was later known as the Emanuel Liechty farm. 

For several years Rev.

Lehman was alone in the ministry in the Berne church, so on April 13, 1856 the congregation cast lot for an assistant. There was no church building as yet, so the lot was held in the home of John J. Sprunger. 

There were eight were eight candidates, Ulrich Amstutz, Christian B. Lehman, Christian Schneck, Christian Sprunger, Christian P. Sprunger, John J. Sprunger (Johannes), Peter E. 

Mr. and Mrs. Abraham B. Sprunger

Sprunger, and Peter (Pete) Sprunger. The lot fell on Christian Sprunger and he was ordained by Rev. Ulrich Kipfer. 

On March 21, 1859 a conference was held by two small Mennonite churches in southeastern Iowa which later made a lasting imprint on the local church, so it does not seem out of place to tell about it here. This conference issued a call to all Mennonites in America to join in a union movement in order that united mission work, both home and foreign, could be carried on by their united efforts. Resolutions were passed and arrangements were made to do some home mission work. On May 28, 1860 another conference was held in Iowa.

This was constituted as the first General Conference of Mennonites of America and it undertook four lines of work, For eign and Home Missions, Publication, and Education. The members of the Conference were cognizant of the fact that trained men were needed if they were to launch such a forward movment, so they decided to found a school at Wadsworth, Ohio where young Mennonite men between the ages of eighteen and thirty could obtain the requisite training. It was necessary to do some agitating and solicitation of funds, so Daniel Hege, minister of the Summerfield, Illinois church was sent out to do the necessary work. He came to Berne in 1861 or 1862 to contact the church here with the new movement, and to take subscriptions to the new Mennonite paper "Das Christliche Volksblatt." Up to that time the people here knew nothing of church papers or church news. Some people thought it a sin to read anything but the Bible. That group also opposed special training for young men, so they were much opposed to Rev. Hege and the new projects. They accused him of preaching a "new teaching." But others approved of the new movement and helped support the work of the newly organized conference, even though they did not join the conference until later.

Another matter that greatly concerned the church about that time was the breaking out of the Civil War in 1861. War is always an anxious time and perhaps even more so to a group

that takes the stand against it that the Mennonites do. When the draft was enforced in October 1862 quite a number of Mennonite men were called, not only young men, but men with families and all had to go to Wabash, Indiana for examination. That was probably the most anxious time of all, for the women did not know if the men would come back or not. 

However, they were released from their obligations of going if they paid $300 each or if they were able to secure others to go in their stead. There is no record to show who or how many were drafted. Two young men enlisted. One was John 

Jacob Baumgartner, step-son of Rev. Christian Baumgartner, who enlisted in the 44th Indiana Regiment September 19, 1863.

He was wounded, captured, and imprisoned at Andersonville, Georgia, where he died of starvation on June 22, 1864. He was not quite twenty-five years of age when he died. The other was Peter Stauffer, half-brother of Mrs. John Lehman. was captured by the Southern soldiers at Fort Smith, Arkansas.

He became ill with dysentery and was taken to the hospital 

August 20, 1864 where he died on November 13 of the same year, aged thirty-two years.

The forms of worship in the early church were simple, humble and unritualistic. And there was no set time to begin a service. They usually waited until there was a crowd, but Grandpa Soldner used to get impatient and he often said, "Es isch Ziet a z'fa." But the preacher only looked around and said, "Ja, der Johannes isch no nid da," and probably mentioned several others by name who had not yet arrived. 

No doubt, those men knew the congregation would wait for them and made no efforts to get there on time. A service lasted fully two hours, often times three and even more. The preacher always read a whole chapter for a text, then usually began with the story of Adam and Eve and went on through the Bible story to Revelation. The sermons did not deal much with a personal salvation. After the preacher had finished the sermon the "Armendiener" or another preacher or perhaps both testified that what the preacher had said was all right. They called this giving "Zuegsamme." He who gave the testimony re-

mained seated, looked neither to the right nor to the left, but kept his eyes glued mostly to the floor. Christian Lehman was Armendiener for many, many years. He almost always began in the same manner, "I cha ja und ame saega zu alles dem es gredt worde isch." He usually cried when he gave his testimony which often times lasted a half hour. The prayers were not free and spontaneous, for in the very early days they used prayer books. Whenever there was a guest preacher the minister always quoted Acts 10, 33b, "Now therefore are we all here present before God, to hear all things that are commanded thee of God."

Congregational singing has always been and is to the present time characteristic of Mennonite church worship. Even during the stormy times of bitter persecution the Anabaptist congregations made prominent use of singing in their worship. 

Many of the hymns composed by them under those trying circumstances and used for the edification and strengthening of their faith were afterwards collected and published under the title, "Der Ausbund." This book was used here in the early days, though not to the exclusion of the Psalm Book, for Psalm singing was long a part of church worship. The arrangement of the Psalms was quite different from any music of the present day. The four parts were set entirely separate and independent of each other, each having its own clef. Rhythm was discarded entirely, the notes were all held in equal length and a pause was made at the end of every line. 

They had no choirs, choristers or musical instruments in the church. They were lovers of instrumental music, but the practice to which musical instruments were commonly used in those days brought ill repute upon their usage in church services. They were regarded as sinful, worldly and of the devil. But they had some good singers and they served as song leaders. Johannes Sprunger was the first song leader in the Berne church. He sang, "fa la ut la fa," to get the pitch and then the others joined. Those who were blessed with especially good voices sat at the table with him. How many of the readers remember that table when Johannes, David,

Stephen, John and Abraham J. Sprunger, Mrs. Elizabeth 

Zuercher, Mrs. Katie Reusser and Mrs. Peter Habegger sat there? Later on, David Sprunger and J. P. Habegger also served as song leaders and Abraham Baumgartner who was song leader in the Baumgartner church for many years also served occasionally in the Berne church. The latter was a very fine singer and taught vocal music for a time, but the church did not approve of vocal music in the church. D. C. 

Neuenschwander is the one remembered by most people as song leader and he served faithfully and efficiently for the longest period of time. All in all he served about forty years. 

They were also very strict about many other things and when church members did not behave they were severely reprimanded by the church leaders. If they refused to give heed church trials were held and if the violators still remained obstinate the church councils expelled them from membership or put them on probation until they became obedient. They were not supposed to marry outside the church, but John J. 

Baumgartner, grandson of Rev. David Baumgartner, married a woman who was not a member of his church. He had violated the church rules so he had the choice of confessing or being expelled from the fold. He confessed, kept the woman, promised not to do so again and kept his word. We cannot help but wonder if they did not see the humor of the situation, for John could not honestly say that he was sorry for violating that particular church rule!

They fettered the gospel with petty and tedious, almost vexatious rules and the church members were supposed to pay strict attention to them. To say they were not religious would indeed be deviating from the truth, but it is safe to say that they placed the main emphasis upon the wrong points. 

But they were very sincere, and that, no doubt, overbalanced some qualities they lacked otherwise. 

Pride is apt to show itself most conspicuously in bodily adornment, so, in order not to be worldly, they were very slow to adopt any changes in dress. Their habits in dress. were simple, and non-conformed to the world. The women

wore long, plainly made dresses, without pleats, ruffles or lace trimming, and if the figure in the material was larger than a pin head it was just too bad. But not wearing an approved color was almost as bad. One Sunday a young woman appeared in a pretty blue dress, a slightly different shade of blue than was usually worn. It was a very plain dress, without a ruffle or anything fancy, but the shade of blue was "off color," so the preacher asked the Armendiener to ask the girl to confess. But he refused to do so because he could see nothing wrong in the matter and thus saved the day for the innocent girl.

The Peter and John Luginbill girls were usually ahead in style. Their parents were not so strict, but there were some who scolded about them a great deal, saying they were dressed too finely. That Peter Luginbill liked pretty things is illustrated by the shawl incident. One time when he took grain to the Fort Wayne market he brought his wife a pretty double woolen shawl with fringe. She wore it to church only to have the preacher's wife appear the following morning demanding that the fringe be cut off. Mr. Luginbill replied that they could put his wife from the church if they liked, but he had paid a good price for the shawl and the fringe would stay on. His wife compromised by folding the fringe up on the wrong side and tacking it so it wouldn't show. 

All the women wore aprons, but not fancy ones like ours of today. They were sombre colored and gathered very full on a band that was tied around the waist, with the ties brought around to the front and tied in the middle. One woman remembers well the time when she first shifted the bow to the side and how she had to hide it for fear some of the church leaders would see it! Some years later it was customary for the young ladies to wear white starched aprons to church and they were shorter than those worn by the older women. 

In October 1867 two sisters had a double wedding. For such an occasion they wanted their apron ties a bit fancier so they used some red thread to edge the tie ends. That really was too much and they were reprimanded for such a

breach of conduct. But more about these young ladies later. 

The women also wore neckerchiefs. These were folded in half diagonally and worn with the point down the back, the ends were brought around to the front, crossed and tucked in under the apron. A few wore fringed ones but the fringe was not prominent against the dark background, for the neckerchiefs were dark too.

Caps were essential in all public services, to conform as they believed to Paul's question, "Is it comely that a woman pray unto God uncovered?" Somehow they overlooked his teaching when he said that, "her hair is given for a covering." 

The caps were black and rather close fitting, with a black ribbon bow and streamers at the back. They usually were made of cashmere, though there were some who could afford silk ones. One young woman wanted a silk one so much that she offered to do without butter a whole summer if her mother would buy her one. The mother decided that such sacrifice deserved reward and later borrowed a pattern and made one. 

In the winter the women wore head shawls over the caps, in summer they wore bonnets, pasteboard bonnets, over them. 

Later, some lined their light colored bonnets with pink so they would have pretty complexions. Some dared to discard the caps and wear thin veils when the weather was hot. There were some who thought that women's heads should be covered whether in the home or in church, so they had little head shawls which they wore at family prayers. They even had them for guests who might not be wearing a covering on their heads.

Coats for women were unknown in the early days. They wore long capes, without collars, gathered on a band at the neck. Shawls came into style a bit later and at first fringed ones were considered too worldly, so they were very plain. 

Those of you who remember Grandma Bixler, mother of the late David Bixler, and "Bierli Eisi," mother of Jeff

Sprunger, and can visualize them, have a perfect picture of the early style in dress. To the time of their death, in 1913 and 

1918 respectively, they continued to wear plain dark dresses,

with the neckerchiefs and aprons, the black close fitting caps with the bows and streamers down the back and the bonnets and shawls. But these seemed to suit their personalities. We cannot imagine them in any other garb. 

The women were supposed to comb their hair straight back, without a parting. On one occasion when some girls in the Berne church were to be baptized they had combed their hair with a parting in the middle, and the preacher refused to baptize them until after they combed their hair in the approved style.

At funerals of relatives and when taking Communion the women had to wear small white head shawls. But their aprons and other articles of clothing also had to conform to certain rules for taking Communion. One time a woman went to church, forgetting that it was Communion Sunday (which was on Easter Sunday for many years). When she arrived she remarked, "Oh, now I am wearing the wrong apron." It is not known if she was allowed to partake of the 

Communion or not.

But the men also came in for their share of peculiar homemade clothes. They wore low crowned, broad brimmed woolen hats, and their trousers had an entirely different cut from the ones of today. Their coats were also cut differently and hooks and eyes were used instead of buttons. There were no turned down collars on their coats, but we are sure some of the men yearned for them. The two young men who married the two sisters mentioned before dared to have them on their wedding coats! But if they thought they could do such a daring thing and get by with it, with no intervention from the "church fathers," they expected too much, for they had to go upstairs where they were dealt with in no uncertain terms. The young ladies waited and waited and at last began to wonder if they were going to be married or not. Somehow the difficulties were overcome and they were married and lived happily ever after. But the preacher who married them was taken to task by another one who thought the former should confess because he married the two couples who did

not conform to the approved style of dress, but that he refused to do. 

How many of you remember when the men began wearing long knitted scarves in winter? They had to confess for that, if you remember. And do you remember Grandpa Soldner's variegated one which he wore many, many years? 

The men too had to wear their hair in an approved fashion. They were allowed to wear beards but they had to be cut a certain way. To get an idea of the style you need merely to observe the way the Old Amish men wear theirs. 

But a mustache was absolutely taboo, perhaps because of the military appearance. When anyone saw a man with a mustache he at once concluded that he was an officer in the army. 

The clothes problem was indeed a very large one in both churches, but it did not assume the proportions in the Baumgartner church that it did in the Berne church. In fact the members of the Berne church used to accuse the members of the Baumgartner church of being "stuck up." But no matter who ventured to wear or do something new, they always came in for a great deal of criticism. For instance, when Mrs. 

Jeff Lehman's twin sister died in 1864, Rev. Lehman started to sing a song at the funeral. That was the first time there was singing at a funeral and some of the old folks did not mince words in criticism of him. And as late as 1879, when 

Johannes Sprunger started to sing a song at the cemetery, when AnaLisa Sprunger was buried, it also was an occasion for much criticism on the part of the old conservative group. 

We wonder what this group said when Grandma Soldner quit wearing the white head shawl for Communion! She said it did no good, and anyhow the women only vied with one another to see who could have the whitest one. So it was she who had the courage to discard it, and others followed her example and in time it was a thing of the past. 

As we have said before, Communion services were, for many, many years held on Easter Sunday. A special service was always held on Good Friday and the business meeting followed. However, the younger people were not allowed to

remain for that, and no matter how cold the weather, they had to wait outside for their elders. A great deal of fussing took place and most of it was about clothes and they were told what they should wear for the Communion services the following Sunday. The meetings often lasted until late afternoon, but the discussions were mostly about non-essentials. 

It seems the religion of those days reached little higher than the observance of set rules regarding clothing, and clothes did nothing to bring them nearer to God. They concerned themselves too much with things that looked religious. Their ideas about clothes never could make them clean in spirit, thought and deed. They overstressed that particular point to the neglect of something more important. But there are usually exceptions, and as we find them in almost everything in life, so do we find them here. We know of instances where no work was done on Good Friday or Ascension Day and the mothers took time to explain to the children the meaning of the holy days. Surely religion meant more than mere observance of rules to those mothers. In some families there were morning and evening prayers, and on Sundays the children were not allowed to go to the woods to play or to go visiting until after they had read a chapter out of the Bible. But those children soon learned where to find the shortest chapters! 

Some of the young girls and boys used to go to the home of Christian F. Stauffer, a school teacher, on Sunday afternoons. Mr. Stauffer had brought an organ when he came here in 1853 and the young people loved to watch him play. It was the first organ in the vicinity and it created much interest for the young people. They thought it great fun the way he ran his fingers over the keys, and they had many a good "sing" there.

Generally speaking, the people were quite sociable. The story is told of a man who had no wagon, who, when he wanted to go visiting hitched his oxen to the sled, even in summer.

But when the weather was unfavorable visiting was out of the question, for then the roads were well nigh impassable. 

In that case they had to walk. But rainy weather during the

22 

summer months did not keep the young people at home for they usually walked anyhow. If it was wet underfoot they carried their shoes and stockings until they reached their destination, then they put them on. It was not at all unusual for young men and young women as well as boys and girls to go barefooted to church and no one looked askance at them for doing so, nor at the men when they wore rubber boots to church. Even the preachers wore boots, sometimes with one trouser leg over one boot and the other one hiked above the other one, or tucked inside of it. In fact, there was one man who in later years often taught his Sunday School class in bare feet. It would have been better if the present length of women's dresses had been in vogue in the early days, for they had to do so much walking through dust and mire. But if the dresses had been shorter the ladies would hardly have gone visiting or gone to church or to the "sings" barefooted.

But walking was the order of the day. A few rode horseback to church—but almost everyone walked, old as well as young, and some had to walk several miles to church, many carrying babies. Many who had horses and wagons and later on buggies walked because they felt it was not right to make their horses work on Sundays after a week of hard work. They felt more sorry for the horses than for their wives! One woman showed us how they made beds out of pillows in which they carried the babies to church. As long as the babies slept they reposed quietly in their soft beds, but when they woke up the beds were laid aside on the bench or on the floor. 

At first it was customary to have only home weddings and they were usually held on Thursday evenings, sometimes on Saturday evenings. Then later on, for a period of about thirtythree years, church weddings were the rule, and the newlyweds walked home after the wedding. There were a few who had buggies, by 1880, but very few.

But we must turn to more serious things. In 1865 another preacher in the person of Peter Habegger came with his family from Switzerland. He settled in this community so he assisted in the ministry to the time of his death May 15, 1872. He was

generally well liked, he was very fond of children and they in return loved him.

Late in March 1868 Rev. Lehman with his family and a few others moved to Elkton, Missouri where he started a little congregation. But before he left, the congregation cast lot for an elder to take his place. The lot was held on March 

17 and was between Peter Habegger and Christian Sprunger.

It fell on the latter and he was ordained by Rev. Lehman. 

There are no records to tell of the work of the early preachers who counted for so much in the beginning of the Mennonite church. If they who did the noble work could be here now and in turn graphically tell us of their experiences, what a life of adventure we should have! Instead, we have to content ourselves with the bare passing mention of those early days.

After Rev. Lehman departed for Missouri the congregation decided to elect another minister. The usual method of electing by lot was followed, but it was the last time in the history of this church. As was customary, a special Sunday was designated to elect the minister after which the members of the congregation had the privilege of nominating anyone as candidate for the lot, and the nominations were continued until closed by a motion to that effect. That time the designated date was August 23, 1868. On that morning Christian B. Lehman, the Armendiener, gathered seventeen or eighteen books, Bibles or Testaments and song books, with clasps, and set them on the table. In one of the song books, "Der Ausbund," one belonging to his wife, he placed a slip of paper on which was written, "Herr aller Herzen Erkenner zeige an Welcher du erwählet habest." There were seventeen young men in the lot that time and they were supposed to pass by the table and each select a book. If they were to pass in review before us now we would see, Peter Burkhalter, John Gilliom, Abraham Lehman, Peter Lehman, John P. Luginbill, Jacob Nussbaum, John 

Nussbaum, Abraham Schneck, John Soldner, Abraham A., Christian P., Jacob, John J., John P., Peter (Pete), Peter A., and Samuel F. Sprunger. When Samuel F. Sprunger, better

known as "Sammie" passed by he picked up a book, set it down and picked up another one. That one act changed the entire course of the future history of the church in a most unexpected MOR "Der Ausbund" which "Sammie" picked up 

August 23, 1868. It is now in the possession of Mrs. Menno Eicher of Berne manner, for by selecting the second book, "Sammie" was chosen as the new minister and he was then installed by 

Rev. Christian Baumgartner. 

Much has been said from time to time in criticism of selecting a minister by lot, but surely the lot as it was cast that day was not an accident. It is generally conceded that it was the purpose of God that Samuel 

Ferdinand Sprunger was chosen. 

We know of three people who nomi- nated him. One was his sister Elizabeth (Mrs. C. P. Sprunger), another was the second wife of his brother-in-law, Mrs. Abraham A. Sprunger, and the third was Mrs. John C. Nussbaum (formerly Regina Oberli). Some said his name was entered because he had the "gift of gab," but be that as it may, Sammie was not happy to be among the candidates. 

He was working at Sam Lehman's at the time, and on the morning of that eventful Sunday Mrs. Lehman asked him what he would do if the lot fell on him. He replied that he would go away, that he would not accept it. Some have said that whenever anyone asked him what he would do if he

were elected he replied that he would go away to study. It is quite probable that as the time drew near he felt like running away, that the very idea of it almost overwhelmed him.

But he accepted it as the will of God and almost right away entered into the spirit of it with all his heart and soul and he became determined to prepare himself for his divine calling. 

The church did not believe in an educated ministry, but Sammie would not consider his calling without special training. The Wadsworth school had been completed and was ready for occupancy by autumn of 1866, but no qualified teachers seemed available, so this delayed the opening of the school until January 1868. Naturally, Sammie turned his longing eyes toward this school. It seems that everything was working out according to a plan, "And we know that all things work together for good to them that love God, to them who are the called according to His purpose"; but the members of the Berne congregation thought otherwise. Those were trying days for "Sprunger Sammie." He wanted to go away to study, but with few exceptions, the congregation opposed the plan. In fact, most of them thought that now that he was in the ministry, though not yet twenty years old, he should conduct himself like the older people. Hunting was a past time which he loved, but he was severely criticized when he went squirrel hunting with some friends. But Rev. Christian

Baumgartner upheld him in his desire to go to school, and it was finally decided that he should go.

It was certainly not an ordinary matter for a Mennonite lad of those days to go to school, so we can well believe that the day he left for Wadsworth was a red letter day for him. 

His close friend, Jerry Luginbill, drove him all the way to school with his father's horse and buggy.

The first impression on entering school had a depressing effect upon him. At home he was used to seeing the preachers clad on Sunday in swallow tailed coats, fastened with hooks and eyes, and a standing collar; at Wadsworth he found his teachers wearing coats fastened with buttons, and with lay down collars, and their other apparel in harmony with what

men wore in general at that time. At first he wondered what he could possibly learn from that group. He was not at all happy, and repeatedly felt like running away, but he wisely decided to hold on until after the arrival of the new teacher. 

If the incensed members of the Berne congregation had known how matters stood, they, no doubt, would have said, "Serves him right, he should have known better than to go off to school." But soon after, he found to his joy and satisfaction that his tutors were far ahead of him in Biblical knowledge and practical Christianity and concluded it was the place for him. Still later he was grateful for that period in the school which was one of the greatest moulding influences in his life. 

It was there that a flame was kindled within him, and he kept that fire burning; the fire in his soul never was extinguished. 

He often said afterwards that that was the time when he really began to live.

In the spring of 1870, when Sammie had been in Wadsworth about a year and a half, he returned home for a short vacation. The time was Good Friday. As the people went to church they were anxious not only to see him, but also to hear what he had to say. But when he appeared they were totally unprepared for what they saw. He carried a Bible under his arm, something most extraordinary, and he was utterly changed in appearance. No longer did he wear his homemade coat with a standup collar. His coat was too long and it didn't have the right cut. He wore a stiff white collar and a white shirt with a black stripe. And he wore a supposedly silver watch chain, a very thin thing about ten inches long that had cost him fifty cents. That really was enough excitement for one day, but more was to follow. He was asked to give the testimony that day, and as we have said before, the one who gave it always remained seated. But not Sammie, he shocked the audience by standing. The congregation kept silent, it was a breathless moment. Then he astonished them again, for he no longer spoke in his mother tongue! His testimony must have been of a different nature also, for he spoke on Isaiah 53. He must have sermonized a bit. It was something

entirely new to hear a student and the youth of the church enjoyed it. He made a good impression on them. It did not take long for them to realize that they were hearing something out of the ordinary. But the old patriarchs of the church thought it sheer pride. They were more concerned about clothes than with the contents of a sermon and what Sammie gave them that day was more of a sermon than they were accustomed to hearing.

After Sammie stood up to speak many eyes turned toward his fiancee who was probably having palpitation of the heart at that moment. They wanted to see how she was "taking it." 

Evidently she was master of her emotions and conducted herself admirably, for she was afterwards complimented on how she had succeeded in keeping calm under such-shall we say trying-circumstances. She heard women whispering all around her, saying, "He only stood up to show himself." But it is no wonder they were stirred, for after all, that was a pretty fair amount of excitement for one day. 

Generally speaking the people were more opposed to him than ever, but those who favored him said, "Sam can stay here, he need not return to school, for he can already preach good enough." But he replied that he had only just learned that he knew nothing, that it was therefore very necessary that he prepare himself better, and now that he had the opportunity to do so he would avail himself of it. So he went back to school and on the part of the old patriarchs there was more talking than thinking. They knew he would not stick to the old order and they were afraid he would cause the downfall of the church. One old man thought Sammie represented the beast spoken of in Revelation and that they needed to beware of him, that they should fight him, in fact. 

The next year was soon up and Sammie was graduated from the Academy. When he returned home to take up his duties as a minister he realized that certain methods and changes were necessary. The old idea of religion was proper dress. He stressed change of heart and righteousness. He tried to make it clear that Christianity is not a religion of

rules but one of divine example. He felt he had a great task to bring his congregation nearer to God, a worth while and noble incentive. But the older people looked askance at his daring revolutionary methods. Everyone was uneasy from the start, it was like starting out on an open angry sea, not knowing what the end might be, and the oncoming tempest was even greater and of longer duration than they anticipated. 

The basic fact is this, the fight was not for religion, and jealousy did not enter into the matter at all. It was a case of one personality against others' opinions. It was not upholding any Biblical fact or truth. They did not approve of an educated minister, his change of language and change of style in clothing. But we must be honest with them and admit that they were honest in their belief that they were right in sticking to the old order. It was no selfish quarrel as to who should gain something. It was just a difference as to the best way of serving the church. So the church divided into two temporary factions with one adopting a more liberal church policy. 

It is a very sorrowful story but very human. The opposing faction made things most difficult for Sammie but he exercised his right as a minister and took command, even though there was hard feeling against him. The hour of uncertainty shows up cowards as well as heroes and here was his chance to show what he was. The opposing faction became so incensed that they would not let him preach in the church, but Christian Baumgartner and his congregation took kindly to him and invited him to go to their church and preach on alternate "Der Sam" as he appeared about 1871

Sundays. Some of the younger people of the Berne church stuck to him and affiliated themselves with the Baumgartner church. Then they demanded that Sammie be given the use of the church on alternate Sundays when Christian Sprunger did not preach, so it came about that on one Sunday he preached in the Baumgartner church and the next in the 

Berne church. 

It is not definitely known when Sammie preached his first sermon, but the one dated July 9, 1871 is the earliest one in his file of sermons. The text was: "Ho, every one that thirsteth, come ye to the waters, and he that hath no money; come ye, buy, and eat; yea, come, buy wine and milk without money and without price. Wherefore do ye spend money for that which is not bread? and your labor for that which satisfieth not? hearken diligently unto me, and eat ye that which is good, and let your soul delight itself in fatness. Incline your ear, and come unto me: hear, and your soul shall live, and I will make an everlasting covenant with you, even the sure mercies of

David." Isaiah 55:1-3. 

Both sides were equally determined and in the face of this difficulty how could one ever expect a united church? 

His opponents had their deep convictions, but so had he, so the alternative was to fight and fight it to a finish. But the word "fight" here must be understood. It was not a battle of words or physical strength. Perhaps Sammie's most effective weapon was his calm and quiet disposition. Somehow he had acquired the secret of being confident and calm through it all. 

But the solution seemed impossible and Sammie became so discouraged he wanted to quit and go elsewhere. No doubt, it was a passing fit of depression such as we all have at times. 

They do not do us much harm provided they come seldom. 

It is human and Sammie was very human. Had it not been for a certain woman he probably would have gone. Sammie had married the girl mentioned before, the year after he returned from school. His mind was made up to go some place where the people were not so narrow-minded. She, however, took her stand and said she would not go with him, that he

could not be certain he would not have to go through the same thing elsewhere, that after all the thing to do was to fight it out here. The question has often been asked, "What would have become of the church if it had not been for 'der Sam'?" 

Perhaps we are justified in asking, "What would have become of 'der Sam' if it had not been for the good judgment of his wife?"

But this question is fair, "If Sammie had not insisted on an education what would the church be like today?" It is hard to conjecture, but one need not go far to see examples of what it might be like if they had stuck to the old order. 

It was sorely in need of new ideals and standards. They needed to make contacts with other churches and they needed to learn the art of tolerance. If no higher lights had come in from above to rouse them to nobler things it is more than likely that the church would have been lulled into a deadly slumber.

Sammie made up his mind to stay but he was determined to stick to his convictions. But he was also sympathetic. He urged those who went to hear him to attend Christian Sprunger's services the following Sunday when he preached in the "lower" church. He was called "der chlien Spitzbub" by some of the opposing faction and even though they did not go to hear him, they wanted to know what the "Spitzbub❞ said.

Today as we look back on results we can say that perhaps the split was worth while, in order that a more tolerant church might evolve. At any rate it is certain that without the intervention of God's grace on His servant it would have been impossible. But God gave wisdom, discretion and necessary firmness and through His grace Sammie became the instrument in accomplishing a work necessary to the existence of a stronger united church.

It was not all smooth sailing; there were those who were so indignant that they left the church and moved elsewhere, all because of Sammie. But people are leaving churches even today because they cannot have their own way. Human nature expresses itself in many ways, even in churches. Now-

adays when things go wrong we are often pointed back to the delightful days of long ago, but these experiences take the glamour off the idea that all went well in the long ago. 

Much was gained in having a determined young preacher.

Today there is no one who will gainsay that point. Sammie's going away for an education had a good effect on the young people and later several other young men went to Wadsworth to school. Among them were Eman uel J. Liechty, C. A. Neuen schwander, Abraham S. Lehman, John A. Sprunger (Hämi Hans),

Abraham S. Lehman and David C. Lehman. 

Both Rev. Kipfer and 

Rev. Christian Baumgartner preached in the 

Berne church at times, but their responsibilities were confined to the 

Baumgartner church. S. 

F. Sprunger, however, served two congregations regularly, and while that would not be so difficult today it was no easy matter in his day. The two settlements, Münsterberg, as the Berne settlement was called, and the 

Bäraschwamm, as the

Emanuel J. Liechty, David C. Lehman 

John A. Sprunger, C. A.

Neuenschwander country settlement was

22 

called, were too far apart to make the work easy. He had to walk much of the time, especially when the roads were not fit for travel, and he had to walk across fields and woods or along fences. In fact, in the early years of his service he had no alternative but to walk, for he had no wagon or buggy. 

So, much time was spent in walking from one settlement to the other. His home was about midway between the two churches. 

One Sunday he walked to the Baumgartner church for services. 

The next Sunday he had morning services in the Berne church. 

The two churches were from six to seven miles apart, so Rev. 

Sprunger had plenty of exercise! 

In 1872 S. F. Sprunger's two congregations joined the 

General Conference and he represented them at Wadsworth that year with one vote. Their interest in missions and general church news grew rapidly from then on. 

Der Sam, as he was now called, tried to point out the unimportant place clothes should hold, that in some places frock coats were worn, in other places another kind, and that after all the condition of the heart was the important thing. 

Knowing his attitude along that line the people gradually changed their styles, conforming more and more to those of the general style of the period. By 1875 a bride had the courage to wear a violet colored dress in basque style, with a row of buttons down the front. She also wore a pretty small black silk cape over her shoulders, and this time there was no criticism from the preacher. 

When Sam Baumgartner's two daughters, Mary and Caroline, members of the Evangelical church, were allowed to wear low-crowned, narrow brimmed hats with a narrow blue ribbon around the crown, the news spread like a wild prairie fire over the community. Hands were help up in horrified surprise. 

Waggy tongues declared, "Das isch groszer Hochmuth," "das isch vom Duefel," "dem Sammy Baumgartner sini Meidli göh zum Duefel so gschwing as er si cha hi bringa." Rev. Sprunger had no objections to hats but he did not dare suggest them. 

Several years after his return from Wadsworth, his good friend

C. J. van der Smissen came to Berne with his wife and two daughters on a visit. The ladies wore hats, consequently the ladies here thought, "If they can wear hats, why can't we?"

Shortly after, several of the women here bought hats. There were some in the Baumgartner church who wanted to wear hats too, and somehow let it be made known that they were going to do so, that they were tired of laundering their ruffleedged bonnets. Rev. Baumgartner said they would have to take them off if they did, but that time the women got ahead of him. Finally in the late seventies Abraham Baumgartner made the motion in a business meeting that the ladies be allowed to wear plain hats, for it was considerable work in a family where there were several girls to keep the bonnets laundered properly. Some objected because of the expense involved. They said it meant buying winter hats too-and so it did-and has continued so to the present day-only now it is three to four a year for some! One lovely old lady wanted to conform to the new style of wearing hats, yet was not willing to give up her close fitting black cap, so she compromised by wearing both at the same time. No doubt many remember how funny the little old lady looked! 

Mrs. Peter Habegger (Without her hat)

During the early history of the church, when nearly everybody kept wine and cider in the home and at almost every social gathering a little brown jug or red pitcher was in evidence, no one looked askance on anyone, not even on church members, no, not even on the preacher for visiting the public drinking places. Christian Baumgartner was known to 2

stop at the Vera Cruz saloon on his way to church when he preached, but it was not held against him as long as he drank moderately. At weddings it was not unusual to have the bottles right on the wedding tables. There were a number of 

The Abraham Baumgartner Family devoted souls who abhorred the custom, and S. F. Sprunger was among the first to both privately and openly preach the doctrine of temperance. He preached strongly against the patronizing of saloons. In fact he preached total abstinence every time the opportunity presented itself. Once at a wedding in 1879 some one opened a jug of wine and offered him some. He right then and there preached a sermonette on temperance. That was like him, he was not going to let an opportunity like that slip by. There were some who drank to excess and according to the records the church tried to deal with that problem by witholding Communion from them until they mended their ways.

The first recorded business meeting was held October 7,

1877, in the Baumgartner church with S. F. Sprunger serving as chairman and Abraham Baumgartner as secretary. On that date Peter Bixel, Abraham Baumgartner and Jacob Leh 

Jacob Lehman teaching Emily Schneck the Golden Text man were elected deacons. Mr. Bixel had twenty-four votes, Mr. Baumgartner eighteen, and Mr. Lehman sixteen. According to some there were deacons before 1877 but they do not remember when the first were elected or how many there were. 

In that same month S. F. Sprunger was elected delegate to the 

Western District Conference held at West Point, Iowa. Here again it seems expedient to give a little Conference information. "In 1868 there was a great demand for ministerial assist-

ance in small and new churches. Desirous of doing something toward satisfying this demand several churches in Iowa and Illinois formed a union. Christian Krehbiel first suggested 

Peter Bixel such a Conference and it was upon agitation by him that the first session was called October 4, 1868 at Franklin, Lee County, Iowa. The organization was named Western District Conference of the Mennonites of North America. After these small churches had labored on alone for seven or eight years other churches began to join them, the church in Berne being the first to unite. By 1888 there were so many co-operating churches (thirty in all) and these were scattered over so wide a territory that the organization was unable to satisfy the various. local interests. Prior to this, a local conference had been organized in Kansas, so the Western District, being no longer the

most western one, decided to discontinue the organization with the understanding that every church wishing to do so could unite with the Kansas Conference, and the others would form a new organization. So they met and organized another Conference and the name 'Middle District Conference' was adopted."

As the above paragraph states, the Berne church consisting of S. F. Sprunger's two congregations, was the first to unite. 

That must have been in about 1876 and in November 1878 the two congregations entertained the Western District Conference in the Baumgartner Church. There was a typhoid fever epidemic on just then and Grandma Bixler (Mrs. John) remarked, "No doubt the meetings were very good, but it would have been more to the point to look after the sick, they are more important."

Rev. Christian Baumgartner had passed away shortly before the Conference. He and S. F. Sprunger had been close friends and had worked together harmoniously. The latter felt his loss keenly and he was left to serve the two congregations alone.

One wonders why no records were kept before 1877. Perhaps there was not much of an organization in the church. 

We are prone to believe there was not, but the year 1877 introduced the first of many changes and innovations that were to follow.

The Christian Sprunger congregation did not follow their example. They continued in the same simple manner in which they began as a congregation in 1852. They made no changes in their form of worship or in their church organization. They had only one office besides the minister, that of 

Armendiener-deacon, and he who was elected held the office for life. Besides taking care of the poor fund he announced the couples who wished to marry. He was also responsible for the care of the church building, but he did not do much of the cleaning. That task fell to his wife and daughters, and it was Mrs. Lehman who usually went early winter morn-

ings to build the church fires. Mr. Lehman, no doubt, was busy doing the chores on the farm. But he was also a carpenter and he officiated as coffin maker and undertaker for the congregation.

The first record of trustee election was on May 31, 1879 when Sam Lehman with thirty-six votes, Peter Bixel with twenty-three votes and Abraham Baumgartner with nineteen votes were elected. The names indicate that these men served both of S. F. Sprunger's congregations, for Peter Bixel and 

Abraham Baumgartner were members of the Baumgartner church.

There were some young people who attended church services of both Berne congregations, so, when in 1879 S. F. 

Sprunger's Berne congregation built a new church they too helped a little in a financial way even though they were not members. But the new building was another bone of contention, some of Christian Sprunger's members thought it much too stylish and they criticized S. F. Sprunger most unmercifully. One of these was Peter M. Neuenschwander, a young minister who had come from Switzerland in 1876 with other members of his family. He had become assistant to Christian 

Sprunger and was the main leader of the opposing faction. 

The church was dedicated November 9, 1879 with Rev.

Peter S. Lehman here from Missouri to preach the dedicatory sermon. Rev. A. B. Shelly and Rev. Christian Krehbiel were also present. On that Sunday the members of both congregations could hear each other sing. Let us hope they were not singing suggestive feelings antiphonally! Mr. Lehman wore a "store suit" and that did not take well with many of the people who accused him of "grosser Hochmuth."
