But the members of the Christian Sprunger congregation also wanted to hear him, his store clothes notwithstanding, so he was invited to preach to them the following Sunday morning. The members of S. F. Sprunger's congregation wanted to hear him again so they also went to the old church that Sunday. So there was a large crowd and not enough seating room for all.

North

The 1879 Church 

I j 

Interior View of the 1879 Church

Then Sam Lehman suggested that they all go over to the new church where there was plenty of room, and with that Rev. 

Lehman picked up his hat and walked over to the new church without so much as asking if all agreed to the proposition. 

Christian Sprunger was not opposed to the new church but had been unduly influenced by Peter Neuenschwander. It was a difficult situation but Christian Sprunger was equal to the occasion and went over. Many followed but there were those who did not know what to do. Some sat on the fence (we are speaking literally), and others walked around crying, also undecided. Among those who stayed behind were Peter 

M. Neuenschwander and his relatives, Abraham Moser, Jacob, son of Rev. Peter Habegger, Peter Mazelin and their families, also Pete Sprunger, half-brother of S. F. Sprunger, and a few others. 

Feeling ran high that Sunday morning, November 16, and every one who remembers anything at all about the occasion reports a sorrowful story. But the break was made and a fortunate thing it was, for it was the first step toward a united church, even though the consummation was not to take place until seven years later. Perhaps the break was easier because of something that had happened the previous week. A granddaughter of Rev. Christian Sprunger had died and the funeral services were held in the new church because the old one was too small. So the going over that 

Sunday was easier, no doubt, because they had already taken the first step several days before.

After that they used the church on alternate Sundays when S. F. Sprunger preached in the "lower" church, and many of Christian Sprunger's members then made payments to help finance the new church. The following year David

Sprunger bought the old church for $25.00, and with the lumber he built a number of horse sheds at the new church which stood for thirty years.

The new church was built on land, consisting of one and sixteen hundredths acres, purchased from Peter Lehman for

sixty dollars and stood directly south, across the road from the old one, facing north. It was forty feet wide, sixty feet long and seventeen feet high. It too had separate entrances for men and women and in the middle of the church there was an extra aisle so the benches would not be together and thus they could not sit side by side. The pulpit was at the south end and a narrow gallery over the entrance. The "baby" room was in the northeast corner, off the foyer. There were two entrances in the foyer leading into the church proper in line with the outside ones, and the foyer was wide enough to allow coffins to be placed there. This was a change from the method in the old church. The cemetery was right beside the old one and whenever there was a funeral the burial took place first and then the services followed in the church. Some time after the new church was built the coffins were placed in the foyer between the two entrances and the men and women walked out simultaneously to view the corpse. 

Isaac Lehman 

In the business meeting of

December 1879 Isaac Lehman was officially appointed as janitor, though he had been serving since the dedication. Some thought he should do the work free of charge, but C. A. Neuenschwander came to his rescue. 

He said he had done janitor work when he was in school in Wadsworth and he had not found the work easy, and he felt there should be some remuneration for the work. So the congregation agreed to pay Mr. Lehman $20 per year. At the same time Daniel Z. Sprunger agreed to furnish the wood for the church stoves at $.85 per cord. 

Isaac Lehman went to Ohio so in 1880 Daniel Z. Sprunger

was appointed janitor at $25 per year, and C. W. Baumgartner contracted for the wood at $1.10 per cord. That same year 

C. A. Neuenschwander was appointed correspondent to "Zur Heimat."

Early in 1881 Ulrich Lehman was elected janitor and he had to furnish brooms and oil for the lamps, getting only $25 per year. This corresponds with the time when Sunday evening services were begun and lamps were necessary. However, when Mr. Lehman was re-elected the following year at $30 he did not have to furnish anything. 

When the two congregations met December 27, 1882 they voted to have a union service January 1, 1883 when they would celebrate the Lord's Supper together. Evidently that was the first union service of its kind. 

No one seems to remember the exact year, but it is generally conceded that it was in the late seventies that the midweek meetings for Bible study were begun. The older people did not approve of them at first. Some thought it was being too much like the Evangelicals. Others said they were merely an excuse for the younger people to get together, so they could walk home together. How many years later was it that the parents fussed because their children would not attend the 

Wednesday evening prayer meetings?

The catechetical classes had been organized for many years and were a feature of both churches. They met in the homes of the pastors. But S. F. Sprunger's home was not within easy reach of the Berne peope. So his father opened his home for classes, and many remember meeting in the home of "der alt Mann" for their catechetical instruction. S. F. Sprunger, as every one knows, always insisted on sticking to the exact truth. 

Once, when in one of his classes the subject was truth he remarked, "Sagt nie ihr wollt nicht heiraten, ihr habt's doch im Sinn." Needless to say he had heard what the young men were talking about in their idle moments.

In November 

1884 S. F. Sprunger's congregations entertained the General 

Conference in Berne for the first time.

The Christian 

Sprunger congrega- tion sent fraternal delegates in the persons of their minister and Christian B. Lehman. 

The younger people did not like the idea of having two separate congre- gations in Berne, so constantly pushed the idea of union. As time went on more of more and Christian "Der Alt Mann"

Sprunger's members attended S. F. Sprunger's services and the points of difference gradually grew less. The younger group appointed Isaac Lehman to call on Christian B. Lehman to see what could be done about the matter, but the latter thought that the suggestion to unite should come from S. F. 

Sprunger, that after all he was the younger preacher and he should ask to unite with the senior pastor's flock. 

But this matter was settled in quite another manner. In 

1886 the church was again too small and both congregations agreed to join in the alteration plan. The building committee was composed of Philip Sprunger, Christian B. Lehman, John 

A. Sprunger, David C. Lehman, Abraham Baumgartner, C. W.

Baumgartner and Sam Lehman. The two congregations also joined in the dedication program on September 12 and every one was grateful that they were drawn together once more,

The church as it appeared after the 1886 alteration

that prejudices were overcome, and misunderstandings, if any, removed. Hearts swelled with emotions of deep joy when for the first time in many years they struck hands of fraternal fellowship. The Christian Sprunger members had been wanting to join with the S. F. Sprunger members in Communion and this was finally brought about the following month, on 

October 3. 

The church, T-shaped, was now much larger, the two entrances were on the east side and the pulpit changed to the west. There were narrow galleries on the north, east and south sides and the "baby" room was on the south side. All in all, it was a fine church building for that period. 

In January 1886 the Baumgartner church voted to give up their services until the return of more favorable weather. 

In the course of time most of the older members in that part of the settlement had died and many of the younger ones had moved away, some closer to Berne. In the meantime more and more people had purchased spring wagons, so it was no longer a difficult problem to go to church, so the Baumgartner congregation also came into the united church in Berne. The following January they voted to sell the church and return the land to Peter Baumgartner, and the money from the sale of the building was put into the church treasury. The church was moved to a Yoder farm west of Berne. Later it was moved to the Abe Biberstein farm farther north, and is now used as a tool shed.

But let us return to the dedication Sunday. It was a great day for everyone. John Moser of Bluffton, Ohio preached the dedicatory sermon on Matthew 7, 25 and fifty-one young men and women were baptized. An account of the revival of that year was ably written by the late Fred Rohrer several years ago and reads as follows: "Fifty years ago Berne had one of the most remarkable revivals it ever experienced. It was not advertised in advance. 

There were no window cards printed announcing what day

it would begin. It received no newspaper publicity. There was no high pressure evangelist employed to stir up the people. 

No outside singers and musicians were engaged to draw a crowd. At that time the deacons and ministers of the Mennonite church considered it a sin to have any kind of a musical instrument in the church. "There were no week-day meetings held in any of the churches aside from the Wednesday prayer meetings. No one was ever asked to go forward and no pressure was brought on anybody. This is how it started: "Miss Emma Reusser felt the need of a re-birth while attending Rev. S. F. Sprunger's Sunday school class. Sunday after Sunday her eyes filled with tears. She was then doing housework in the J. F. Lehman home. Under Mr. Lehman's guidance she received the peace her heart was longing for. 

She told two friends, the Misses Judith and Bertha Liechty, about it and their hearts were touched and regenerated. She spread the good news to her brother Amos, and the Holy Ghost fire fell on him. "About the middle of June, 1886, Amos and a number of girls gave their first public testimony in song at a temperance meeting held in the hall above the Sprunger, Lehman & Co., clothing store. This testimony was the spark that set many hearts on fire. Soon groups of young converts held cottage prayer meetings at which souls after souls were added to the invisible church. "It was an Apostolic, Pentecostal revival the like of which you seldom hear or read about. The Holy Ghost breathed like a gentle, soft wind. Many became convicted and broke down without anybody having said a word to them. It was simply in the air. "One Sunday evening after prayer meeting at the Mennonite church a large group walked down through the middle of Main street, singing and praising until they reached the railroad. There they separated, some going north, some south, others continuing east and the rest turning around, going west.

"The revival lasted through July and August. On Sunday noons and evenings large groups would go to farm homes for lunch where coffee, bread, cheese, pie and cookies were served. The time was spent in singing and visiting. "There were over one hundred, mostly young people, converted. As far as is known all remained faithful to this day. Many passed away and were true to the end." 

Because S. F. Sprunger's conference appointments made it necessary for him to be away quite frequently an assistant pastor was necessary-so in January 1887 the congregation gave a call to I. A. Sommer to become assistant pastor. He preached his first sermon on January 30, on Matthew 9:35-38, and was formally installed as minister October 2, by S. F. Sprunger.

There are two interesting stories about Rev. Sommer that some may remember. On December 30, 1884, Mr. and Mrs. C. W. 

Baumgartner's infant daughter died. The weather was bad and the roads absolutely impassable, so Mr. Sommer carried the coffin on his shoulders through the woods to the Berne cemetery. 

The other incident occurred much later and is of an altogether different nature. Mr. Sommer, like his co-worker, S. F. Sprunger, was very particular about being prompt in everything, and prompt in closing a meeting as well as in opening one. One 

Sunday evening when Rev. Sommer was leader of the Christian Endeavor the time for closing was at hand. To him it was evidently out of the question to continue the meeting even a few minutes longer for he remarked, "If we had time we would have a season of prayer, but the hour is up." 

Since most of the members of the committee to compile the Gesangbuch mit Noten were members of this church, and since Rev. S. F. Sprunger, as Rev. van der Smissen put it, was the leading spirit, it seems permissible to relate in this history the story of how it was accomplished. 

The first effort to have a German Hymn-book was made by the General Conference in 1869. Different sections used different hymn-books, and the Conference decided that it would be of mutual advantage if all the churches used the same hymnal, not only for business reasons, but also because

the feeling of fellowship would thereby be strengthened, and cooperation in worship would be improved. The matter was brought before the Conference and it was resolved: "that it be proposed to the several District Conferences east and west that they agree to the following plan: that the western brethren select and omit those hymns which they can spare from their hymnal, and the eastern brethren select those from their hymnal which they wish to retain: that these selections be sent to Professor van der Smissen, who with the assistance of a committee of three to be appointed for this purpose, shall arrange these for a new hymnal." But these resolutions were not carried out.

In 1872 the Conference decided to publish a new hymnal. 

It first appeared in 1873 and was called "Gesangbuch zum Gottesdienstlichen und häuslichen Gebrauch in Mennoniten Gemeinden."

There is no record of any Mennonite church hymnbook having been purchased by the congregation here before 1886. 

Up to that time they had used their own books that they had brought with them from the "old country." Now the church purchased new ones and they were the second, revised edition which appeared in 1885. 

When the second edition was exhausted the General Conference in 1887, at West Swamp, Pennsylvania, resolved that the Board of Publication should publish a new hymnal with notes. 

Rev. N. B. Grubb and Rev. C. H. A. van der Smissen of 

Pennsylvania, Rev. S. F. Sprunger, Rev. I. A. Sommer and

Joel Welty, manager of the Book Concern here in Berne, were appointed to do the work. 

After much correspondence and preparatory work, especially by the brethren in Berne, the Board of Publication met in Berne in the spring of 1888. They invited Frank Welty, an accomplished musician, to be their player, and D. C. Neuenschwander, director of the church choir, to be the singer. 

It was no small task that was assigned to these men. 

Stacks of letters were received by them, an unbelievable num-

ber, that said, "if such and such a song is not included we will not accept the books."

The men worked industriously for six weeks. The work which continued from morning until night was tiring and very exacting, and they needed recreation. So they used to take time off for hikes or wrestling. Following the tustles and hikes a portion of Scripture was read and a short prayer offered, then the work of compiling was again resumed. But they did not finish the work during the first session. 

Later, the Berne brethren asked that Rev. David Goerz, a musician and singer, of Halstead, Kansas, as representing the brethren from Russia and 

Prussia take the place of the brethren from Pennsylvania at the next session. Rev. Grubb and Rev. van der Smissen agreed to the proposal on condition that the resolutions which had been passed should stand. 

Rev. Goerz then came to Berne for that purpose when they met again for four weeks. Later still, a third session of three weeks was necessary before the work was completed. 

Joel Welty 

Mr. and Mrs. Theophilus Schildknecht, a newly married couple, had come to Berne from Switzerland in January 1889.

Both were school teachers and well trained in vocal and instrumental music, so Mr. Schildknecht also assisted in the work. He was also a composer and he composed or re-arranged the music for hymns No. 9, 402, 574, and 594. S. F.

Sprunger arranged No. 20 and wrote No. 574. D. C. Neuen schwander wrote the music for No. 364 and David Goerz wrote the words for No. 402.

Mr. Welty played all the hymns at least three times. 

C. D. Amstutz of Bluffton, Ohio also assisted. 

The Easterners had asked that the hymnal should be small enough to carry in the pocket but this was not carried out, much to their disappointment, for they liked to carry their hymnbooks home so they could sing at home. About twentyfive years later the General Conference passed a resolution that the hymnal should be published in a smaller form. J. F. 

Lehman thought that a mistake and made an edition of only one thousand, thinking they would not sell. When he announced that the hymnal in small form was ready, orders came, orders for about five thousand. Mr. Lehman then went up to Rev. van der Smissen's study above the Book Store and laughingly said, "You Easterners were right after all."

Even though the General Conference ordered a new hymnal with notes, yet two or three extra sessions were required to have it accepted. Later, it met with a cordial reception and the hymnals sold well, even congregations in Germany ordering some. But only two thousand copies were printed in the first edition. We wonder where those two thousand copies are and if the owners prize them as they should. The Berne Witness Company is now putting out the fifteenth edition.

The Gesangbuch mit Noten has always been considered a very fine hymnal. No doubt this is greatly due to the fact that all the members of the committee but one wanted solemn music, consequently only solemn music was included. No. 

209, "Ich habe nun den Grund gefunden," they sang to the melody "O dasz ich tausend Zungen hätte." Somehow that one member succeeded in getting in the present melody which they never sang.

Later, in 1927, when the General Conference published an English Mennonite hymnal this church was again represented on the committee. J. F. Lehman, a member of this church, and G. Adolph, his son, a former member, both served on the committee which compiled another splendid hymnal of solemn music but this church has not seen fit to accept it.

Consequently the younger generation has not been getting acquainted with their own church hymnal. 

Apparently the last time the congregation gave a ministerial call was on January 2, 1888. That time Joel Lehman was asked to serve as evangelist. In 1890 he was ordained as travelling preacher and still later accepted the call to the 

Salem Mennonite Church 

Sonnenberg, Ohio to take the place of Rev. A. A. Sommer who had to resign because of ill health. 

By then Christian Sprunger was not preaching much anymore. He felt that he was no longer qualified to preach, so preached less and less after the three congregations united. He kept a diary for many years and he recorded the dates and texts of his sermons from time to time, saying S. F. Sprunger was away at such or such a place. Two of the texts listed are Luke 18: 

9-14, on October 22, 1893, and

II Corinthians 6:2, on October 18,- 

1896. One subject he often preached about was "where there is pride there is little chance of going to heaven." His last recorded sermon was dated October 10, 1897 on Galatians 6:1-10.

Joel Lehman 

Before 1884 there was no systematized method of financing the church treasury. They had only a box where the members could deposit their donations. By January of that year there was a deficit of $46.75, so they voted to tax every man who could pay $1.50. In 1886 they decided to tax each man over twenty-one years of age $1.00 and on New Year's

Day of each year to hold a collection to give all members an opportunity to help in church expenses.

Gradually, more and more people had come to the conclusion that the preacher must be paid something for his services, but it seemed there was nothing left after expenses were paid. However, in 1892 there was a balance of $375 which was paid to the preacher. In 1893 the balance was $353; in 1894, $379.13; in 1895, $491.88; in 1896, $489.54; in 

1897, $450.43. One can see at a glance that by this method the preacher never knew how much he would get at the end of the year. But the members helped in other ways. They brought the preacher and his family many things, wood, hay, fruit, potatoes and what not. It sometimes happened that a number of families decided simultaneously to send supplies, but that could not be helped. But there was no complaint from the preacher's wife. She has often said, "The church members were good to us, we always had enough." 

In 1898 it was finally decided that the preacher should have a regular salary of $600 per year, and a little was paid before the end of the fourth quarter. The treasurer did not approve of the "hit or miss" way of paying salary, so finally managed to pay once each quarter. A number of years later the congregation voted to pay their pastor of long standing back salary and this was done over a period of several years.

That time changes things is a recognized fact and we have seen many evidences of it in preceding paragraphs. We have also seen how reluctant many were to adopt new ideas, how afraid they were to accept anything new. The old idea that photography was wrong, that it was a form of idolatry, seemed to persist for a long time. Among those who were opposed to it were David and Christian Baumgartner, Christian B. Lehman and others whose photographs we wish were available now. Those who were not opposed used to go to 

Fort Wayne and other places to have their photographs taken but they tried to do it secretly. The strange thing is this, some were not opposed to having theirs taken if they could have them taken at home. But to go into a picture gallery was sinful. This explains in part the action taken in 1881

when it was voted that David Bixler should not be allowed to take Communion until after he gave up his picture gallery business, and it was not until 1889 that the conservative group came to the following conclusion, "In time we have seen there is no difference between making pictures and having them taken, and neither is sinful, so we conclude that the decision of September 1881 in reference to the picture gallery of David Bixler be rescinded and that we allow him to enter in full membership again and that he be given the right to take Holy Communion." This action was taken upon pressure of their pastor.

So far so good, but this same group opposed Eli Luginbill's band which he organized in 1892, sometime after his arrival from Kansas. That band caused a lot of trouble. 

It was thought Luginbill should not have been taken into church because of it. The green curtains which were put up to separate the Sunday School classes also caused much talk. 

And quite some time later, S. F. Sprunger's thin summer coat, a radical departure from the accepted style of the day, was another controversial subject, as was also the red velvet on the lines of the new horse harness he had bought. 

It is evident that Rev. Sprunger did not hesitate to make changes that concerned himself, but when it came to making many drastic changes in church he had to move slowly, for even the most necessary ones often caused trouble. 

S. F. Sprunger was intensely interested in many things and he tried hard to get others interested in them. His interest in music has been mentioned elsewhere in this book but we should like to add a few paragraphs to show how some of his efforts in that line took form. 

In the early years of his ministry there were not nearly enough song books for all so he would sing two lines of a song and have the people sing them after him, then he sang two more and they again sang these after him and so on and on. It was a slow process but they learned many a new song by this method. He was interested in the men and women who wrote the songs and many church members of today

remember how he used to tell them about the composers and writers and how different hymns came to be written. 

Next to Christian F. Stauffer, Rev. Sprunger was the first Frank Welty with the bicycle which he used to go about giving music lessons to have an organ in his home. No doubt it was the first musical instrument to be placed in the church and Frank Welty was the first to play it. It was carried over many a time for special programs. One Christmas evening when

Mr. Welty played, some commented that "Es wär schön g'si

wenn der Wälty nid danzelie g'spielt hät z'wische die färse." 

The idea of having an instrument in the church did not take well with some. The first time an organ was played in the church Mrs. John Weyman, deceased, cried throughout the entire service. She thought it a sin to have an organ in the church. Later, when the congregation bought its own organ one of the women walked out of church. She refused to have anything to do with "such works of the devil." 

While we are still on the subject of music we should like to add that in the late eighties a number of men grouped themselves together for quartette singing. The first quartette was composed of Ernest Franz, J. F. Lehman, Moses Moser, and Fred Rohrer. Others who sang in quartettes about that same time were Joel Welty, Dan Sprunger, Jacob U. Amstutz and James Sprunger.

Rev. Sprunger's interest in missions, both home and foreign, was such that others could not help but become interested in them. So it is not surprising that the Women's Missionary 

Society was organized in 1887. The first president was Katharine A. Sprunger (Hämi Drini), the first secretary, Dina P.

Lehman (Mrs. Joel Welty), the first treasurer, Anna Lehman (Mrs. J. P. Habegger) and the first cutting committee was composed of Mrs. Sam Lehman, Maria Baumgartner and 

Verena Neuenschwander, (Mrs. Abraham Schneck). Rev.

Sprunger even tried to get the young girls interested, he suggested that they cut quilt patches for the older women to sew into quilts. 

The first "quilting" took place July 25, 1887 when five quilts and five comforters were made at an all-day meeting in the hall above the Sprunger-Lehman store. Since then the quiltings have been an annual affair and more than eight hundred seventy-six quilts and five hundred forty-two comforters have been made. These figures do not include those made in 1916 and 1922. The secretaries failed to record the number made in those years. The first year the Society spent $168 and the first box to needy ones in the mission fields was

sent in September of that year. Their interest in missions grew rapidly from then on.

Missionaries and Mission Feasts were also agencies used for creating greater interest in missions. There were not so many Mennonite missionaries in those days, so Rev. Sprunger secured missionaries of other denominations, the outstanding one being John G. Paton of the New Hebrides. For the Mission 

Feasts he secured prominent Mennonite preachers and they left lasting impressions on many church members. These 

Mennonite preachers came on many other occasions too, for Rev. Sprunger felt that the congregation needed to get acquainted with the leaders of the denomination. They had much to learn from them and many of the people here took a personal interest in the men and have pleasant memories of their contact with them. Rev. Sprunger often gave tribute to them who helped him so much in his ministry. 

Many other outstanding speakers were here from time to time. Those who heard Professor Rappard of Basel, Switzerland are not likely to forget him. Rev. Sprunger considered himself very fortunate to get him and worked hard to get a crowd. And who could forget S. H. Hadley of the Bowery?

His story of the wonderful work done in Water Street, New 

York City, still rings in our ears. And of course we could not forget P. P. Bilhorn and his little folding organ. Then there were Dr. Munz of "Haus und Herd" fame and missionaries 

Merensky of the South African Mission and Hahn of the East 

Indies and many others too numerous to mention. S. F. Scovel of Wooster College who was here Christmas Day 1904 will undoubtedly be remembered for his "Apple cider, cinnamon beer, Christmas comes but once a year."

The contact with people who came to attend conferences from time to time was also a pleasant one. Those were the days when people came mostly by train and they made their stay worth while. The homes were crowded to capacity, many sleeping on the floor, and at meal time as many as three or four tables were served in a home. But they did not make

much of their inconveniences, everyone had a good time and did not mind being crowded. 

People are rather uncertain as to the time when teachers' meetings and prayer meetings were begun to be held in the church. The change was made when some "worldly" programs were given in the hall which they had rented for such meetings, so it was thought best to give up holding religious services in such a place. And as for the prayer meetings, as late as 1891 meetings were still held occasionally in the homes, but according to the information gathered on the subject, we believe that for the greater part they were held in the church after 1887.

In the summer of 1891 S. F. Sprunger had a leave of absence to go to Europe. He went in company with Peter Habegger, Joel Welty, Dan Zuercher and Peter E. Sprunger. 

Frederick Zeller was supply pastor during his absence. 

From 1886, the time when the three churches united, to 1900 was a period of decided changes. Just as civilization was becoming more religious, the church was gradually becoming more spiritual. The changes did not come overnight, that is certain, but they came. And though the idea of aspirations after better things may seem vague to some, yet, we must admit they were there. Several organizations came into being in the period just mentioned, the Temperance Society, the Women's Missionary Society, the Choral Society and the Male Choir. Vocal and instrumental music were also permissible to a certain extent. 

In 1894 the congregation voted to have ushers to find seats for guests and to keep order. The first ushers were Lydia Lehman, Eli A. Luginbill, William J. Sprunger, Isaac Lehman and 

1. A. Sommer. 

In that same year an Erntedankfest, (thanksgiving service) was held on October 8 to give thanks for a bountiful harvest. Two years later the Thanksgiving sales were begun with S. F. Sprunger crying the sale. The next year he announced, "Jakob Habegger wird die Sachen verkaufen" without so much as asking Mr. Habegger beforehand if he would.

Mr. Habegger then continued to cry the sales until Jess Michaud began. Others who cried sales were Jeff Liechty, Jesse 

Ellenberger and Amos Neuenschwander. To many children those sales spelled Thanksgiving Day even more than the big dinners which followed. At first only garden and orchard produce were brought. Then followed popcorn balls, candy, chickens, rabbits, fancy work and what not. In 1900 it was voted to have the Thanksgiving services on the national holiday and the sales continued to be an annual feature on that day until 1929 when it was voted to discontinue them. 

The men had long since adopted a style of clothing that conformed to that of the period and the women were gradually changing their style also. By 1890 or even before the dresses were fancy with buttons down the front, many had puffed sleeves and some had even begun to wear jewelry. 

The first white wedding dress was worn by Clara Bixler, (Mrs. Amos Reusser), in 1896, the material costing twenty-five cents per yard. The wedding styles changed and changed until today we have the white satin gowns and flowing veils, a far cry from the black dresses, aprons, head shawls and big woolen shawls of the long ago. Speaking of head shawls reminds us that only a few years ago one would have said, "head shawls are as out of date as the old mustache cups." Little did we realize that they would be back in style so soon. But the young girls wore simply made calico dresses, even on Sundays, and cotton stockings, silk ones not even being thought of. 

And our mothers used to grease our shoes to brighten them up. 

This was a weekly Saturday night program, to have them ready for church on Sunday morning. We had not even heard of shoe polish then.

We sometimes hear the nineties referred to as "the gay nineties." They may not seem very gay to some but that they were gay compared with former years leaves nothing to question. The dress problem was now mostly a thing of the past, not much reference being made to it except later, on special occasions when Rev. Sprunger preached against birds and

feathers on hats. He did not approve of them because of the cruelty to birds in securing them.

As we think of these incidents, others come to mind. How many remember: when Ulrich Schwartz, son-in-law of Rev. 

Kipfer, dug graves for the Baumgartner congregation, before he joined the New Manist Church; when Pete Sprunger dug graves for the Berne congregation, and when Isaac Lehman later assisted him; the fire in the Baumgartner church, though it did not do much damage; the fire in the Berne church when 

D. Z. Sprunger was janitor, and how he hurried outside after snow and extinguished it; when the boys, barefooted, sat on the fence around the church yard, pants legs rolled up to the knees, waiting for Sunday School to begin; Hillegonda Sprunger's coffin with lace all around it, (July 1887), it was the first fancy one and there were many who did not approve of it; the red kerosene in the lamps along the wall, and the gas lights of the nineties; the deep groove in the railing made by timid Christian Sprunger as he swayed back and forth, one hand on railing and changing his handkerchief from hand to hand; Pete Sprunger, (who was very deaf) standing right by the pulpit, listening to his half-brother preach; 

Bierli Eisi, an invalid of many years on a couch along the west wall south of the pulpit (1899 church); when S. F. 

Sprunger suggested that anyone who got sleepy should stand until the spell wore off, and David Sprunger did just that? 

And do you remember the pigeon holes in the foyer of the old church where you found the Kinderbote in its proper place? 

Perhaps that made more of an impression on us than on some because we had to take our turn in folding them and putting them in the pigeon holes.

Another incident, and far more important than any of the above mentioned is the following. On the day that S. F. 

Sprunger and several others left for Noble, Iowa to attend 

General Conference, October 9, 1906, Deacon Daniel Z. Sprunger passed away. From Noble, Iowa Rev. Sprunger went to Kansas for in his file of sermons is one preached in Alexanderwohl. 

On the first Sunday after his return, November 8, he remarked, "Was habt ihr getan? Ihr habt eine von meinen gröszten Hül-

fen zum Grabe getragen." He felt the loss keenly. He wondered if perchance something had been done that was displeasing to the Lord, and punishment was sent to bring them to a realization of it.

In telling about the early churches we mentioned the fact that men sat on one side of the church and women on the other. The first attempt to sit together was made on Sunday evenings by the young people under the southeast gallery. We cannot say definitely when this occurred but it must have been about 1900. It caused no little amount of talk and now as we think on it, it enables us better to understand how many of the earlier changes caused trouble.

We have often wondered who the couple was that had the courage to first sit together in the Mennonite church! But it gradually came to be a fairly common sight and a few parents began sitting together in the center section of the church even in the morning. But it was not until 1912, in the new church that the custom became general.

We remember the time when a father would occupy a whole bench (they were not so long in the old church) with his sons, he sitting in the middle so that he could by direct or remote control keep the boys on their best behavior. The same can be said of mothers and daughters. 

Daniel Z. Sprunger 

Another interesting thing is this. S. F. Sprunger was not one to be on intimate terms with many members of his congregation and yet almost everyone spoke intimately of him. He was spoken of as "der Sprunger Sam" or perhaps more often as simply "der Sam." Even children spoke of him as "der

Sam" and people still remember the Sunday evening when in Christian Endeavor a young lad announced that "Sam 

Sprunger will lead us in prayer." But Rev. Sprunger did not mind that, he probably enjoyed that intimate way of speaking of him as much as any one. 

There were a number of subjects that Rev. Sprunger emphasized from time to time. One was dependability, another was promptness. These were drilled in so deeply that they are evident in the present generation. It is a weekly sight on 

Sundays or any other day when there are meetings, to see the people hurrying, some even running, when it is time for the last bell to ring. Still another subject that was brought up from time to time was Sabbath observance. He did not approve of the great preparations some people made for a big Sunday dinner. He thought they should be simple, so the women could rest from much cooking at least one day in seven. Another thing he did not approve of was standing around after church to visit. He told people to go home and think on what he had preached about. Perhaps that is the reason there is not so much visiting after services here as in many other localities.

That the congregation was growing steadily is evident, and in 1899 the church was again too small. The question of building a new one was discussed, but was voted down ninety-eight to thirty-seven. They voted instead to enlarge the building the second time and the following committee was appointed: J. P. Habegger, Isaac Lehman, Philip Sprunger,

D. C. Sprunger, Sam Lehman, Dan Stucky and Emanuel 

Sprunger. The subject of a bell was also discussed and it was decided to put it to a final vote Sunday, June 25. There were many who were not in favor of one and it is hard to say what the outcome would have been had it not been for Frederick Zeller who preached that morning. After the sermon 

S. F. Sprunger arose and put the question before the congregation. Then Rev. Zeller asked for "noch ein Wort, Bruder 

Sprunger." He said, "Ich hoffe ihr stimmt alle für ein Glöcklein, es leutet so schön am Sonntag zur Kirche," and sat down.

Well, after that, no one had the courage not to vote for the bell. 

On July 4, J. P. Habegger and S. F. Sprunger went to

Hillsboro, Ohio to buy one. But it was not satisfactory, it was not made of bell metal. It had a good tone but it was not nearly loud enough, so the bell was returned and the present one ordered made in Saint Louis, Missouri.

The dedication took place on July 30, by Rev. David 

Goerz of Newton, Kansas.

The 1899 alteration did not change the church much in general floor plan. The northeast and southeast corners were built out, the east gallery made wider and the steeple made. 

The seating capacity was increased by two hundred fifty-five, thus giving a seating capacity of over one thousand, and the heating difficulty increased in proportion. We often saw the stoves, three of them, red-hot and we still marvel that they did not set the church on fire. 

This time there was a new pulpit (the present one) made by Philip Sprunger, to take the place of the long one which had been in use since 1879. His daughters are not likely to forget the many hours they spent in sanding it. Another story, also an interesting one, relating to the pulpit, is that when the present church was made the committee tried to get wood as near the color of the pulpit as possible. 

The old church was not very high and many a guest preacher expected difficulty in making himself heard. But speaking loudly enough to be heard was not a difficult matter. 

This was due to the fact that the 1879 building committee knew how to build. They had rounded the corners and a study of the interior views of the church will show how it was done.

It may be of interest to some to know that this church supplied a pastor for several families who moved to Woodburn, Indiana in the late nineties. S. F. Sprunger and I. A.

Sommer both served in that capacity as did also J. W. Kliewer who went to Woodburn from Evanston, Illinois where he was a student.

The 1899 Church 

11 

Drawing by Arthur L. Sprunger Interior View of the 1899 Church

April 28, 1902 was a memorable day. It was the fiftieth anniversary of the landing in New York, of the group that settled in this vicinity in 1852. The day was Monday, and the storms of the previous week with the disastrous fires and hours of anxiety were past. On April 22 fierce winds had carried sparks of fire from the Peter Mazelin farm where both house and barn were destroyed to the David Habegger and John McClain farms where the barns were destroyed. Peace and tranquility again reigned in the hearts of the people and everything was set for the great day. On account of the high winds of the previous week the women had not been able to do any baking for fear of fire. So, many of them got up right after midnight, Monday morning. The day was ushered in by a pleasant serenade by the German Cornet band of the town before sunrise.

By nine o'clock the church was filled with guests who had come to pay tribute to the original group who sat together near the pulpit. There were thirty-five surviving members but only twenty-seven were present.

After singing by the congregation Joel Lehman (who had moved back to Berne just two weeks previously) read Psalm 103. He also led in prayer. This was followed by a sermon by 

Rev. Daniel Burghalter of Dayton, Ohio. His text was Psalm 

103 and he stressed how much the people owe to the untiring efforts of the pioneers. 

The next number was the singing of a Psalm by the jubilists, such as they sang fifty years before in their church services. This was especially interesting to those who heard such singing for the first time. This kind of singing has been described elsewhere in this history so we will not dwell on it here.

Then followed the account by Abraham Zurflüh. He gave the story of their travels from Switzerland to America. This, too, was interesting. He spoke of the crop failures, high rents and stricter laws on military service which gave rise to "American fever" in the summer and fall of 1851. They left their homes in the Jura Mountains on March 8, 1852. The

weather was nice but very cold. Upon their arrival in Basel on March 9 they had to have chests and other baggage labeled. 

They also had to pay the first half of their fare from Basel to New York, the total fare for each person being one hundred ninety francs, or $37.52. The trip from Basel to Strassburg was made by train. There, all baggage was examined, though only superficially. The trip to Saarburg was also by train, from there to Commercy, a distance of one hundred twenty miles, they had to travel by stage coach, drawn by eight horses. From there they went by train to Havre via 

Paris and Rouen.

On Friday, March 19, they boarded the Hanneman, an eight-hundred-ton, three-masted sailing vessel from Norfolk, Virginia. It was one hundred twenty-three feet long and twenty-two feet wide. It must have been a chartered boat for it carried no cargo except the food, drinking water, wood and coal, and stones for ballast, besides one hundred seventyfive people, including children. Berths and other accommodations were very poor, and the one stove far too small, so that the patience of the cooks was sorely tried many times. 

The morning of the first day out Johannes Sprunger's youngest child, Peter, died. He was strapped to a board, attached to an anchor and thrown overboard. All in all, five children died on the ocean voyage and all were buried at sea. 

Many were seasick, some only a short time, while others were sick all the way across. That would not be so bad now when the trip can be made in five days and less, but it took them forty days to cross the ocean and it is not at all funny to be seasick that long.

One experience at sea will give an idea what a trustworthy captain they had. It was absolutely forbidden to give any of the ship crew wine or any kind of liquor. One night, not many days after they had sailed an awful commotion took place. 

A drunken sailor had a tiff with the night watchman, so they locked him up. In the morning he was let out and asked who had given him the whisky but he refused to tell. He then had his hands tied and was lifted up in the air on a rope ladder, 3

and the second officer stood threateningly by, but he still refused to tell. Then the Captain announced that he would allow no fire to be built, that he would give no coal or water until he found out who gave the sailor the liquor. Finally some one told him that the passengers in cabin twenty-nine were guilty. They were ordered to appear before the captain and they confessed their guilt. They were not punished but 

Captain Foster took their whisky and locked it up until the end of the journey. No doubt they thought that was punishment enough. 

At three o'clock in the afternoon of April 27 they saw land and the next morning a small steamer came to tow them farther into the bay where it anchored. Then the doctor came on board to see that all was well and at two in the afternoon another steamer came to take them to shore. Some of them had some unfortunate and unpleasant experiences while in New York City. No one could speak English and there are always those who take advantage of such people. In fact, some were roughly treated. 

The next day they left for Albany on the steamer Henry Hudson. The ship was so crowded that many had to stand. 

From Albany they went to Cleveland by way of Buffalo. The trip from Buffalo to Cleveland was the most dangerous part of the journey. Lake Erie was frozen quite far out and appeared as a wide level expanse of snow and the boat had to make its way through ice. They arrived in Cleveland on May 

6 and left for Massillon the same day. That same evening a few of the travellers went on to Sonnenberg, for there were no telephones to announce their arrival. The next day many friends went on to Massillon with wagons to take them on to Sonnenberg.

After this interesting trip by proxy the children of the jubilists sang a song. S. F. Sprunger then gave a statistical report of the immigrants, their survivors and their descendants. 

There were eighty-two immigrants, forty-seven of whom had passed on. Of the remaining thirty-five, twenty-five lived in Indiana, seven in Ohio, one in Kansas, and two in Oregon.

Twenty-eight were farmers, three were preachers, one a doctor, one a night watchman, one a shoemaker and one a stock buyer.

As to faith, twenty-eight were Mennonites, three Missionary Church, two Reformed, one Evangelical. (One not recorded.)

The following figures are approximate: There were three hundred forty-nine children, six hundred sixty-one grandchildren and forty-nine great-grandchildren. One hundred sixtyone had died and one hundred forty-seven had married. 

Five hundred thirty-nine lived in Indiana, one hundred thirty-eight in Ohio, eleven in Kansas, nine in Oklahoma, six in Missouri, three in Colorado, two in Michigan, two in Alaska, one in Pennsylvania, one in Illinois, one in Florida, one in North Dakota, one in Wisconsin, one in Idaho, one in Washington and ten in Oregon.

Four hundred twenty-two were farmers, seventeen busi ness people, fourteen in domestic service, six carpenters, three preachers, three typographers, one kept a book store, two postmasters, two factory workers, two nurses, two doctors, two miners, two stock buyers, one music teacher, one worker on railroad, one mason, one miller, two mission workers, one painter and one florist.

Two hundred thirty-four were Mennonites, twelve Missionary Church, eleven Saints, thirteen Reformed, eight 

Evangelical, two Presbyterian, one Methodist, one Dunkard, one Baptist, one Dowieite, and one Christian.

After a song by the grandchildren of the jubilists, Dr. 

Cleophas C. Baumgartner of Elkhart, Indiana spoke on "Once and Now." He remembered the time when wolves howled around the homes and Bluffton and Fort Wayne were the nearest market places.

After a few remarks a poem was read. This was written by one of the original group, Mrs. Elizabeth Zuercher, who was ill and could not attend. C. M. Stauffer had arranged it in poetry form for her.

As time went on Rev. Sprunger realized that the youth of the church needed a preacher who could preach and instruct in English. And too, the work was continually getting heavier and he did not have the strength to carry on. In reality the brightest days of his ministry were over. An eye operation and a fractured hip had greatly diminished his vitality so he resigned in June 1903.

For the first time in many years the congregation had to look for a new preacher. A call was given Rev. H. J. Krehbiel of Trenton, Ohio but he was unable to accept it. Another one was sent to Rev. J. W. Kliewer at Wadsworth, Ohio. He accepted it and came to Berne with Mrs. Kliewer November 

16, 1903. He preached his first sermon November 22 from the text Isaiah 51:6.

Rev. Kliewer had a brilliant mind. His sermons, faultless in diction, were always carefully prepared and thought provoking. At first many complained they were "too deep," but they gradually came to appreciate them and as time goes on more than ever realize how masterful they were. 

The period from 1900 on marked more new interests, though the changes now were not so radical or so many. The gradual awakening of the church to the need of educating its young people was one of the evidences of new interest in life. 

Rev. Kliewer did much to encourage college education for the young people. There are many indications that the church had caught hold of the spirit of progress. Reading the library report indicates that they had travelled far from 1861 when it was thought a sin to read anything but the Bible. And the following resolutions will throw more light on this subject.

In 1900 the congregation voted "to allow church members who live so far away that they cannot come home for Communion services to take it in churches of other denominations." This was rather inconsistent for they did not officially admit those

Guests of the 1902 General Conference which convened in Berne

115 of other denominations to the Lord's Table until 1935! Another one of 1900 was "to allow the pastor to perform marriages in private homes" and the first one to avail herself of this privilege was Miss Barbara Soldner several days later. 

Another interesting resolution was one made in 1905 when "men were advised not to wear hats in church at funerals of relatives." That same time the subject was discussed whether or not coffins should be taken into the church and the corpse shown. They decided to continue as before. 

As early as 1908 the question of building a new church was discussed, but it was not until April 13, 1909 that it was definitely decided to do so. A building committee was appointed, composed of: J. P. Habegger, president, Levi A. 

Sprunger, secretary, Sam Lehman, superintendent, Philip 

Sprunger, head carpenter, Eli Baumgartner, D. C. Lehman,

Albert Neuenschwander, Emanuel Sprunger and Dan Stucky. 

On July 26 a finance committee was appointed. It was composed of: Sam Soldner, president, J. F. Lehman, treasurer,

Burkhart Lehman, Levi Moser, C. C. Neuenschwander, and 

I. A. Sommer, and in less than two months more than $49,500 was subscribed.

The March weather of 1910 was very favorable for digging the basement and the work progressed so fast that the corner-stone laying was set for June 5. Arrangements were made and invitations were sent out, but suddenly something happened to change all plans. The man who had contracted to lay the bricks realized that he had bargained for more than he could carry out, so he walked out on the job. Three weeks went by before a new contractor was secured, so the cornerstone laying was not held until July 10. 

That Sunday the services began promptly at three o'clock in the church, at the close of the Sunday School. After congregational singing, Rev. H. H. Amstutz of the Missionary 

Church read an appropriate Scripture passage, and S. F. 

Sprunger led in prayer. The church choir then sang, "To 

Thee, O Dear, Dear Country." This was followed by an English sermon by Dr. S. K. Mosiman, President of Central Men-

nonite College, Bluffton, Ohio. He preached on Isaiah 28:16, and I Corinthians 3:11. He clearly brought out the thought that Christ is the corner-stone of all true religion. Rev. H. H. 

Kattman of the Reformed Church then delivered a German sermon based on Psalm 26:6-8. The thank offering held for 

Foreign and Home Missions amounted to $91.37. Another song by the choir and a prayer offered by Rev. C. P. Maas of the 

Evangelical Church closed this part of the service. 

At half past four the congregation went over to the new church where the following articles were enclosed in a sealed box and placed in the corner stone: a Bible, a Gesangbuch mit 

Noten, church membership list, constitutions of the church, the young people's society, the Temperance Society and the church choir, a membership list, program and constitution of the male choir, a German and an English copy of the Articles of Faith of the church, the church publications, "The 

Mennonite," "Der Bundesbote," "Kinderbote," and a German

Sunday School quarterly, a topic booklet used by the young people's society, a church history as was found in the German column of the current issue of the Witness, a report of the last General Conference of Mennonites, a report of the

Semi-Centennial Anniversary of the General Conference of 

Mennonites, a Bundesbote Kalendar, constitution of the General Conference of Mennonites, constitution of the Middle District Conference, a picture of the old church and a copy of the current issue of The Berne Witness. 

Rev. J. W. Kliewer, pastor of the church, laid the stone, after which I. A. Sommer, editor of the church papers, offered the closing prayer. The congregation then sang, "Herr entlass uns mit dem Segen." It was fortunate that the weather was ideal, for a large crowd of fully fifteen hundred people had gathered to witness the ceremony. 

The following year Rev. Kliewer was called to the presidency of Bethel College at Newton, Kansas to succeed President Wedel who had passed away. He tendered his resignation on April 30, 1911 and on July 30 preached his farewell sermon to a large audience. "That evening another special ser-

vice was held. It was a free for all service at which many took active part. At the close of the meeting a farewell reception was given the pastor and his family putting them through a hand shaking ordeal at which several hundred persons took part.” 

Rev. Kliewer went to Newton well prepared. He had ten years' experience as pastor and during his time here had been a diligent student in his profession. He had served the congregation here since November 22, 1903 and he had served it well. 

He was a worthy successor to S. F. Sprunger. Under his pastorate the congregation had continued its steady growth until it numbered over eight hundred communicant members. It was during his pastorate that the Union Meetings were inaugurated and they have since been carried on annually. "He was prominently identified with every religious, moral and intellectual enterprise of the town, community and county, such as the County Sunday School work in which he supervised the teacher training department for several years; the 

Christian Endeavor and temperance work and the lecture course. It was he who urged the building of the new church as much as anyone and under his leadership the congregation undertook to build the magnificent new structure. "Not only did his own congregation with all its auxiliaries keenly feel the loss through Rev. Kliewer's departure, but there was ample evidence that the whole town and community felt the same way for he was highly respected and loved." 

After Rev. Kliewer's departure S. F. Sprunger resumed the pastorate pro tem. His eight years' vacation had refreshed him in body, mind and spirit. This time he had a much larger congregation to serve. It was truly a different picture from that of 1871. The Sunday School had fifty-seven classes with an enrollment of eight hundred ninety-seven, with an average attendance of about eight hundred; there were one hundred ten in the home department and one hundred thirty-five in the cradle roll, making a total of eleven hundred forty-two. The 

Christian Endeavor Society had a membership of about four hundred, and the Women's Missionary Society a membership of

three hundred ten. The German Temperance Society membership numbered three hundred seventy. That society, by the way, was an important factor in the development of the church, and more than any other agency gradually changed the public sentiment of the community in favor of temperance and against saloons. For many years it was the only one of its kind in the 

United States and its influence for good went out far and wide. 

The church was completed in March 1912 and in the meantime great plans had been made for the dedication services to be held on Easter Sunday. Of course everyone was happy to have such a beautiful new structure, yet, there was a strange feeling in the hearts of many that could not quite be analyzed. So much had taken place in the old church, so many of life's memories were connected with it, that to leave it thus seemed almost like leaving part of themselves behind. Joy and sadness seemed to possess them at one and the same time. 

The least they could do was to have special farewell services before the final leave taking. The first of these began Sunday morning, March 31. That morning, Rev. J. F. Balzer, (son-inlaw of Rev. van der Smissen) of Mountain Lake, Minnesota, then studying in Chicago, preached on Christ's triumphal entry into Jerusalem. In the afternoon one thousand pupils and teachers attended Sunday School and it was then that the pupils received instructions where to sit in the new church. A farewell prayer meeting gave opportunity for many heartfelt prayers. In the evening the Christian Endeavor had a farewell service. Rev. van der Smissen presented a picture of church missions after which grateful prayers and testimonies by many of the members told of many blessings they had received in the old church. 

On Good Friday, April 5, another farewell, in fact the farewell was held in the old church. Rev. van der Smissen read Luke 23:26-29 and offered prayer. Rev. S. F. Sprunger then read Psalm 103:1-5 and remarked that it should really be a memorial service as well as a thanksgiving service. Rev.

I. A. Sommer then preached on II Timothy 2:8. Rev. Joel Leh man also made a short address on Hebrews 13:7.

After the

19 congregation sang several verses of No. 164, S. F. Sprunger gave an address. He asked the congregation how many had buried relatives or friends from the church and nearly the whole congregation stood up. The church had been in use for thirty-three years and in that time there were more than seventeen hundred Sundays. At least one hundred guest preachers and missionaries had occupied the pulpit, among whom were Father Schneller from Jerusalem, John G. Paton, missionary to the New Hebrides, Professor Rappard, Missionaries Dirks and Diegel, and Dr. Herzler. They had also enjoyed the visits of prominent Mennonite ministers among whom were 

A. B. Shelly, Christian Schowalter, Christian and Jacob Kreh biel, Henry J. Krehbiel, Gustav Harder, N. B. Grubb, J. B. 

Baer, M. S. Moyer, Peter Balzer, David Goerz, Jacob Wiebe, Daniel Hirschler, John Hirschler, Dr. S. S. Haury, and C. J. van der Smissen. 

Parts of songs No. 584, 527, 283, and 62 were also sung during the service. Then Rev. P. E. Penner closed the service with prayer.

In the afternoon the Women's Missionary Society had its farewell service. Rev. P. E. Penner spoke on John 12:20-26.

Several musical numbers were rendered, a solo, a quartette, and a double quartette and remarks were made by several members of the society and by S. F. Sprunger. 

The dedication took place on Easter Sunday, April 7.

Promptly at nine oclock, S. F. Sprunger opened the service with invocation, which was followed by congregational singing (No. 9). Rev. Albert Schumacher, pastor of the Grace Mennonite Church, Pandora, Ohio, read an appropriate Scripture passage after which S. F. Sprunger read the solemn dedication service and delivered the dedicatory prayer. 

After a song by the choir, "Wie heilig ist diese Stätte," Rev. J. W. Kliewer of Bethel College, Newton, Kansas, former pastor of the church, preached in the German language from the text I Peter 

2:4-5. He said an Easter service and the dedication of a new church in one service were in perfect harmony. Without Easter the dedication of a new Christian church would be mean-

ingless. In his sermon he drew the analogy between the construction of a new church building and the building up of a spiritual temple of God with "living stones," true Christian members.

A selection by the male choir preceded the English sermon by Rev. W. S. Gottshall of Bluffton, Ohio. He preached on Haggai 2:9 and compared the beginnings of the children of Israel and their tabernacle worship in the wilderness with the small beginnings and the humble meeting house of the Berne congregation in pioneer days, and then the growth of Israelitish worship until "the glory of this latter house" in the prophetic vision appeared, with the growth of the Mennonite congregation to the present and its beautiful new church. After prayer by Rev. C. H. A. van der Smissen and congregational singing (No. 185), a collection was raised to build a new church at Champa, India, which amounted to $322.00. After announcements the male choir sang "Gross ist der Herr." The song took so well that the choir was asked to sing it again in the afternoon. This service closed with prayer by Rev. Gottshall.

The afternoon was turned over to the Sunday School for an Easter program. For this service the large church proved to be far too small. It was crowded to its capacity, with people standing in the aisles and back of the choir loft. The crowd was variously estimated from twenty-five hundred to three thousand, and many could not get in at all. The program was such an interesting one it is worth telling about here. 

The first part of the program was a processional by over thirty boys who marched to the front and sang, "O, the Stone is Rolled Away." Roland van der Smissen rendered a Scripture reading in German and Rev. H. H. Kattman of the Reformed Church led in prayer.

A song by a mass choir of Sunday School pupils, directed by Menas Baumgartner followed, then a declamation by 

Edna Lehman. The next number was an exercise, "Morning

Bells," rendered by a number of children while a chime was rung in an adjoining room. This was followed by a duet by

Cordelia Riesen and Florence Lehman, and that by a declamation by Willard Hirschy.

Next was an exercise by a number of little boys and girls carrying a motto "He is Risen," and then another processional, the most interesting one of the program, while Miss Elfrieda Franz sang "Die Osterlilien." It consisted of ninetynine little boys and girls of the primary classes, between the ages of four and seven, marching two by two, the girls carrying carnations and the boys palm branches, from the east gallery down to the pulpit and back again. 

Another pretty exercise was "Victory Through Jesus" consisting of five little girls representing the five letters of the name Jesus and a row of little boys carrying the letters spelling the word "Victory." A big cross beautifully decorated with Easter lilies by Florist Emil Nagel was placed in the centre of the platform.

Another children's chorus and a declamation by Martha Bagley and a chorus by the older girls of the Sunday School followed. Rev. Albert Schumacher then gave an address, speaking chiefly to the children, using the beautiful new church as a type of our bodies as the temple of God. A collection for 

Sunday School work and missions amounted to $89.22. A repetition of the male chorus' "Gross ist der Herr" concluded the afternoon service.

The evening service was devoted to the rendition of the beautiful cantata, "New Jerusalem" by the mass choir of the church, assisted by quite a number of singers from other churches in town. The choir numbered over one hundred fifty voices and was drilled and directed by Professor O. E. Richard of Fort Wayne, and accompanied at the piano by Miss Rose 

Lehman. Miss Kathryn Egly sang the soprano solos, Miss 

Helena Liechty the alto, Menno E. Stauffer the tenor, and G. Adolph Lehman the baritone. Miss Alieda van der 

Smissen and Clinton A. Lehman assisted in quartette singing. 

Under Professor Richard's excellent leadership the choir had been trained up to a point of efficiency that it had probably never reached before. The collection in the evening for the

The First Mennonite Church, Berne, Indiana

benefit of the choir amounted to $72.60. Thus ended another memorable day in the history of the church. 

There are several interesting things to remember in connection with the building of the church and the dedication of it. The money was all subscribed by people connected with the church, the gifts varying anywhere from ten cents to one thousand dollars, and all paid in. There were three hundred forty-eight paid in subscriptions, including thirteen gifts of one thousand dollars each. So there was no begging for money on Dedication Sunday to pay for the church. There was a balance on hand, instead. 

Henry W. Myers of Fort Wayne was the architect and

Charles L. Saunders of Portland did the brick work. The officers of the church then were, S. F. Sprunger, pastor, Frederick 

Sprunger, C. W. Baumgartner and C. A. Neuenschwander, deacons; Emanuel J. Liechty, Dan Stucky and Levi A. Sprunger, trustees; Fred Rohrer, clerk, and J. F. Lehman, Sunday 

School Superintendent. Emil Liechty was the janitor.

The church, Gothic in style, is one hundred fifty-eight feet long, eighty-five feet wide and forty feet high, inside, and has a seating capacity of two thousand. It stands on the site used in former years for stalls and hitching racks, directly east of the old church. One fine thing about the church building is that there are no big posts or corners where one cannot see the pulpit. The arrangement of pews in semicircular style was also a great improvement over the old. The new barn one hundred thirty-two feet by one hundred eighty feet accommodated ninety-five teams and vehicles. 

The event drew much interest from neighboring towns and cities. There was even a special train, a Mennonite Church special, between Fort Wayne and Berne to bring guests, and the regular train between Fort Wayne and Richmond was held in the evening until after the services were over to accommodate guests going south. Meals were served in the basement at noon and again in the evening (one meal fifteen cents, two for twenty-five cents). About three hundred people were served at noon and many more in the evening. Re-

Interior view of First Mennonite Church

porters from Fort Wayne and Decatur were present, representing the Fort Wayne Sentinel and the Decatur Democrat and Herald. It is not likely that those who were present that day will ever forget it.

When S. F. Sprunger resumed the pastorate in 1911 it was not for permanency. He was to serve only until a new preacher could be secured, and it was June 8, 1912 before the new one arrived in the person of Peter Reuben Schroeder. But 

Rev. Schroeder had not yet been ordained as elder so Rev. 

Sprunger had to continue in service until January 25, 1914 when he ordained Rev. Schroeder into that office.

The church was now under a new regime and more changes were inevitable. The first of these came the following 

August when the congregation voted to change the afternoon Sunday School hour to the forenoon. Many were happy, they favored the change. Others were not so happy, they wondered what they were going to do Sunday afternoons. Rev. Kliewer had made the suggestion to do this very thing in 1904, but it had not carried. It was not an easy matter for the country people to make three trips to church every Sunday and those who lived far away did not attempt more than two. They usually went to homes nearer town for Sunday dinner where they were always welcome. Where indeed was the family near by that did not prepare extra for Sunday dinner for just such guests?

That was the pre-automobile age and even though buggies were common by then many thought three trips too many for horses that had worked hard all week. Consequently many walked to one or two of the services, so the change to a forenoon and an evening service made it much easier for them. 

We can still visualize various groups as they walked east, west, north, south, some up the railroad, others down, as they went home from the Christian Endeavor meetings on Sunday evenings.

Another change that was made in August 1912 was the establishment of a nursery. The congregation voted to set aside one room in the basement where babies could be cared

for during Sunday School and church services. Since then it has been a regular feature of church work, different women and girls taking turns caring for the little ones so that the mothers can attend the services undisturbed. 

Still another change came in March 1914 when the individual communion cups were purchased. Even as far back as the eighties there were some who wished for better communion service. But let us go back still farther to the very earliest period. What the ministers in the Baumgartner church first used is not known. Christian B. Lehman made a wooden goblet which was used from the earliest time in the Berne church to 1886, it is thought. It has been handed down from father to son to grandson and is now in the possession of Wilber E. Lehman. It is shown in the accompanying illustra- The earliest Communion goblet used in the first Berne church tion. Evidently S. F. Sprunger used a glass one for several people distinctly remember seeing red wine in the goblet. No one seems to remember when the silver service (also illustrated

here) was purchased, but it is thought that it was shortly after the three congregations united in 1886. At first there were only two silver goblets, one wine pitcher and one plate. 

According to the records some silver communion service was The silver Communion service which was used prior to the present individual service purchased in 1898 of David Bixler. This must have been the time when the second wine pitcher, two more goblets and one more plate were purchased for it was our task even as a young girl to help polish the silver when needed and we do not remember when the second set was purchased. 

At first the members went up to "the front," one at a time to receive the Communion, each one going up twice. So it is not surprising that on such occasions the services lasted four hours and even more. Then it became customary for a row to go up at a time. Still later it was served at the seats, which custom is followed to the present time. 

March 1, 1914 was another red-letter day when the dedication of the pipe organ took place. In 1902 there were many objections when the Vocalion pedal organ was purchased. 

There were pipes for it but they were never put up. Now, less than twelve years later there was a two manual organ with one thousand three hundred fifty-nine pipes! S. F.

Sprunger composed a poem for the occasion and Professor 

Daniel A. Hirschler of Newton, Kansas and the Ladies' and

Men's Choruses gave a splendid program. Many who attended the services saved their programs, but many of us were not present so we present both poem and program here. 

Orgel-Praeludium 

Hören wir heute die Töne der Orgel erklingen, Soll es die Herzen zu freudiger Dankbarkeit stimmen; 

Zu jeder Zeit mahn' es, in Freude und Leid,

Gott stets zu Ehren zu singen. 

Herr, unser Herrscher, Du Schöpfer der lieblichen Töne, Gib, dasz dies' Orgeltöne Dich preise und kröne; 

Nicht nur das Ohr rühre der mächtige Chor, Sondern die Tiefen der Seele. 

Wird wohl das Rauschen der Töne die Trägen erschrecken? 

Wird es, wie liebliches Säuseln, die Schlummernden wecken? 

Zeigt es den Steg dem, der auf irrendem Weg? 

Ist es dem Müden ein Stecken?

Musik ergötzet und rühret der Menschen Gefühle, Aber die Lieder ergreifen die Tiefen der Seele, Und das belebt, ja, das erquickt und erhebt, Hin bis zum himmlischen Throne.

Nie darf die Musik verdrängen die menschlichen Stimmen, Höher nur soll sie stets heben die Herzen und Sinnen, Nur als Geleit dien' sie in Freude und Leid,

Wenn wir anbeten und singen. 

Wenn einst verklungen hienieden die lieblichen Töne, So führ' uns, Herr, zu der Schar jener himmlischen Chöre, Wo es ertönt: Heil dem, der uns hat versöhnt! 

Heil Ihm, dem Vater und Sohne!
