Gesang von der Gemeinde, (Gesangbuch No. 473). 

Handel When the United States entered the World War in 1917 and the draft was enforced no fewer than one hundred sixteen young Mennonite men had to register. Of these, fifty-four were drafted for service, some serving overseas. Some of them were non-combatants and because of conscientious scruples refused to receive pay from the government, so the church took up offerings to give them financial support of $5.00 per month. The church also supported the YMCA movement in the army.

Due to the War more and more English was being spoken in the church, so it is not surprising that English sermons became a feature of Sunday evening services. During Rev. Kliewer's pastorate English services were held occasionally on Sunday evenings, but there was no regularity about this. In 1914 or 

1915 it was decided to hold an English service the second Sunday evening of each month, beginning at seven o'clock. By 

1918 they were held every Sunday evening, and by 1922 an English service was held once a month Sunday morning. The change to a German sermon every other Sunday was made shortly after-and the change to only one German sermon a month was made in the early part of Rev. Suckau's ministry. 

The Parsonage 

On February 9, 1920 the congregation decided to build a parsonage on the site where the old church stood. Since the erection of the new church eight years previously the old one had been rented out for lecture courses and various other kinds of meetings. Now it was torn down and the parsonage built, Rev. Schroeder and family moving into their new home 

October 19, 1920.

That same year the congregation also voted to finish moving the old cemetery across the street to the M. R. E. cemetery west of Berne. Throughout previous years more or less moving of graves had been done but even after the decision of 1920 some of the graves were never moved and quite a number of people still lie buried under the paved road, the parking lot and the filling station, north of the parsonage. 

In 1928 Rev. Schroeder resigned the pastorate to accept the call to the presidency of Freeman Junior College at Freeman, South Dakota. The following account of the farewell services for Rev. Schroeder and family is from the Berne Review of Monday, July 30, 1928. "Seating two thousand people, the large Mennonite Church was filled nearly to capacity last evening when Berne and community turned out en masse to participate in a most impressive and pathetic farewell service for Rev. P. R. Schroeder and family who leave Wednesday for Freeman, South Dakota, where Rev. Schroeder becomes president of Freeman Junior

College, a Mennonite church school. 

"Former members and friends from Fort Wayne, Bluffton,

Monroe and other cities, together with members from all churches in Berne joined to make the farewell service perhaps the largest in Berne's history. "The hundreds who filed by in a forty-five minute procession to bid farewell, the parting words of encouragement and good will and the scores of tear-stained cheeks were concrete evidence of a wide-spread and deeply rooted friendship cultivated during sixteen years of conscientious ministry to his congregation and devoted service to the community. "The Missionary and Reformed churches dismissed evening services, their pastors appearing on the program, Rev. 

Sauerwein leading the devotionals and Rev. Egle delivering a very effective address on behalf of the Ministerial Association and the community, using the text, 'And thou shalt be missed because thy seat shall be empty.' I Samuel 20:18. "Rev. Egle, secretary of the Ministerial Association, expressed heartfelt regrets at being forced to bid farewell to a

26 

brother pastor whose friendship he cherished, whose work was invaluable, and whose preaching was sound in doctrine. "Ben Sprunger read an interesting report on the growth of the Church and its organizations during the sixteen years when the hand of the departing pastor guided the affairs of a large and growing church to its present membership of one thousand forty-two. His report follows in part: "We shall, in this report, attempt to enumerate some of the benefits that the Lord has bestowed upon us during the sixteen years of ministry of Rev. P. R. Schroeder. "From figures in the year book of 1911, which was the last one published under Rev. Kliewer's ministry, the membership numbered seven hundred eighty-five. Of these, four hundred eighty still hold their membership in the church, many have died and some have moved away. At the present time the membership is one thousand forty-two. ""Giving for benevolent purposes has increased very much during all these years. In 1911 the total amount given for foreign and home missions was $2400, which in 1927 was $10,- 027. This is the largest amount except that in 1926 which was $12,000. The total amount given in the past sixteen years for foreign and home missions is over $145,000. The Women's 

Missionary Society gave over $16,000 during this time, an increase from $287 in 1911 to $2900 in the last report of one and a half years. The total of all organizations for the sixteen years is over $300,000. This increase in giving for missionary purposes was made possible by increased membership in the church, prosperity in business, bountiful harvests and large wages of laboring people. But we believe Rev. Schroeder's preaching on tithing and to give freely and willingly and the encouragement from missionaries was responsible to a great extent in bringing these figures up to where they stand. "Not only in giving of alms has the church contributed to Christ's cause, but also in sending out men and women who are giving full time to Christian service. Although it has been impossible to interview these concerning the factor that led them to their fields of labor, we feel quite safe in saying that

God used our pastor as a means in influencing some of these, if not all, in some way, or at least in part to make the supreme decision and consecration. He was an ardent solicitor for recruits in Christian service. New activities in the church were opened where many opportunities were offered to the young pople to serve their Lord and Master, such as the Intermediate Christian Endeavor Society, and about a year and a half ago the Gospel team work, also the Summer Bible School where the seed of the Word of God is sown which in time will bear fruit. ""The mid-week prayer meetings have been enlarged to take care of the needs of the great number in attendance. 

From a single group it has been divided into many groups well organized under competent prayer leaders and Bible teachers. 

In this way all present have an opportunity to take part. "During his ministry here Rev. Schroeder preached one thousand sixty-six sermons. The total number of sermons preached in Berne and in churches visited away from here is one thousand three hundred fifty-two. These were of many and varied texts, such as exhortation, tithing, discipline, redemption, divinity of Christ, virgin birth, faith, hope, love and many others. Many were comforted, received new hope, food came from the Word of God to those that hungered and thirsted after righteousness; to the sinner it cut as a two-edged sword. "In one hundred forty-three funeral sermons words of comfort and hope were spoken by him to bereaved families and loved ones; many of these words will not be forgotten. 

He also ministered to the sick and at death-beds, presenting the Great Physician as the one and only helper, comforter and sustainer in time of distress and sorrow. 66 "There were five hundred thirty-six baptized on confession of faith during these years. By his instruction, the preaching of the Word, and the leading of the Holy Spirit, they were led to see their sinful state and were brought unto repentance and acceptance of the Lord Jesus Christ as their Savior and made a covenant with God to be faithful unto death. "It was his privilege to perform one hundred forty-nine

wedding ceremonies through which by the Word of God they were led into their new and happy career where they share with each other joy and sorrow, as being bound together through the love of Christ. Besides he attended and delivered addresses at many silver and golden wedding anniversaries.' "Rev. Joel Lehman paid worthy tribute to the ministry of Rev. Schroeder recalling its similarity in Scriptural doctrine to the preaching of previous pastors. In seventy-six years the local church has had only five ministers (seven is the correct figure, E. F. S.). "Mr. and Mrs. Schroeder, in brief talks, tendered thanks to the Ministerial Association, Bible School Board, the officers and members of the church and the many others who extended an unusual fellowship, cooperation and spirit of love. "Another church organization fostered by the pastor and senior Christian Endeavor leaders was the Intermediate Christian Endeavor Society, organized in 1913. This branch of the young people's work has been of untold value to the church. 

Membership has nearly doubled. "About seven years ago a new type of work, the distribution of leaflets, was begun through a Good Literature Committee. An average of five thousand leaflets have been distributed gratis each year. Several hundred new books have been given the library by the committee. "In his talk, Rev. Schroeder disclaimed any honor for the increase in church gifts and membership, having ‘done no more than the Lord required.' This final expression was the hope for a continual spiritual prosperity of the church and all its organizations. "At a church conference in Bluffton, Ohio in 1911, Rev. 

S. F. Sprunger heard Rev. and Mrs. Schroeder sing a duet, 'Christian are you up and doing?' The young couple was given a call to serve the local church largely as a result of that song. 

Last evening, seventeen years later, the couple sang the same duet as a most impressive farewell injunction.”

Again, the congregation had to seek a new pastor. This time they secured Rev. Cornelius H. Suckau, missionary to

India who could not return to the foreign field at that time. 

He was engaged temporarily in 1928, coming with his family to Berne from Kansas September 7. He preached his first sermon September 11. In January 1930 he received the call to serve indefinitely and he has continued to the present time. 

Last year in January the German services were changed to the third Sunday afternoon of the month, thus leaving Sunday mornings entirely for English services. Another change of last year was brought about when the basement of the church was remodeled to make room for the junior department of the Sunday School, thus answering a long felt need. 

In 1937 a change was made in the janitorship of the church. 

Emil Liechty had served continuously from September 1, 1911 to September 30, 1937. In that time he was absent only eleven days, two Sundays when he was ill, three days in

January 1934 after his wife's accident, and four Sundays when he visited out of town, and he also missed serving for two funerals, his son's and his father's.

In June 1938 Rev. Suckau had the honorary degree of Doctor of Divinity conferred upon him by Wheaton College. In the summer of the same year his congregation granted him a leave of absence to avail himself of the gift of his daughter Edna of a trip with her to Europe. During his absence, Rev. 

Peter J. Boehr supplied as pastor. 

All the organizations of the church have continued to grow and the interest in each one has grown correspondingly. 

To get an idea of the growth one needs merely to turn to the statistical pages though we must admit that there one cannot get an adequate picture of the work and interest, for that one must turn to the following pages where the history of the church organizations is graphically told. 

And now as we look back over the past hundred years, what does the picture reveal? Perhaps the word panorama should be substituted for the word picture, for we have had a constantly changing scene before us. 

We have seen that after a time a new conception of re-

ligion placed emphasis upon more vital things, that a sincere people with hope, courage and a strong, serene faith in God could overcome prejudices and accomplish great things. 

We have the greatest admiration and respect for them, for paving the way to better things. True, some did not approve of the changes made, but after all, one cannot be too critical of them. If they could have lived longer, many would have fallen in line and kept pace with the changing years, of that we are confident. The dread of making changes was not peculiar to their period. People still shrink from making them, even today, so it is not strange that they with their background and training were hesitant. They did the best they could, those men of the early days. And all that has been accomplished in the past one hundred years cannot be attributed to any one person, no, not even to several persons. Rather it is a result of the cooperation between pastors, officers and church members. The insufficiency of human strength is one of the most apparent facts of life, and it was only by the combined strength of all that great results could be brought about. 

And what sacrifices they made! That they were more concerned with the future of the church than with their own petty notions and pet grievances is evident, and so a stronger united church emerged. When we are old and spin the threads of memory will we be able to recall with a thrill some such sacrifices that we have made? Have we kept pace with the changing years? Have we utilized the law of growth? 

As we look into the future, what awaits us? Who knows what lieth in store? No one can say. But if we keep a constant vision of growth before us and maintain a trust in God, we know that He also will help us to accomplish great things for Him. The possibilities are limitless. Let us remember that as souls need fresh experiences, so does the church. It is not the beginning of a race that counts, rather the ending; not where did we start, but where do we finish? 

Let us also remember the words of the mature Paul- "I have not attained. I press on." ..

Forerunners-Early Informal Groups

The story of the birth, development, and progress of this most important department of our church is one which presents a tremendous challenge to the present generation. The ease with which anyone today may attend our Sunday School, and the smoothness with which our organization operates, affords us only a small realization of the crude conditions prevailing during the first few experimental years after the school was born. The founders were men who most certainly possessed unquenchable zeal for the service of the Lord. To move forward in the establishment of this work amid obstacles of material inconvenience, poor transportation facilities, and the greater obstacle of sincere opposition from fellow Christians who could not see God's will in the undertaking, required rare strength of spirit and a divinely inspired vision of the great work which needed to be done.

888 Prior to the first organization in 1874 several groups had been meeting more or less informally in various school buildings. It is reported that Peter Lehman and P. A. Sprunger, among others, led such groups. Very little is known about these simple gatherings since no records were left. It appears, however, that they were significant in the limited social life of the time and contributed much to the spiritual welfare of the participants. Undoubtedly these gatherings gave birth to the larger conception of an organized school for the teaching of God's Word within the church. 

Sentiment Not Unanimous for the First Organzation 

On May 3, 1874, a small group of rugged pioneers and aggressive church members, under the able leadership of Rev. 

S. F. Sprunger, united their interests and efforts in forming the Sunday School which has continued to this day. 

Sentiment was by no means unanimous for this move. 

Growth was in spite of discouragements and restraining forces of conservatism. There were parents who dared not attend or send their children to this progressive institution. Others would not participate because of scruples against the leader of the movement. Rev. Sprunger had a few years previously completed his training at Wadsworth, Ohio, preparatory for the ministry, and there were many who objected to this learned young minister who, they thought, had fallen prey to education and worldliness.

Peter Burkhalter Only Living Member of Original Personnel 

Of the original officers and teachers, only one member is still living-Peter Burkhalter. He was the first assistant superintendent and served as teacher for many years. Mr. Burkhalter is still actively attending the Sunday School. Following is the original officer and teacher personnel:

Many Churches Represented in Sunday School Membership This Sunday School met originally in the Berne church where the present parking lot is located, north of the parsonage. Since information concerning this period is scanty (incomplete records between 1874 and 1879), it is not known definitely whether this school met throughout the year. It is known, however, that Sunday School was held only on alternate Sundays for a period of time. An interesting observation from the available records reveals the fact that the membership of the early school consisted of members from other denominations in this town in addition to those from the S. F. Sprunger, C. Sprunger, and C. Baumgartner congregations. The organization year did not correspond with the calendar year; new appointments were made in April or in May.

The sessions opened at nine o'clock in the morning with song and prayer and continued for two hours. The classes convened for one hour followed by a song service and special talks by the teachers, known as "Anreden."

Teaching Objective Impressed in Original Resolutions 

The purpose and standards of the school, the order of procedure, and the teaching objectives are clearly and interestingly expressed in the resolutions which were adopted at the very beginning. The original rules as they stood in 1874 were: "Regeln fuer unsere am 3ten Mai, 1874, gegruendete Sonntagschule 

1. Eröffnung der Sontagschule mit Gesang und Gebet.

2. Nach Eröffnung übernimmt jeder Lehrer seine Klasse und unterrichtet sie nach den ihm verliehenen Kräften. Die 

Hauptsache soll sein im Unterricht nicht den Buchstaben zu lehren, sondern das Gelesene aufs Herz anzuwenden. 

Denn der Zweck der Sonntagschule ist nicht der: Verstandesmenschen aus den Kindern zu machen, sondern vielmehr sie zu Christo hinzuführen damit sie Kinder Gottes werden. Der Sonntagschullehrer, so wie alle Sonntagschulbeamten, sollen sich daher als Arbeiter im Weinberg des Herrn, als im Dienste des Herrn, Herrn, stehend betrachten. 

3. Jeder Lehrer soll sich befleiszigen zur rechten Zeit am Ort und Stelle zu sein. Auch sollte er wenn nicht dringende 

Umstände as nötig machen, niemals fehlen; wenigstens sollte er dann immer jemand an seinen Platz stellen. 

4. Wird gewünscht dasz die Anwesenden, welche nicht als 

Beamten, wie Superintendent und Lehrer, in Anspruch genommen sind, sich zu ein oder mehreren Bibelklassen ordnen, wo sie dann miteinander ein Schriftabschnitt lesen und denselben durch gegenseitiges Fragen und Beantworten besprechen. 

5. Nach Abschlusz der Klassen eine viertel Stunde Gesangübung mit den Kindern und eben so lang mit der gewachsenen Jugend, besonders zur Einübung unserer Kirchenmelodien. Die übrige Zeit steht offen für Anreden an die 

Sonntagschule. 

6. Soll jeden Monat eine Kollekte gehalten werden zur Bestreitung der etwaigen Kosten welche durch Anschaffung der nötigen Bedürfnisse entstehen mögen; wenn aber da nicht nötig, so soll es für sonstige gute Zwecke verwendet werden. 

7. Anfang der Sonntagschule um 9 Uhr Vormittag. Dauer der Klassen von 9 bis 10 Uhr. Gesang von 10 bis halb-elf 

Uhr. Von halb-elf bis elf Uhr Anreden an die Sonntagschule. Schlusz mit Gesang und Gebet. 

8. Endlich, laden wir alle, nicht nur die Kinder unserer Gemeinde, sondern auch die in unserer Umgebung wohnen-

den, freundlich ein unserer Sonntagschule beizuwohnen und Teil zu nehmen. Gott gebe uns Gnade und Kraft von oben seine Lämmer zu weiden nach seinem Wohlgefallen. Amen!"

English translation: 

1. Opening of the Sunday School with song and prayer. 

2. After the opening exercises each teacher takes charge and instructs his class to the best of his ability. The main point should not be to teach the word-mechanics of the studied portion, but to apply this portion to life. For the purpose of the Sunday School is not to educate the members, but to lead them to Christ so that they may become children of God. The Sunday School teacher, as also each 

Sunday School officer, should consider himself as a laborer in the Lord's vineyard. 

3. Each teacher shall make efforts to be present on time. 

Unless important circumstances demand otherwise, he should never be absent; he should, at least, always appoint a substitute in this event. 

4. It is desired that those present who are not serving in an official capacity, such as superintendent and teachers, group themselves into one or more classes where a passage of Scripture may be read together and studied with questions and answers. 

5. A quarter of an hour song service with the children and an equal length of time with the young people for learning our church songs shall follow the class sessions. The remainder of the time is open for addresses in connection with the Sunday School. 

6. A collection shall be taken each month toward the defrayment of the various expenses which occur for the necessary maintenance. If not needed, the collections shall be used for some other good purpose. 

7. Beginning of the Sunday School at 9 o'clock in the forenoon. Duration of the classes, from 9 to 10 o'clock. Song service from 10 to 10:30. From 10:30 to 11 o'clock, Sunday 

School addresses. Close with song and prayer.

8. 

Finally, we wish to welcome cordially, not only the members of our church, but also those who live in our neighborhood to fellowship with us and to take part in our Sunday School. May God give us grace and strength from above, to feed His lambs according to His good will. Amen!

Rudimentary as these declarations may seem at first thought, yet a tremendous challenge is presented that of "leading men to Christ." Impelled by the resources of such a godly foundation, the Sunday School has grown in spite of the many discouragements of men. Workers were solemnly and emphatically made aware of their duties and responsibilities. 

"Anreden" Made Impressive with Object Illustrations 

Perhaps no other feature caused such lasting impression among the older folk as the "Anrede," or "Ansprache" as they were sometimes called. The custom was to assign a subject to some teacher who gave his discussion on the subject before the entire Sunday School during the closing half-hour period. 

Various unusual and conspicuous objects were displayed before the school by the teachers to impress their thoughts upon the listeners. In one case, the account of the origin of man was vivified by the display of a hog's rib. The extraction of the rib from Adam was reproduced by removing the rib, used in the illustration, from the vest pocket. The impossibility of salvation by works was illustrated by displaying a bulky ladder (referring to the ladder in Jacob's dream). I. A. Sommer convinced the attentive school of the inevitability of reaping what we sow by wearing his vest with all the buttons offset. 

Among the subjects discussed were, "Selig, was ist selig?," "Evangelium, was ist es?", and "Gehorsam oder Tun." Teachers took their turn in giving these object lessons in spite of fear complexes or speech difficulties.

Classes Changeable in Quest for Ideal Grouping 

The Sunday School was subject to much rearranging in the early periods as was probably natural in a new and exper-

imental organization. Classes were organized, rearranged, and discontinued frequently in the search for the ideal system of grouping and classification. Suggestions from the members were invited and encouraged. There were originally two departments, the "Männliche" and the "Weibliche" (the men's and the women's). The men's and the women's classes were located separately, and a similar seating arrangement was observed in the church services. 

At the very beginning of the school there were a total of twelve classes. This number increased to fifty-five in 1887 which is more than the present number of classes, fifty-three. 

This is partly accountable to the fact that the C. Sprunger and the S. F. Sprunger congregations had just united, making a larger Sunday School. There was a resolution in effect that eight scholars was the highest permissible enrollment in a children's class, thus increasing the number of classes. Records reveal that frequently there were classes of only one or two members. An interesting provision was that the teacher was to report to the superintendent if any member of his class was not congenial to the group due to age, size, temperament, or conduct. Classification was then made according to environment.

Classes were designated according to age, location, or literature used, such as: Heft, Gallerie, Ofen, Kinder, Lämmerweide, Glocke. The entire school was later divided into three major classifications, A. B. and C. A more complete classification has since been made into the following departments: 

Adult, Intermediate, Junior, and Primary.

Substitute Teaching Appointments Made Impromptu 

An addition was made to the teaching department in 1883 known as the "Stellvertreter Klasse" (substitute teachers class) of which Jakob Lehman was the first instructor. Any member of this class was likely to be called upon to serve at a moment's notice. An air of hushed suspense hung over the class as the curtain was pushed aside, and Superintendent S. 

F. Sprunger would cast his eye over the group, finally beckoning with the motion of his finger the one chosen to teach that

day. Originally, a substitute appointment was made by the teacher who expected to be absent. This assignment was given for a time to the secretary and later to the superintendent. 

Inconvenience Braved in Maintaining High Attendance Records 

Punctuality, and thoroughness were important elements in the Sunday School service. Much credit is due Rev.

Sprunger for his persistent efforts in building habits of punctuality and dependability in his workers and pupils. 

His achievements in this respect were very remarkable, and to this day the community is blessed with the fruits of his work. Each service begins at the stroke of the appointed hour, and the congregation is almost entirely in the pews. Members are still led to feel that they have fallen on an inexcusable point if they are not present on time.

One of the remarkable things about the Sunday School was that the percent of attendance was nearly the same when automobiles were not known, and traveling locally was done mostly by foot, as the percent of attendance today. Inclement weather conditions and mud roads were hardly justifiable excuses for absence. One is amazed in this day at the incidents related by the older people in the community of how traveling afoot was a commonplace. Entire families, including families with children who needed to be carried, walked to church Sunday after Sunday, sometimes a distance of a number of miles. Frequently these trips were made two and three times on a single Sunday.

Under those conditions pride of dress disappeared, for many times bare feet were seen wading through the mud on the way to the Sunday School; rubber boots were the usual foot-gear during wet seasons. It was not considered disgraceful or a breach of etiquette to congregate in the above manner, for the superintendent and the teachers were amon them. It is said that one good brother, who was a teacher in the Sunday School, appeared regularly at the Friday evening teachers' meeting in spite of very unpleasant weather.

If the weather was warm and muddy, he appeared in his

bare feet. Such devotion readily accounts for the growth and progress of the school. 

Rapid Growth of Membership 

The first enrollment is estimated to have been between 50 and 75. The first record is a total enrollment of 128 in 1877. 

The membership was more than doubled in the next decade. 

The growth of the school within the 65 years is phenomenal. 

A graphical study will confirm this. The total active enrollment is shown at significant years in the history of the church and Sunday School.

School changed from afternoon to morning. 

Accommodations for the Farmer-Bureziet 

The Sunday School met from 9:00 to 11:00 o'clock in the forenoon during the first year or two of its existence. In about 1876, however, to accommodate those who could not attend in the morning and to avoid conflicts with the church services, the time was changed to 2:30, Bureziet, in the afternoon.

Bureziet (farmer's time), used for the convenience of the farmer, was of local coinage and was one-half hour faster than standard time. Through the years there frequently were appeals to have the time of meeting changed back to forenoon. Farmers found it too inconvenient to travel back and forth several times a day. There was objection to this plan by S. F. Sprunger and others on the grounds that to leave the afternoon without a sacred service would give too much opportunity for unholy Sabbath desecration and mischief to the young people. 

However, the plan to revert to the forenoon session was finally adopted as a response to the numerous appeals. This change was made after the dedication of the large new church on 

August 5, 1912.

Scope of Sunday School Broadened-Branch Schools 

Until the merger of 1886, when Rev. C. Sprunger's and 

Rev. S. F. Sprunger's congregations united, the Sunday School organization was largely independent of the church proper.

Business was transacted by the teachers, and it was not until a number of years later that the management and control was given into the hands of a Sunday School Committee elected by the church. After the union of the two congregations Rev. 

S. F. Sprunger sought to broaden the scope of the Sunday 

School by establishing neighboring branch schools. This was a decided advantage to the distant members from the angle of transportation. Among these branches were notably the Abraham Baumgartner school, and Striker church Sunday Schools.

The French township or the Christian Baumgartner church Sunday School was organized about the same time as the Berne church school in 1874. The information concerning this school may not be entirely correct since no records can be located. Rev. S. F. Sprunger served two congregations on alternate Sundays, those in the Berne and Christian Baumgartner churches. It may be that when Rev. Sprunger made his customary call at the latter church, he stayed in the afternoon to superintend a small Sunday School. C. M. Stauffer was active superintendent in the Berne church school during the Sundays that Brother Sprunger was absent. It is known that some people were active members for some time in both schools, especially during the limited period of time when the Berne school met every other Sunday, making attendance possible at both places. Peter Burkhalter, who was the first assistant superintendent of the Berne Sunday School, recalls vividly the experience of reading the first organization report before the French township school. Because of transportation difficulties the school at first met only in summer. It is thought that this Sunday School was discontinued in about 1880, a year before Rev. S. F. Sprunger moved to Berne, because of the entry of certain names in the records of the Berne school in that year. Better transportation, the new Berne church, and increased interest among the young people were important factors in effecting this change. 

The Sunday School in the Baumgartner school, now known as the Beer or District No. 4 school in Monroe township, was organized on May 5, 1889, with about 60 members.

The enrollment grew to over 100 during the summer and the following two summers, there being no sessions in winter. 

Joel Lehman and John J. Amstutz were superintendents; Joel 

Welty, assistant superintendent; F. G. Eichenberger and Jacob

U. Amstutz, secretaries. There were six German classes, and later additional ones were added to accommodate increased enrollment. Among the latter was a French class, taught by John Gerber; this was soon discontinued because the need no longer existed. This school was completely in sympathy with the main Sunday School and was organized because of transportation advantages.

The Striker church Sunday School, with Joel Welty as superintendent and Moses Moser, assistant superintendent, met for a summer or two in about 1887 or 1888. The group met in a church formerly located on the present Dennis Striker farm several miles north of Berne. Very little is known of the personnel and details of this school. The enrollment is thought to have been about 25 or 30.

John A. Sprunger First Song Leader; No Accompaniment 

This community has long been characterized as a musicloving one. No doubt, this trait was made manifest in the very first Sunday School sessions. On the other hand, this institution has also contributed much toward the development of this characteristic. The song services were enthusiastic; singing was from memory and from the heart. A half-hours' singing followed the class periods in the early Sunday School. 

John A. Sprunger was the first song leader and led the group without the help of accompaniment; instrumental music was considered sinful and was not allowed. D. C. Neuenschwander for many years inspired hearty and general participation in this part of the service. 

The first recorded use of song books was in 1880 when a special collection was taken toward the procurement of the "Jubeltöne," a hymnal in use at that time. It is interesting to note that personal contributions toward this cause as low as

twenty-five cents were individually recognized in the treasurer's book. The first purchase consisted of about twenty-five hymnals which were carefully distributed and collected each Sunday. An interesting resolution specified the proper distribution, one book to each small class and two books for each large class. Racks were soon built on the backs of the seats which eliminated this weekly distribution. As the years progressed, the copies became worn and obsolete and were followed by other publications, some of which are: Frohe Botschaft, Perle, Evangeliumslieder, Die Kleine Palme, Evangeliumssänger, Songs for Service, Hymns of Praise, and the present 

Tabernacle Hymns No. 3. 

First Accompaniment on Small Pedal Organ 

Although instrumental accompaniment had been introduced into the church some years previously, it was not until 1901 that opposition was sufficiently overcome to pass a resolution sanctioning the use of such accompaniment in the Sun day School. Mary Lehman (Mrs. Lewis Reiff) and Rosa M. 

Lehman (Mrs. M. M. Baumgartner) were the first accompanists, playing a small pedal organ, an instrument said to have been placed in the church by Dan Sprunger and J. F. Lehman. 

Recognition is due Mrs. M. M. Baumgartner for her contribution of many years of faithful service as accompanist for the 

Sunday School and other organizations. Mrs. Baumgartner, after receiving her appointment from S. F. Sprunger in 1902, served continuously until in recent years when several musicians have shared this task, each serving a portion of the year. The fact that musical accompaniment was a blessing and a help to group singing was confirmed in 1902 when a large new Vocalion pedal organ was purchased by the church and the Sunday School. Rented pianos were also employed until the installation of the new pipe organ and piano. E. A. Luginbill, Wilbur B. Nussbaum, and M. M. Baumgartner have served as choristers in recent years. S. F. Sprunger and J. F. Lehman, who superintended the school for many years, also directed the singing during the opening and closing periods.

Bible Memory Given Thorough and Enthusiastic Expression 

The German Bible was used exclusively as a basis of study in the original adult classes. The younger scholars used the "Bibelgeschichte" (Bible Stories), a book specially adapted to this age. The quarterly was introduced in 1880 and was definitely established three years later. It has since been used as a basis of study in almost all classes. The lessons used were not standardized, but were local with the respective publishing houses. The lesson outline today is furnished by the International Council of Religious Education. The present quarterly is published by our own Mennonite General Conference.

A much neglected feature in our Sunday Schools today was given thorough expression in the early days-Bible memory. Testimonials to the value and blessings received from this phase of the Sunday School are frequent. As a motive to induce Bible memory, recognition was given in the form of special awards. It was considered a signal honor to receive the final token of recognition, a card with the class roll printed on it, for having completed a series of memory assignments. 

The usual lessons, at times, were interspersed with memory assignments, such as the Books of the Bible. This work was revived to some extent in recent years in the State Sunday 

School Bible Memory and Story Telling Contests in which our school participated.

Everyone who attended the primary classes within the last fifty-five years recalls very vividly the "Bildersaal" (Picture Roll). This pictorial aid was introduced in 1883 and has been an important supplement in the clarifying of the lessons to the primary pupils. It is still in use today. 

Another method of attracting the attention of the Sunday School children in the teaching of the lessons was the "motto." 

Impressions received from these colored blackboard illustrations will never be forgotten. The title, theme, and Golden 

Text of the lesson were printed neatly in German and a corresponding illustration drawn attractively for the children.

These were hand-drawn for a few years by Dr. E.

Franz and later by Abraham Boegli. Superintendent S. F. Sprunger supplemented these "mottoes," as they were called, with special applications.

Of the early Sunday School papers, the best known was the "Lämmerweide" which was used for thirty-five years. 

Other papers, some of which were given to adults as well, were: Himmelsmanna, Zur Heimat, Heidenwelt, Evangeliumsmissionsfreund, Kleine Glocke, Buds of Hope, Dewdrops, and the Kinderbote. The latter has been forwarded to each family of the Sunday School for the last fifty-three years. The great influence which these papers have exerted upon the life and character of the individual and community can never be fully comprehended.

Rapid Introduction of English Language 

The change of language in the literature and class discussion of the Sunday School was incidental to the change in education, national progress and the World War. C. A. Neuenschwander, who served as secretary for more than fortythree years, wrote his annual reports and the minutes of various business meetings in neat German script. E. W. Baumgartner read the first English organization report in 1919. 

There was probably an English class as early as 1878 or 1879. 

The first record of such classes was in 1880-English classes taught by C. M. Stauffer and C. A. Neuenschwander. The members of the former class were Burton Eley, Allen Zimmerman, Thomas Zimmerman, and Edward Liddy; those of 

Mr. Neuenschwander's class were Adam Schroll and Harry

Zimmerman. Other teachers who taught early English classes were Edward H. Schowalter, Kathryn S. Lehman, Solomon Hirschy, Anna D. Sprunger, Dina Lehman, B. F. Welty,

Moses Moser, and Amos Reusser. The following items taken from the treasurer's reports will afford an interesting perspective of the rapidly diminishing use of the German language. The total number of German and English quarterlies purchased are listed for the years mentioned.

1910 740 German quarterlies 55 English 1917 650 German 

660 English 

1938 225 German 1165 English 

Teachers Trained Thoroughly with Methods Available 

The success of our Sunday School can be largely attributed to the careful training and selection of teachers. The first requisite has always been that the teacher possess the vital spiritual life himself. Since 1891 normal classes have been maintained for the training of prospective teachers. 

Sometimes difficulties were encountered, and interest had to be further stimulated. The teacher training classes met regularly during the usual Sunday School session in the early days. In recent years they have met on Wednesday evenings. 

At one time catechetical instruction was also under the tutelage of the Sunday School, and here particularly future teachers were schooled in the great fundamental doctrines of the Christian faith.

Another custom which contributed tremendously to the efficiency of the teaching force was that of the Friday evening teachers' meeting. The teachers' meeting was first called in 

1880. The earlier meetings were held bi-monthly, and each teacher felt it his sacred obligation to attend every session. 

Some of the older teachers recall the indomitable spirt of their fellows who permitted neither mud nor blizzard, nor any surmountable obstacle, to stand in the way of their presence at these sessions. Much profit in the way of teaching preparation was received in discussing together the advancing lesson. 

In one particular case, four lessons were studied at a single meeting. As was mentioned before, the teachers transacted the business of the school in the early meetings. The meetings were held for many years on the second floor of the Sprunger, Lehman and Company store.

Teachers have frequently had opportunity to become fam-

iliar with new plans and methods of teaching. This Sunday 

School has been well represented at Sunday School conventions. J. F. Lehman was a much respected leader in the 

State Sunday School Association. As early as 1892 Amos 

Reusser represented our school at the Sunday School convention at Pulaski, Iowa. This school and district was represented at the International Sunday School Convention in Switzerland in 1913 with J. P. Habegger as delegate.

The teaching objectives were made emphatic and were thoroughly impressed upon the teachers in the early days. 

The teaching was not to be mechanical, but the Word of God was to become vibrant in the hearts and lives of the members. 

The importance of not only stressing methods, but teaching 

God's revelation in its fullness, was challenged.

Much Opposition to First Christmas Program 

Almost throughout the history of our Sunday School there have been gala days for the children. Parents have, except in the beginning, cooperated wholeheartedly in making these events impressive. The annual Christmas program is always inspirational with its appropriate exercises, songs, and recitations. The original programs were given either morning, afternoon, or evening on Christmas day, frequently replacing the usual Sunday School session. Children were assigned recitations for the first Christmas program in 1880, but it was not until two years later that these programs received the official approval of the board and members. Parents' opinions concerning this event had been carefully solicited. Accusations were numerous that an undertaking of this kind was too progressive and not in conformity with the established Sunday School principles. The extent of this sentiment can be realized when we learn of one parent who, as punishment, refused to give his children Christmas candy if they should so much as attend the event. A similar reaction was manifested when the Christmas tree was introduced in 1887. This was indeed a memorable occasion for the children for it was equipped with lights and decorations. Rev. Sprunger had tried

several years before to have a tree but there was too much opposition. When he suggested the idea to the Baumgartner 

Church School the majority was in favor. So it was they who had the first Christmas tree-they also had the first Christmas program. The custom of presenting gifts to the younger children has been continued since the early days with the exception of a few holidays, designated as White Christmas, when the children brought their gifts of money instead. There have been occasions when the annual Christmas program was given wholly or in part by the adult members. In 1915 a picturesque 

Christmas program was given by the adults consisting of chorals and selections used previously in the church and 

Sunday School. 

Patriotic Tribute Rendered on "Kinderfest" Celebration

In the early summer, usually on July 4th, a patriotic tribute was rendered in the form of a children's program, commonly known as the "Kinderfest." A committee was appointed to improvise a platform and seating accommodations for the many who took part and attended this event at Lehman's Grove. Included in the programs were drills, pantomimes, recitations, and patriotic songs. The community would turn out enmasse with picnic baskets and lunch equipment. 

Custom and tradition made attendance an obligation. The significance of the day never was lost sight of, and the programs were deeply impressive to both young and old. 

Auxiliary Departments of Sunday School

A new department was established in 1909 to facilitate Sunday School fellowship and instruction for those unable to attend the school-the Home Department. Many infirm people and mothers with children found attendance at the regular Sunday School very difficult because of lack of convenient and comfortable transportation. This accounts for the high membership of 144 at its inception in contrast with that of today, sixteen. I. A. Sommer was the first superintendent; he was succeeded by Sarah Ann Sprunger( Mrs. Ben Sprunger)

who served very capably for almost eighteen years. Her long service was terminated by her sudden death in 1928, and Mrs. 

D. C. Neuenschwander took charge for the rest of the year. Mrs. E. A. Luginbill has been head of the department since 1929.

The Cradle Roll (Wiegen-Register) was added the following year, 1910. Mrs. Della Neuhauser has served the longest as superintendent of this department, thirteen years. Succeeding officers have been Mrs. T. H. Soldner, Mrs. Fred Rohrer, Mrs, Maria Neuenschwander, Mrs. LaDoyt McCrory, and 

Mrs. C. H. Suckau. The membership consists of infant children of parent Sunday School members. The purpose of this department is to induce Sunday School instruction in the home among the children and early enrollment into the school. The first and present membership are almost identical, one hundred twenty-eight and one hundred twenty-six.

Financial Growth of Sunday School 

The increased scope and activity of the Sunday School are made graphic in the annual financial reports. For the purpose of comparison and to give a visual picture of the growth, the following annual expenditures in intervals of twenty years are given: 1880-$73.88 222.99 1900 1920 1073.43 

19371814.54 

The receipts are composed of weekly children's and monthly adult offerings. It is of interest to note that the first monthly offering taken in 1883 amounted to $4.80. A substantial portion of the receipts is not needed in the current expenses of the school and is put into the church benevolence fund. Contributions have been made regularly toward state and conference Sunday School work.

Noise Competition an Early Problem 

From the school's beginning until 1937 classes of all ages met in the main auditoriums of the various churches. How-

ever, in recent years classes had become more and more cramped in the limited space available, and teachers of children's classes, particularly, had found it increasingly difficult to teach because of noise competition resulting from the great number of classes reciting in one auditorium. This problem was a major one in the early Sunday School and has never reached satisfactory solution. A resolution was passed in 1883 asking teachers to modulate their voices. This problem has been lessened materially by moving the primary and junior classes to a newly equipped basement auditorium. This change was made in 1937, and many advantages have been realized in this separation. The young children meet in this spacious room for enthusiastic exercises in the opening and closing periods. Numerous individual class rooms are also provided in the basement for class recitation. Frieda Lehman has been the capable superintendent since the change was made and Ralph Amstutz, the assistant. 

A change made earlier as a result of the same problems was in 1891 when the primary classes were united into two large groups, the boys and the girls. Before this time classification had been made into smaller groups. A. A. Lehman recalls many interesting experiences as teacher of the first boys' class on the east balcony of the old church. Sarah C. 

Sprunger (Mrs. Jonas Soldner) was teacher of the first girls' class after this change was made.

Only Four Superintendents During the 65-Year-Existence of the Sunday School

The memorializing of the Sunday School would be incomplete without acknowledging the work of some of the officers and teachers who through persistence, prayer, and faithful performance of duty have contributed inestimable good to the Sunday School and community. A brief retrospect brings to our attention a colorful manifestation of human efforts in seeking to effect the will of God. 

Rev. S. F. Sprunger, in addition to serving two churches, was also responsible for the founding and success of our

present Sunday School. His supreme trust in God and his strong characteristics as a leader were made manifest here. 

He served continuously as head of the school for more than thirty-two years, 1874 to 1906, braving every onslaught of opposition, wisely guiding his co-workers, and marching steadily forward toward his goal. He was a man commissioned with a heavenly vision and he strove for the fulfillment of that vision with the fortitude of a Paul.

In 1907 J. F. Lehman stepped into the place of responsibility occupied such a long time by the founder of the school. 

The Sunday School continued to prosper under his devoted leadership. Motivated by the desire to see the Sunday School and community prosper spiritually, his work has been an im-. portant factor in the development of these two institutions. 

He was a faithful steward in this position for twenty-two years, 1907 to 1928.

Succeeding him was Edison Sprunger who served capably for nine years. At the present time Freeman Burkhalter superintends the work of the school. An interesting observation is the fact that during the entire sixty-five years of the school there have been only four superintendents.

C. A. Neuenschwander- 

Distinction of Longest 

Service as Officer 

Much credit must be given to C. A. Neuenschwander for having the longest record of service as an officer-more than forty-three years, 1874 to 1917. 

Through all these years he was secretary of the school and left neat and legible records for posterity. A beautiful custom of this strong character who left a 

Christian A. Neuenschwander

wonderful testimony with his fellowmen was the insertion of a little prayer or a word of praise below his yearly written reports and above his endorsement. E. W. Baumgartner, Palmer Moser, and Lowell Sprunger have succeeded him in the order mentioned. There also have been only four secretaries during the entire history of the school. 

Teachers Who Have Earned Long Service Recognition 

A number of teachers have distinguished themselves with a remarkable record of fifty years or more of service. Among these are J. P. Habegger, A. A. Lehman, D. C. Sprunger (deceased), and J. F. Sprunger.

The following have served as teachers from forty to fortynine years (list not complete): Menno Burkhalter, Joel Lehman, Helena Liechty, Judith Liechty, E. A. Luginbill, A. N. 

Sprunger, Ben Sprunger and D. J. Sprunger.

Sunday School Members in Productive Fields of Endeavor 

It has long been said that an institution is known by its fruits; the Sunday School is no exception. The teachings of the great evangelical truths which have been ingrained for many years into the lives of its members have been translated into many departments of service. Nothing bespeaks more convincingly the wholesome, Godward influence of the Sunday School in shaping the lives and destinies of its pupils than a panoramic survey of the various fields of activity in which the members or former members are engaged. There are hundreds of industrious, God-fearing farmers who have dignified their work by establishing influential homes in the community. Successful business men have been reaping the fruits of honesty and integrity in their dealing with countless people whom they contact. There have been through the years almost innumerable public school teachers, Christian men and women, coming from the ranks of our Sunday School, who are wielding inestimable power in the building of character in the boys and

girls throughout the land. There are also the many talented and learned men who have earned positions of trust and responsibility in the colleges, universities, and conservatories. 

Some of these are lending their talents to the training and development of personalities in the classroom; others are in administrative positions. Some are in the service of the Lord as ministers of music in prominent churches of our land. 

Scores of faithful husbandmen have given their lives to full time Christian service proclaiming the gospel to people of every color and every clime as ministers and missionaries. 

We must not forget to give due recognition to the many of the fair sex who have married men in the above callings and who are perpetuating in their homes the principles of love and truth learned in our Sunday School. Can any one pretend to doubt that the blessing of God has been on this great, great work these last sixty-five years?

Rich Heritage Given 

The splendid growth of this institution is certainly indicative of God's blessing and approval of this important work of the church. Countless men and women owe their gratitude to the faithful leaders and teachers who have pointed them to the Way of Life in the classroom. Our young people are given a rare and splendid heritage upon which to build. Visitors from far and wide are impressed by the many strong and unusual features of this largest school in the Conference. It has been an important factor in adding lustre to the life of the church and community of Berne. In speaking of Sunday School-the one man who stands out clearest in the minds of many is J. F. Lehman. Sunday after Sunday, for twenty-two years, he took his place as leader of this important church organization.

JAPHET F. LEHMAN 

Japhet F. Lehman was born on a farm adjoining the present corporation limits of 

Berne, Indiana, on June 8, 1860. He was the son of Rev.

Peter S. and Verena (Sprung er) Lehman, who were na tives of Domong, Canton Bern, 

Switzerland, and emigrated to the United States in 1852. His father was the pioneer pastor of the First Mennonite Church at Berne and consequently his only son was brought up in the Christian faith and doctrine of the Mennonite

Church. 

Japhet F. Lehman 

In April 1868 his parents, with their children, moved to Missouri and in the year 1880 he returned to Berne which was his home until his death. 

His formal education was limited to eighteen months in the subscription schools of Hickory County, Missouri, and one term of three months at the Moody Bible Institute, Chicago, which was the first term that the school was in session. 

During his early manhood he was identified with various business enterprises at Berne, becoming partner in a general store, a flour mill and a harrow factory. After a fire in 1888 which wiped out these enterprises he engaged in well drilling and later in the insurance business. In 1891 he became the local manager of the Logansport Natural Gas Company and held it until the service was discontinued in 1907. He was director and vice president of the First Bank of Berne from 1906 to 1919 and its president from 1919 until his death, during which period it enjoyed a steady growth. He was instrumental in organizing the Swiss Milk Company of Berne in

1908, and was an officer of the company until it was sold in 

1917. He was also president of the Globe Hatchery at Berne from its inception in 1919 until his death, and was a director of the Berne Furniture Company which he helped to organize in 1925. This identification with various business enterprises in the Berne community reflected his business philosophy that local business projects should be sponsored so that opportunity for work may be offered to the citizens and the general welfare of the community enhanced.

With these private interests he combined diversified and fruitful public service. He was president of the first town council in 1887, treasurer of the first school board appointed the same year, first president of the Berne Fire Company, organized in 1895, postmaster during 1897-1908, president of the board of education, 1914-1917, elected president of the Berne Business Men's Association when it was organized in 1914. He organized and was president of the lyceum lecture course committee, was member of the village park board during 1925-1932, and a member of the Adams county board of review and of the Adams county tax adjustment board in 

1932. He was a charter member of the German Temperance

Society which was organized in February 1886 and this society was very influential in banishing the saloon from Berne more than thirty years ago.

Mr. Lehman was an ardent advocate of temperance and sobriety.

In the First Mennonite Church at Berne, of which he was a faithful member, in the denomination and the church at large he held various positions of trust and responsibility. At the time of his death the Berne Witness stated, "next to his uncle, the late Rev. S. F. Sprunger, Jeff Lehman was the most influential member of the local Mennonite church with reference to the growth of its influence in the community." He was elected superintendent of its Sunday School in 1907 and served until 1928. He organized and taught the first teachers' training class in the Sunday School in 1907. 

Mr. Lehman participated in the organization and founding of the Christian Endeavor society and the Choral society of the

church. For many years previous to his election as superintendent he taught a Sunday School class and was assistant superintendent. At the time of his death he was preparing the Sunday School lesson which he planned to teach that 

Sunday morning. He was treasurer of the finance committee of the church which obtained the subscriptions for the money used to erect the present building of the church. 

As manager of the Mennonite Book Concern from 1896 

until 1930, he developed an extensive, self-supporting publishing business to serve the educational and spiritual needs of the General Conference of Mennonites of North America. 

He was also a member of the Board of Publication of the 

General Conference for more than thirty years. At the time of his death, Dr. S. K. Mosiman, then President of Bluffton 

College, stated that "Perhaps no man had the confidence and good will of the General Conference of the Mennonite Church to the degree that Mr. Lehman enjoyed, and I speak from a wide acquaintance of the work of the conference over a period of forty years."

For many years he was a member of the executive committee of the International Council of Religious Education and for twenty-five years he served on the executive board of the 

Indiana Council of Religious Education. 

Although his formal education was restricted to a few terms, he had a very alert and active mind; he was judicious and prudent in his opinions and decisions; he possessed an innate genius for assimilating a large fund of information and functional knowledge by wide reading and keen observation. 

He had a zealous desire that not only his own children should be provided with better and more adequate educational opportunities than he enjoyed, but that desirable opportunities should be offered the children of his community and church. 

Consequently, he took an active interest and supported all worth while educational projects in the community and the church. He was a member of the first committee appointed by the Middle District Conference in 1896 to plan for the establishment of a Church college. For more than thirty-four years

he was a member of the Board of Trustees of Bluffton College, Bluffton, Ohio, and President of the board for many years. 

In the funeral sermon Dr. S. K. Mosiman epitomized his philosophy of life,-"Righteousness, love and faith, therefore became the triune of his life. He hated all forms of unrighteousness and sin. Therefore, he was first in every organization for social and civic improvement, as well as a leader in his church which he loved. Naturally, when a man links himself with God, his very acts assume a different character. 

Surely his work of righteousness brought him peace and the effect of his righteousness was quietness and confidence forever... and to the family, may I say that your father left you a wonderful legacy, the heritage of a great and noble soul, a rare spirit among men-yours to enjoy." He was married to Elizabeth, daughter of Christian Neuenschwander of Berne, on February 7, 1883, and they had six children: Rosa Mina, wife of Menas M. Baumgartner of Berne; 

Gustav Adolph of Rochester, N. Y.; Clinton Arnold of Villa 

Park, Ill.; Gertrude Emma, wife of William A. Albrecht of 

Columbia, Mo.; Clarence Oliver of Geneseo, N. Y., and Florence 

Elizabeth, wife of Vernon C. Ramseyer, Oskaloosa, Iowa. He died at Berne, November 13, 1932.

THE LIBRARY 

The Church Library was organized in 1882; thus it has given fifty-five years of continuous service to our church. 

Records are very meager and not always accurate, but reports for thirty-nine years show that approximately 45,547 books were read in that length of time. This is an average of 1,168 books per year for these thirty-nine years. If we may assume that this average was maintained for the sixteen years in which no record of the number of readings is given, then the approximate total of books read is 64,198. 

The largest number of books read in a single year was 3,615 in 1899. In fact, the period from 1896 to 1900 may well

be called a "Golden Era" because of the great interest shown and the thousands of books drawn from the library. 

It might be of interest to know how many patrons there were in all these years, how many of our people availed themselves of the opportunity of using the library. Here the records are even scantier. They show that in twenty-one years there were 4,067 readers, making an average of 194 readers per year. 

If in the remaining thirty-four years this average was kept up, then we may conjecture a total of 10,663 library patrons. The year 1896 stands high with 552 readers; 1903 is second with 

390, and 1931 third with 308 readers.

Individual honors should be given to one person (not named) who read 70 books in 1906; to an eighty-year-old man (not named) who read 35 books in 1895; to Grandfather John 

Soldner, who read 65 books in 1899, at the age of eighty-four; to Peter Gilliom who read 35 of the 40 German books read in 

1930; Evelyn Stucky who read 55 books in 1935 and 63 in 1937, and to Henrietta Sprunger (Nussbaum) who for two consecutive years (1929-1930) ranked first with 54 books to her credit.

Thus we have the bare circulation statistics of our library. 

But there is an interesting story back of the figures-the story of the beginning of the library, its purpose and aim, the rules and by-laws laid down by its founders,-in fact the entire history of the library is fascinating.

The actual organization of the library took place on February 8, 1882, although the need for it had been felt for some time previous. This need is expressed in the purpose of the organization,-namely, "to provide Christian literature and uplifting reading matter for both young and old members of the church." At this time our people did not have access to the many magazines, religious periodicals and books which are now available, and the necessity for a church library was very real. 

The first committee members were Rev. S. F. Sprunger, chairman; C. M. Stauffer, secretary; D. C. Sprunger, treasurer; 

Peter Bixel, Solomon Wittwer, Christian B. Lehman, and David

S. Sprunger, librarian. These men of God drafted Rules and

By-laws to govern the new organization and also stated what type of books should be kept in the library. Besides stating the duties of the officers, the by-laws included these items: 

6. The funds for the Library shall be secured by gifts and free will offerings whenever the committee deems it necessary. Books may be donated to the Library. 

7. The readers shall be responsible for the books they take out of the Library. In case of loss or damage done to the book, the reader shall refund for same. 

8. Only religious books or books endorsing religious principles shall be kept in the Library. 

The kinds of books which the Library shall contain, as stated by the committee, are these: 

Uplifting books

Church History Nature Stories 

Travel Poetry 

Christian Biography

Historical books Books of Geography and Ethnology Good Christian Story books Home and Foreign Missions Children's Story books 

Since the first librarian, David S. Sprunger, finished his term, twenty librarians have succeeded him, serving for varying lengths of time. Dr. Amos Reusser, one of the earlier librarians, held the office for six years, the longest time on record. Rev. I. A. Sommer and J. B. Welty each served five years. In more recent years, Carl Luginbill and LeRoy Stauffer each acted as librarian for four years. There have also been a large number of assistant librarians. 

The number of books on the shelves has varied considerably from year to year. Starting with only 74 books in 1882, the library now has 908 books. At first there were many more German books than English, but gradually the English took the lead. New books were added from time to time, and unused or worn-out copies were taken from the shelves. As early as 1897 books were disposed of at an "Antique Book Auction," receipts being $9.50. Another time many books were given to a missionary, and still others were sold

at annual Thanksgiving sales. In 1933 quite a number of worn copies were given to the children. 

The year 1902 was one of progress, for 146 new books were added, the old ones were recovered, and a new catalog was printed. Again in 1915 changes were made. The library was closed three months during which these changes and additions were made. The following year great interest was shown in the library, and it is noteworthy that books of biography were the most popular.

The year 1919 is important in the history of the library. 

Until then it was located in the old library room in the east end of the church, and accommodations were not so good. 

Patrons had to call for their books while standing in the lobby which was often cold and drafty. Also, the librarians were troubled with cold feet. Accordingly a window was built into the room from the interior of the church, and readers called for their books at this window. At this time a thorough “house-cleaning" was made of the books and 247 were removed, many of them totally unused or badly worn; 110 new books were added. The German records say that a general “freshening up" was needed.

Offerings were provided for in the by-laws of the organization for the purchase of new books. In 1922 the library committee sacrificed these offerings in favor of funds for the European sufferers, but in the following year a plea for new books brought in $30.00. One thousand new catalogs were also printed. 

On March 19, 1924, Rev. I. A. Sommer, chairman of the library committee, passed away. He had been connected with the Church Library for many, many years. 

The year 1925 is the only year on record when no books were lost. It was also at this time that interest began to wane in the library, because "the 'Good Literature Committee' of the Christian Endeavor Society has started a book table, where everyone can take good books." This table was very popular because it was so convenient just to take a book rather than to call for name and number in the library. The library com-

mittee, realizing the need, was forced to devise another way of drawing out books and stimulating interest. 

Accordingly, in 1926 the following plan, written by Rev. 

C. H. van der Smissen, secretary of the committee, was adopted: "Since the library room is too small to allow anybody to go in and to select the books desired, we would propose that the congregation give permission to the library committee to bring the book cases into the teachers' meeting room; through the trustees of the congregation to get book cases for the books which the 'Good Literature Committee' has given us, and for some reference works which we hope to purchase, a table on which to read or to write, a rug that the feet get not so cold on the cement floor, six chairs and a good lamp above the table; and that we can have the library open at least one evening in the week at a time when the church is warmed anyway. Like it has been before, the library would be open on Sunday before the Sunday school and after service; also Sunday afternoon and in the evening before society. "If this is granted, people can select their books or use reference works. The committee would supervise the work, give out the books and put down in the record the names of those who get books and the number and title of the book. We believe, that since there is no good library in town the church library in this manner could be a blessing to those young people who want to read."

In connection with this plan 200 new books were added and the old card system was abolished. Patrons could now call for books without having to present their cards. 

In 1931 a new and improved catalog system was adopted. 

A complete list of the books in the library was sent to the Sunday School Times for recommendation, and the list was highly approved. A complete set of Matthew Henry's commentary was put on the shelves. Over 2,000 books were drawn out, showing new interest. 

In cooperation with the "Good Literature Committee" the library committee, in 1936, was granted the use of the book table to display new non-fiction books. The committee also

appointed several persons to give book reviews during the year. 

These two factors helped to stimulate interest in the reading of books of a devotional and inspirational nature. 

Last year, 180 new books were placed in the library, some of these, however, being replacements.

And so the work of the library goes on year after year, rendering a definite and valuable service to the church. Cold figures and statistics cannot measure the blessing it has been. 

Only those who have availed themselves of the opportunity the library affords can know and understand the contribution it has made.

THE TEMPERANCE SOCIETY 

Berne was founded in the year 1871. It received its name through Joe Crawford, at that time one of the County Commissioners, who suggested the new station on the nearly completed G. R. & I. railroad be called Berne, in honor of the Bernese settlers of this community. 

It used to be a "wet" town. Very early in its existence the first saloon was established. The Swiss people did not know the saloon by that name. Some called it "grocery." 

A certain man by the name of Taylor secured a lot from old Abraham Lehman. When Mr. Lehman asked him what he wanted to build, he was told a saloon. But Mr. Lehman had never heard the word before and inquired what a saloon was. He was told it was a place to eat and drink. Mr. Lehman thinking he meant a restaurant, sold him the lot and the first saloon in Berne became a reality. 

Mr. Lehman and all the people around Berne soon found out what a saloon was. Rows and fights were soon a commonplace. In fact, so common that a dog fight attracted more attention than a saloon brawl.

At one time two young fellows, who had started fighting, rolled clear across Main Street locked in each other's arms. 

When the fight was over one of the boys missed part of an ear and the other, part of a finger which each had severed

from the other with his teeth. Women and children and teams suffered much from debauchees, who often left the teams standing at the hitching rack from noon till nearly midnight and let their families wait for them either at home or in stores in town.

When Berne had come to have a population of only about four hundred, four saloons were doing a thriving business. 

Almost everybody patronized the saloons. Not only men who made no profession of Christianity, but church members and even preachers frequented the saloons and took their glass of beer as well as anybody else. Fathers would go in with their sons, each taking his drink, this belonging to their routine of life. When farmers brought their grain and livestock to town, they always expected a glass of beer together with their pay check. After going into a store to purchase a bill of goods, the merchant again had to "set it up" before settlement was made. 

Nearly everybody kept wine and cider in the cellar at home, and when there was a social gathering, log rolling, barn raising, grain threshing or even a wedding feast, the custom was to serve wine and cider to the people. 

While there were a number of people who could take their glass of wine and beer regularly and would never become intoxicated, there were others who could not control their appetites and would drink to excess. They were made unfit to attend to their duties and were neglecting their homes and families. It was deplorable at times to see the many drunken sots staggering around outside the saloons, on the sidewalks, in the streets, and sometimes in the stores of the merchants. 

The families of many of these were often in dire need of the necessities of life but were left unprovided. Poverty and suffering were in evidence on every hand. 

Even though it was the popular custom in those days to patronize the saloons, there were a number of Christian men in our town and community who abhorred this custom and shunned intoxicating liquors, preaching both openly and privately the doctrine of temperance. One Christian townsman advocated the very unpopular idea of total abstinence. 5

During the winter of 1885-1886 some of these townsmen had several informal "counter" meetings, reviewing the awful situation in which the town was obliged to be at that time and on February 4, 1886, nine of them came together and organized the "Christlicher Mäszigkeits-Verein von Bern" 

(Christian Temperance Society of Berne). 

These nine charter members of this society were: J. F.

Lehman, Moses Moser, Joel Welty, John A. Sprunger, Levi 

A. Sprunger, Fred Eichenberger, C. C. Sprunger, Eli Riesen and N. G. Fankhauser. J. F. Lehman was appointed as chairman, who with Joel Welty, Moses Moser and two other charter members were to draw up a paper stating the rules, regulations, and purpose of this organization and present them at the next meeting which was to be held February 18, 1886. 

This first committee was appointed for a term of six weeks only. As per record a total of twenty-three men joined the new movement this same evening it was organized and on the following day seventeen more signed their names.

When the second meeting convened Moses Moser delivered a stirring address on the subject: "The Evils Caused by Intox icating Liquors." A second message followed given by D. C. 

Sprunger on the subject: "Our Duty as Christians." He emphasized that it is not enough that we personally shun intemperance, but that it is our duty as Christians to do what we can to keep this drink evil under control. The object of this society, originally, seems to have been more that of an Anti- Saloon society than that of strictly temperance, that of creating a sentiment to compel the saloonkeepers to run their business within the bounds of the law. 

Membership grew very rapidly despite the fact that many people of Berne and community were opposed to this movement. Even a great many church members of our own Mennonite church, as well as members of the neighboring churches, did not sanction this undertaking. By the end of the first year, however, seventy-nine had joined the society and were very enthusiastic in supporting the work the society proposed to do.

In creating a temperance sentiment among the people of the community no one has done more than Rev. S. F. Sprunger, pastor of our Mennonite church for over thirty years. Long before the temperance society was founded, Rev. Sprunger took a radical stand for temperance and abstinence, preached against intemperance from pulpit and platform, distributed temperance leaflets and periodicals, and was thus successful in influencing the people against the use of intoxicating liquors. 

It was chiefly through his influence that the organization of the temperance society was instituted. Next to Rev. Sprunger should be mentioned J. F. Lehman, the society's first President and head of the group of organizers, and for twenty-two years superintendent of the Mennonite Sunday School. He was also one of the successful pioneer business men of Berne. 

D. C. Sprunger, who joined the society the next day after it organized, was an ardent supporter of the temperance cause and a fluent and influential speaker. 

J. F. Lehman and D. C. Sprunger each served fourteen years as chairman of the temperance organization. John Christian Rohrer, Berne's veteran tailor, a native of Switzerland, was a man who also exerted a great influence in the cause of temperance. He came to this place in 1885. Being a total abstainer himself, he preached and insisted on total abstinence, and is still remembered by those who knew him as a very faithful member of the temperance society.

Fred Rohrer also joined the temperance society soon after it was organized and was a very faithful worker in the interest of temperance from its very beginning. It can truly be said of him that "He labored more than they all" to close the saloons of our town and make Berne "dry." For more detailed information on the "Saloon Fight in Berne," procure the booklet that bears that name and read it. Edited by Mr. Fred Rohrer, copies are still available at the Berne Witness Company office. 

At the second meeting where Moses Moser and D. C.

Sprunger made their rousing speeches, filling the people with enthusiasm for temperance, the members present decided that

the name of the society should be "Christlicher Mäszigkeits- 

Verein von Bern" (Christian Temperance Society of Berne). 

A committee was also appointed at this meeting to formulate a tentative constitution for the society and present it at the next meeting on March 4, 1886. The members of this committee were J. F. Lehman, Joel Welty, D. B. Hirschler, A. M. 

Locher, D. C. Sprunger, Moses Moser and Joel Lehman.
