At the March 4, 1886 meeting, the tentative constitution was presented, some changes made, and adopted, and at the following meeting it was decided that the constitution be printed as soon as possible. Officers were also elected for a term of three months. These with the committee members constituted the program committee. The term of service of the committee was later changed to six months; this went in effect November 6, 1886, and still later the term of service was increased to one year.

For the first three years the meetings were held bi-weekly, twenty-four to twenty-six meetings a year, including some special sessions. Since 1889, regular monthly meetings were held except when special occasion called for an extra session. 

Where were these meetings held? At first not in any church building; people were opposed to it. Not even the 

Mennonite Church would open her doors, even though the pastor, Rev. Sprunger, joined the society soon after its birth and was much in favor for what the society stood. Therefore, the organization had to look for other quarters to give their programs. So during the winter months the members assembled in a hall above the Sprunger & Lehman Clothing Store. 

It was the old "Champion Block" brick building, the eastern part of which was the first brick store erected in Berne in the year 1881. In this hall the charter members of the temperance society met when they organized and also when they drafted the constitution for the society. 

In the summer time the meetings were held in the schoolhouses throughout the neighborhood. The first temperance meeting of the society, which is recorded as being held in our old Mennonite Church was that of May 30, 1887, more than

a year after the society was founded. After that, opposition by our church officials and church members began to dwindle away. For many years the church has now been the place where the temperance society has met regularly and carried on its work.

During the first few years after the society had started, the programs given by its members were almost exclusively in the German language. The constitution was also printed in German. The addresses were German, singing, including special numbers and congregational singing, was German. A 

German booklet "Lieder zur Ehre des Erretters" was used for a long time as a temperance song book. Readings, essays, dialogues, and declamations were mostly given in German. 

German tracts on temperance and anti-tobacco leaflets, also in German, were occasionally distributed at the meetings and a German paper "Der Arbeiter Freund" was regularly distributed at the close of the meetings for many years. Many out-of-town men who were engaged to speak at the meetings on special occasions such as annual meetings, also spoke in German. Among others who came here and gave German addresses were Dr. Frederick Munz, of Cincinnati, Ohio; A. J. 

Bucher, Cincinnati, Ohio, and Phillip Vollmer, of Dayton, Ohio.

These men are still remembered by many of the older members of the society, for they delivered very powerful messages. 

Gradually, however, the English language came more and more into use. More frequently English speakers from out of town were procured to speak at the meetings. As the younger talent of the society began to take active part in the programs they preferred to use the English language. 

Many years ago the German predominated and English was the exception-today it is just the reverse. Seldom are German addresses given to public audiences and music in the 

German language is rarely heard.

The "Christlicher Mäszigkeits-Verein von Bern" exerted a great influence in the upbuilding of the people of Berne and

community. In time it created an anti-saloon sentiment. People began to see what harm the saloons were doing in the community. They also realized that it was next to impossible to keep them under control. Controlling the saloon evil had been emphasized by a speaker at the second meeting of the society as being the duty of the Christian people of Berne and community.

The saloonkeepers always over-stepped the laws. So finally the temperance people concluded the only way to control them was to exterminate them. This monstrous job was undertaken in the year 1902. At that time the town was having an oil boom. Oil men came, moving in in such numbers that houses could not be built fast enough to accommodate them. 

But most of these were a rather undesirable class of citizens, living from hand to mouth, many of them spending a greater part of their salaries in the saloons which were running wide open without any regard for the law whatsoever. The better class of the people of Berne were afraid of this foreign element and the influence it might have on our community. So they decided to put forth an earnest effort to close the saloons. 

They thought now was the time before they became entrenched behind this re-enforcement of bad men. 

On September 9, 1902, a remonstrance campaign was planned. The ministers of the town had a meeting at the parsonage of the German Reformed Church that day, with the 

Rev. E. G. Sandersen of Indianapolis, then connected with the 

Anti-Saloon League of that city. J. F. Lehman, Fred Rohrer,

E. M. Ray and James Sprunger were also present at that meeting. The whole plan for starting and keeping up a remonstrance campaign was explained by Rev. Sandersen. 

After a long and hard battle by the temperance people of Berne the saloons were finally closed and on March 10, 1903 the Berne Witness said: "Berne is now dry."

Meanwhile, during all the time this battle was on against the open saloon, the temperance society carried on its work, holding its regular monthly meeting. Many important themes relating to temperance, total abstinence and other vital sub-

jects were discussed during the many years this organization presented its programs to the public. At the end of each year the Society had a special program, "Das Jahres Fest." For the occasion out-of-town speakers were very often engaged to deliver the annual temperance message. Also the treasurer and secretary gave their reports on the past year. 

Of special interest was the 25th anniversary meeting held on February 8, 1911. The speaker at this meeting was Dr. A. 

J. Bucher from Cincinnati, Ohio. His subject was "Christliche

Freiheit und Abstinenz." It was a powerful message. At the close of the meeting Dr. Bucher sang a solo "Der Beste Freund." 

The secretary's report showed that the society up to that time had held nearly two hundred fifty temperance meetings, more than five hundred messages had been delivered, and approximately five hundred special musical numbers rendered. 

The membership was now three hundred seventy-three, fortyfour having moved away. At this meeting the chairman, Rev. 

I. A. Sommer, presented to the audience a petition to the 

State Legislature of Indianapolis, which was in session at this time, petitioning them to amend the constitution with an amendment to prohibit in the state of Indiana the fabrication and sale of intoxicating liquors. The audience voted unanimously for this petition.

There is no doubt that the temperance sentiment of the people of Berne had a great influence in the State and the Nation in bringing about State and National prohibition. The work of the German temperance society of Berne was not without results. What was accomplished here gave the people in this State and other States courage to relentlessly bring war against the saloon and not to rest until it was banished from the country. This came to pass early in the year 1920. 

The liquor prohibition amendment was proposed to the legislature of the several States by the sixty-fifth Congress, December 18, 1917, and in January 29, 1919 the United States Secretary of State proclaimed its adoption by thirty-six states, and declared it in effect on January 16, 1920. So now we had national prohibition and the saloons had to cease.

For several years more, after State and Nation had gone dry, our temperance society carried on its work. Then at some meeting during the fall of 1925 the statement was made. by a certain member that the purpose for which the temperance society was founded was now nearly achieved and that the constitution as it was drawn up at that time was not in keeping with present day conditions. Therefore, the society might as well be dissolved or at least a change made in the constitution. The Executive Committee of the society thereupon appointed a special committee to revise the constitution and also to translate it from German into the English. But some of these members were at once of the opinion that the society had accomplished its purpose and were much in favor of disbanding. Therefore a joint meeting of the Executive

Committee and Special Committee was held on December 7, 1925, and after about two hours deliberation it was decided to call a special meeting of the society for December 16, 1925 and submit for its adoption or rejection the following resolution: "Since the purpose for which our society was founded, namely, to promote temperance, has now almost been achieved, we believe that our society might now disband." 

At this meeting after less than an hour's debate the society voted for its adoption.

One more meeting was to be held, the fortieth anniversary meeting. This took place on February 7, 1926. Professor

Quiring from Bluffton, Ohio was the speaker engaged for the occasion, and after him followed J. F. Lehman, chairman of the body of men who founded the society forty years before. 

He touched upon some important events in the temperance movement that transpired during the years of the organization's activities. It was an interesting talk. Two special numbers of music were given by male voices directed by D. C. 

Neuenschwander. According to the secretary's report read at this last meeting the society now had a membership of nearly six hundred. Over this period of years of activity the society held four hundred seventy-eight meetings, and over

one thousand messages on temperance and other important subjects were delivered.

Rev. S. F. Sprunger gave many messages on important themes relating to temperance and very ably discussed them. 

Also J. F. Lehman, I. A. Sommer, D. C. Sprunger and Fred 

Rohrer often spoke at the meetings, and many others from among its own members gave temperance talks. 

The record shows that during the forty years about one hundred members were lost through death and many of them moved away.

Of the charter members, the nine who organized this society February 4, 1886, four are still living, namely, Moses Moser, Levi A. Sprunger, C. C. Sprunger and 

N. G. Fankhauser.

After the fortieth anniversary the work of the society came to a close. National Prohibition was now in force and temperance agitation was not further needed. This was the consensus of opinion of the society members. But the laws prohibiting the sale of intoxicating liquor were in force for fourteen years only. National prohibition came to a close 

December 5, 1933. The Eighteenth Amendment was repealed by a majority of the states and old John Barleycorn was resurrected again and in his vacated grave was deposited the remnant of the Eighteenth Amendment, probably the most controversial statute in American history. Liquor could now be sold again though it was to be strictly regulated. In many places, however, the laws were not observed and people drank more than during the time of the open saloon before prohibition.

Soon after the repeal of the Eighteenth Amendment, the need was felt by several of our Mennonite brethren of organizing another temperance society and warning the people against the evil results of drinking as well as other dangers threatening especially the youth of today. Therefore the church board of the Mennonite church called a meeting for April 17, 1934 for the purpose of discussing the question whether or not a temperance organization should again be formed. At this meeting a short oral history of the old tem-

perance society and its purpose and work was given by J. P. 

Habegger. After deliberating on the question it was decided that a society should be organized. A committee was appointed which consisted of the following members: E. W. Baumgartner, Chairman; Gordon Liechty, Secretary; J. P. Habegger, O. F. 

Gilliom, Edison Sprunger, Howard Lehman, and Wilson Gilliom. 

The committee was to formulate plans for organization and to draw up a tentative constitution and present same at the next meeting which was to convene Tuesday evening, May 1st, 1934. At this meeting the constitution, after it had undergone several changes, was adopted by this assembly. A motion was then made and seconded that the acting committee serve until the close of the next meeting which was set for May 25th, 1934. Opportunity was given to those present to sign up as members of the society and many joined this new organization. Ballots were also prepared by the acting committee and distributed for permanent organization. The result of the first election was as follows: President, E. W. Baumgartner; 

Vice President, Edison Sprunger; Secretary, Gordon Liechty; 

Treasurer, Wilson Gilliom; Executive committee members, O. 

F. Gilliom, Frieda Lehman, Irene Sprunger.

The society was now ready to hold its regular monthly meetings. For the first three or four months these meetings were held the last week of the month during some week day evening. But this soon proved to be quite unsatisfactory due to the many conflicting activities of the community. The time was then changed by permission of the church board of deacons to the first Sunday evening of each month beginning at 7:45 o'clock after the Christian Endeavor meeting.

The membership of this society increased rapidly the first year, one hundred thirty-five joining the society. Since then it has nearly doubled. The record shows that two hundred sixtyone are enrolled at present. The notable increase the past year is largely due to the faithful efforts of the membership committee composed of James Habegger, chairman, Curtis 

Lehman, Eddyth Baumgartner, Betty Sprunger and Anna

Marie Amstutz. The present executive committee for the year July 1938 to June 1939 is composed of Carl Lehman, President; Donald Sprunger, Vice President; Martha Gilliom, Secretary; and Howard Gilliom, Treasurer. Committee members are Theodore Sprunger, James Habegger, and Anna Marie Amstutz. 

The programs given by the Christian Temperance Union have been interesting and instructive and a real spiritual atmosphere has prevailed throughout these meetings. 

As the work of the old German "Christian Temperance

Society" has been blessed by the Lord and the purpose accomplished for which the society had been founded, so may the 

Lord continue to bless the present "Christian Temperance

Union" of Berne, and may His benediction rest upon each member and may the good work prosper which this society is doing in His name and for His glory.

THE WOMEN'S MISSIONARY SOCIETY 

In I Samuel 7:12 we read-"Samuel took a stone, and set it up between Mizpeh and Shen, and called the name of it 

Ebenezer, saying, Hitherto the Lord hath helped us."

In looking back over the past fifty-one years truly the members of the Women's Missionary Society can also say- "Hitherto the Lord hath helped us."

It was on February 3, 1887, when at the suggestion of the late Rev. S. F. Sprunger, at that time pastor of the church, that a number of women met at one o'clock in the afternoon in the hall above the Sprunger & Lehman store. Rev. Sprunger presided at the first meeting. The meeting was opened by Scripture reading and prayer. Rev. Sprunger then gave a short address on missions and read a story showing how we who had been privileged to hear the gospel should out of gratitude put forth every effort to bring the gospel to those who had not heard it. 

At the first meeting it was decided to take as our motto 

I Peter 4:10-"As every man hath received the gift, even so minister the same one to another, as good stewards of the

manifold grace of God" and in Galatians 6:9-"And let us not be weary in well doing: for in due season we shall reap, if we faint not."

Fifty-one years ago when the society was organized it had thirty-three members, today five hundred two. Many a sister who helped in the organization and early years of this work has passed on to glory. 

Usually twelve meetings were held during the year, consisting of essays, talks, declamations, Bible verses, also biographies of great missionaries. 

For many years the programs were in German, now they are mostly in the English language. A word of commendation should be said here for our older sisters who do not understand the English language so well, nevertheless are faithful in their attendance and support, gladly giving in for the benefit of the younger sisters.

The first twenty or twenty-five years our farmers did not have tractors or automobiles and therefore often during the busy season found it difficult to take time out to drive to town with horse and buggy. It was therefore not unusual to find many of the sisters walking to town for the meetings. Special mention should be made of one sister, Mrs. Maria Habegger, who lived four miles away and often walked in for the service. 

Later Mrs. Habegger moved to town close to the church and is still a wonderful support in prayer as well as faithful attendance at the age of ninety years. Rev. S. F. Sprunger during his pastorate was a faithful attendant at the programs and often gave much encouragement to the society by his timely remarks. 

Another item of interest in the early days was the singing of Psalms. At different times the following rendered service by singing these Psalms-Elisabeth Zuercher, Elisabeth Welty,

Catharina Reusser, Anna Sprunger, Anna S. Liechty, Cath arina A. Sprunger, Elisabeth S. Lehman and Anna Nussbaum.

In the early days of the society much sewing and work was done and sent to India, China, Arizona and Oklahoma. Some

also was distributed to the city missions and among the needy in our community.

A few years ago the society added another committee, called the Relief Committee, to assist in the homes where death has entered.

A special need was felt by Mrs. C. H. van der Smissen that an organization should be effected for our young girls. She worked faithfully in this work and organized the Intermediate and Junior Mission Bands. 

We feel so privileged that the dear Lord can use even the weak vessels and that they may have a part in the great work for the Master. We have reasons to believe that much missionary interest and spirit has been awakened through the activity of the Women's Missionary Society. 

Our plea to the young people is expressed in the song we often sing:

It pays to serve Jesus, whatever betide, It pays to be true whate'er you may do; 'Tis riches of mercy in Him to abide, It pays to serve Jesus each day.

The Lord hath blessed in the past and we are confident He is able and willing to bless even more in the future.

THE CHORAL SOCIETY 

The birth of this organization may be traced to the time when several of the music lovers of this community instituted singing schools in different localities. As early as 1875, more than sixty years ago, practices were held in homes. Some of the music leaders who directed this singing were John Baumgartner, J. A. Sprunger, and David Scheidegger. A little later, Rev. S. F. Sprunger started the first community chorus. This chorus met in homes week after week rehearsing for the first Christmas program at the old church on the north side of the road, across from the present parsonage. 

Rev. Sprunger, who was an ardent lover of music, and who

appreciated the uplifting force it could be to the young people and the church, himself directed the choir for some time. 

However, when he discovered the musical ability of D. C. Neuenschwander, he encouraged him in the study of music and imparted to him all the knowledge he had received while studying at Wadsworth, Ohio. Then when Mr. Neuenschwander was only eighteen years of age, Rev. Sprunger passed to him the responsibility of conducting this small choir of sixteen voices.

When the first Mennonite conference was held in Berne, the choir practised long and hard and sang during the threeday session. The choir had by this time increased to twentyfour voices.

This community singing, we might say, was the beginning of the musical training of our community. 

It is most appropriate to interrupt this historical sketch at this time to pay tribute to David C. Neuenschwander, to whom is due more credit than to perhaps any other single person for the promoting of good music in the church and community.

Mr. Neuenschwander received his musical foundation in the District Number Nine School of Monroe Township. He was at this time fourteen years of age and already at this early age showed decided musical taste and ability. Due to circumstances, no formal musical training was offered him, but his ambition to know more about music spurred him on to make a study of it. Although self-taught, his skill as a musician soon became evident. He learned to play several instruments, and, possessing a beautiful, strong voice, he was recognized as the best tenor singer of the community. 

He owed much to Rev. Sprunger for the encouragement and assistance he received in his musical career. Rev. Sprunger in his directing continually placed the greatest importance on the emotional content of a composition. Unless the singers conveyed the message to the hearers, he considered the rendition a failure, no matter how beautiful the harmony or how accurate the rhythm might have been. Mr. Neuenschwander

was an apt pupil, and he held up this same high standard, besides exacting strict attention to matters of tempo, shading and attacks.

While still in his early twenties, Mr. Neuenschwander became the recognized choir leader of the community. He assisted other churches as well in starting choirs. For a period of about thirty years he unselfishly gave of his time and talents to build up the choir of his church. He directed the chorus without interruption during this time with the exception of a short time when Joel Welty, who also made a study of music and who had received some training, took charge during the absence of Mr. Neuenschwander when he spent some time in the West.

During the early years of its existence the choir sang from hymn books, Schmauck's Harmonie, and Zion's Sänger. 

The singing was without accompaniment and the pitch was obtained by the use of a tuning fork. 

The choir's first attempt to render an oratorio was in 1891, when they sang "Creation."

This was sung with both piano and reed organ accompaniment.

Frank Welty was at the piano, and Mary Lehman played the organ. A choir was solicited from house to house by signature to undertake this difficult proposition. J. F. Lehman was the general manager of the undertaking. A baby grand piano and also an organ were borrowed, as the church had not yet installed an instrument. 

Mary Lehman (Mrs. Lewis Reiff) 

First church organist

The next year the choir presented "The Messiah." These

. 

oratorios were rendered in the German language. Mrs. Linder, from Angola, sang the soprano solos, D. C. Neuenschwander, and J. F. Lehman the bass, and Joel Welty, the tenor solos. 

However, the oratorios proved too difficult for many to appreciate and understand, and the choir again took up more 

The first choir simple music in the form of short choruses and cantatas, some of which were Tempel Klänge and Geistliche Chöre. 

In the early years of 1900 many of the younger members of the choir voiced their desire to take up some cantatas and oratorios in the English language. Mr. Neuenschwander preferred not to undertake any conducting in this language and so the services of Guy Latchaw, then of Bluffton, Ohio, were procured to render "New Jerusalem." Later it was again presented and Mr. Davis, the composer, himself sang the bass solos. Two times it was sung with Eli Luginbill as director. 

Another undertaking was that of singing "The Holy City," and was in charge of Mark Evans, of Lima, Ohio. 

When the present church edifice was dedicated, in 1912, the choir again sang "New Jerusalem" and was directed by 

O. E. Richard, of Fort Wayne, Indiana.

By this time Mr. Neuenschwander had laid aside his baton

for regular choir conducting, as most of the singing was in the English language. However, he was still the director of the congregational singing of the church.

He was succeeded by M. M. Baumgartner, who served for many years. Other directors who followed are G. A. Lehman, C. O. Lehman, M. E. Stauffer, Harold Reusser, and Freeman

Burkhalter. 

Occasionally the services of outside talent were procured for choir conducting. Among them, beside those already mentioned, are Harry Kohler and Russel Lantz.

As accompanist, Mary Lehman, after serving many years, was succeeded by Rose Lehman. Later, when a pipe organ was installed, Miss Lehman played the organ and Miss Sybilla 

Sprunger was chosen pianist. Other organists and pianists who followed are: Mrs. Albert Winteregg, Edna Burkhalter, Evangeline Rohrer, Ruth Liechty, Elaine Baumgartner, and 

Mrs. Sherman Stucky.

Outside of home talent, the following have assisted at the piano and organ: Mrs. Pearl Mann, Mrs. Harry Kohler, Otto Holtkamp, and Clare L. Edwards.

This community has been blessed with good soloists, and although many singers of wide renown have appeared in the choir's presentations, often successful renditions were given using only home talent.

In the early years of its existence, the following served as soloists: Ida Lehman, Mary A. Sprunger, Edna Soldner, Helena 

Liechty, Sarah Ann Burkhalter, Henrietta Welty, Sarah Gilliom, N. G. Fankhauser, J. F. Lehman, Nathan Sprunger, C. E.

Stauffer, D. C. Neuenschwander and Joel Welty. 

These were followed by Kathryn Egly, Martha Baumgartner, Cordelia Riesen, Elfrieda Franz, Mrs. Herman Lehman, Mildred Habegger, Gladys Stauffer, G. A. Lehman, C. O. 

Lehman, M. E. Stauffer, M. M. Baumgartner, C. T. Habegger, Martin Baumgartner, Samuel Flueckiger, Wilbur Nussbaum and Freeman Burkhalter.

Cleve Briscoe Graa 

The Choral Society presenting Haydn's "Creation," May 8, 1938

Among those who have assisted as soloists are: Mrs. Anna 

Davis, Mrs. Guy Latchaw, Mrs. Florence Sprunger Starr, Mrs. 

Jewel M. Lovejoy, Janet Schumacher, Helen Ruth Baumgartner, Mrs. Virgil Moser, Guy Latchaw, Jesse Klopfenstein, Emil Taflinger, Gaius Baumgartner, F. Marion Cheney, and 

William D. Vennard.

It has been the established custom for the choir to rehearse on Tuesday evenings for a number of weeks before a presentation. However, in the last year this custom has been broken and Monday has been chosen instead. 

The oratorios and cantatas most often presented are "New 

Jerusalem," "The Holy City," "Hymn of Praise," "Creation" and "The Messiah."

The average membership of the choir for the last several years is one hundred twenty-five.

An outstanding accomplishment of the choir has been its rendition of "The Messiah" during December for the last three years. Russel A. Lantz, of Bluffton, Ohio, directed the choir on these occasions, and Mrs. Sherman Stucky served as pianist. 

The choir was accompanied by the Bluffton College Orchestra. 

This spring "Creation" was again beautifully presented by this choral society, conducted by Freeman Burkhalter. Mrs. 

Sherman Stucky accompanied at the piano and Clare L. Edwards of Fort Wayne, Indiana at the organ. Mr. Burkhalter, a graduate of Northwestern University, and now supervisor of music in the local schools, has been directing the choir for the last several years.

The history of the Choral Society would not be complete without a biographical sketch of D. C. Neuenschwander who was its director and inspiration for many years. But we wish to include it for another reason, for his contribution to congregational singing.

DAVID C. NEUENSCHWANDER 

David C., the son of Christian P. and Anna Lehman

Neuenschwander, was born in 

Adams County, Indiana, June 

2, 1857. He received his early training from his parents, Christian F. Stauffer, Hämi 

Pete (Peter A. Sprunger),

Abraham S. Lehman and S. F. 

Sprunger. 

Even as a small boy he was much interested in music and learned the minor and major keys from leaves off the forest trees while herding cows. He had an untiring desire to learn to read music. 

When he was fourteen years old he got a good musical foundation from Abraham S. 

Lehman, his school teacher,

David C. Neuenschwander who had studied in the Mennonite College in Wadsworth, Ohio. David also wanted to go to Wadsworth to school but his interest in music and not the ministry was the wrong subject as far as his parents were concerned. They had no objection to the school, but they were certain that the study of music alone would lead to dancing and general worldliness. 

So David had to content himself with what he could get at home. He also had some training under Hämi Hans (John A. 

Sprunger), another Wadsworth student. Hämi Hans could direct the singing but was not much of a singer himself, so when David was eighteen years old he was asked to become director. He then directed choirs for the Defenseless Mennonite Church as well as his own, and inspired people to sing and appreciate better music.

At the age of twenty-five, he was married to Katherine, daughter of Rev. Peter S. and Verena Sprunger Lehman. They were the parents of five children, Zilla, who died at the age of two, Wilbur, Harry, Milo and Olga (Mrs. Ernest Stengel).

For many years Mr. Neuenschwander, in partnership with 

Dan Shalley, operated the Berne Milling Company until it burned down in 1889 or 1890; then he purchased land just east of the corporation line and farmed this for twenty-five years. 

During all these years he kept up his enthusiasm in music, directing the singing for Sunday morning services as well as directing the choir when cantatas, oratorios or any music was to be rendered for special occasions. It was a great pleasure to him to have his son, Milo, accompany his baton by playing either the organ or piano. Mr. Neuenschwander directed the following oratorios and cantatas: "The Messiah," "Creation," "Hauschild's Männerchöre," "Das Lied von der Glocke," "Geistliche Chöre," "Schmauk's Harmonie," "Evangelische

Zionssänger," "Kinder-Harfe," "Zion's Weckstimmen," "Jugend-Harfe," "Sang und Klang," "Stadt Davids," "Nazareth" and "Jerusalem."

Both Mr. and Mrs. Neuenschwander were always interested in young people, their home was always known to be open to the public. They were also interested in unfortunate children who lost their mothers. They took them into their homes, fed and clothed them, bought books for them and sent them to school and helped them in other ways. They also donated liberally when people were seeking funds for a worthy cause.

Mr. and Mrs. Neuenschwander moved to town in November, 1915 and lived in the first house west of the Mennonite parsonage until their death which occurred within a week of each other, in 1935. Mr. Neuenschwander passed away January 5 and his wife, January 12.

THE CHRISTIAN ENDEAVOR SOCIETY 

Motto chosen in 1894: "Whatsoever ye do in word or deed,

do all in the name of the Lord Jesus, giving thanks to God and the Father by him." Col. 3:17. "Our beloved pastor, S. F. Sprunger, for some time had a desire that the young people should take more active part in the spiritual work of the church, especially in the prayer meetings. The same desire became more and more prevalent among the members, until finally a society was formed. "The pastor called all the standing committees of the church to meet on February 8th, 1894 to consider the matter. 

These appointed seven persons with special instruction to deliberate on the possibility of founding a society. They met on the evening of February 14, decided in favor of such a society, and formulated its purposes. "The matter was brought before the young people of the church on February 19. That evening the plan found accord and a committee of seven again were appointed to draw up a constitution. This committee was composed of the following: 

S. F. Sprunger, Chairman; I. A. Sommer, Eli Luginbill, William

Sprunger, N. G. Fankhauser, Judith Liechty, and Lydia Lehman. "A fitting constitution was drawn up and presented at a meeting March 14. Thirty-one persons signed and the society was temporarily established. It was decided that the first meeting of the new organization was to be held on the first 

Sunday evening of April, 1894. The following officers were chosen for a temporary term of three months, at the expiration of which the society was to be permanently established: I. A. 

Sommer, chairman; A. A. Lehman, assistant chairman; N. G. 

Fankhauser, secretary, and E. A. Luginbill, treasurer. "These officers then appointed the following committees: 

S. F. Sprunger

Program 

Barbara Sprunger Membership 

Andrew Burry 

Peter A. Habegger 

Nathan Sprunger 

William J. Sprunger 

Lydia Lehman

Christine Steiner

Relief 

Noah Wulliman

Joel Kirchhofer 

Emil Flueckiger 

Maria A. Sprunger 

Ella Neuenschwander "So the first Sunday evening in April, 1894, the Society started its meetings. These were well attended. The Society began its activities with fifty-two members and within three months the membership increased to ninety active members. "The Society chose Colossians 3:17 as their motto, 'And whatsoever ye do in word or deed, do all in the name of the 

Lord Jesus, giving thanks to God and the Father by him.'

The purpose of the Society was to encourage and establish an earnest Christian life among the members who should be active in the work of the church, and endeavor to win souls for the Lord and church." -A translation from the first records written by N. G. 

Fankhauser and I. A. Sommer. 

The new organization progressed and grew rapidly. It filled a definite need for a definite work by the young people of the church. For the first time these young people were able to take an active part in the work of the church. They used and are continuing to use this opportunity to good advantage. 

The Society has grown from the fifty-two members at its inception, to three hundred seventy active and two hundred thirty honorary members.

An excerpt from a quarterly report by I. A. Sommer in 1894 reads as follows: "In this quarter the work in general went on quietly without any outstanding encouragements or discouragements. 

Many members were inspired through the prayer meetings to search the Scriptures more diligently and systematically go to God in prayer with those things they have at heart. "Voluntary offerings came in well. At the close of the year there is a neat sum ready to be used for a good purpose. "Our hearts are filled with praise to the giver of all good gifts for the willingness to work to which the officers, commit-

tee members, and the members of the society gave evidence." 

For many years Bible Study Classes were conducted by the Christian Endeavor. Missionary books and biographies were studied. Books on personal work and Sunday School helps also were included and proved beneficial to the different departments of the church. 

Missionary work has been one of the greatest interests of this organization. One year a program was given and an offering taken for the erection of a new mission station in India. Another year funds were raised for the erection of a station in China. A mission drive was conducted for raising money for a boys' school in Taming Fu, China, and for medical work in India. From time to time money has been sent to home and foreign missions, and for the help of needy Russians. 

The Society co-operates with the Reformed church and other churches in Berne for the annual Easter sunrise prayer meeting.

Young People's meetings have been sponsored the past four years with such noted speakers as Dr. Walter Wilson, Rev. Isaac Page, Dr. M. R. DeHaan, and Rev. Vance Havner.

Many have been led to Christ and to a closer walk with Him. through these services.

Besides the development of individual Christian life and leadership the Society has been responsible for much work of a very practical nature. Throughout the years the members of the relief committees have given faithful and most unselfish service. Their work has been a great comfort and help during sickness. They have devoted much time to singing, reading and praying with the sick, and watching through long hours of the night at their bedside. At time of death their thoughtfulness and help were a great comfort to the bereaved.

The good literature committee has furnished thousands of tracts through the different tract racks in the church. Books have been furnished for the book table and magazines made available.

Complete records of the different presidents of the organization are not available. Those on record are the following:

I. A. Sommer, J. F. Lehman, E. A. Luginbill, William J.

Sprunger, Amos Reusser, D. J. Sprunger, D. C. Welty, A. N. 

Sprunger, A. A. Lehman, Menno Burkhalter, Jonas Sprunger,

M. A. Neuenschwander, M. E. Stauffer, Helena Liechty, Alfred 

Sprunger, Albert Neuhauser, Waldo Lehman, E. W. Baumgartner, Inda Sprunger, Palmer Moser, Howard Liechty, Howard Lehman, Edison Sprunger, Clarence Liechty, Wilbur 

Nussbaum, D. O. Liechty, Gordon Liechty, Ralph Amstutz, Gerald Stucky, and Carl M. Lehman.

As we look over the past and then onward into the future, we may well consider the words of the late J. F. Lehman in an early report, "If this evening we have the conviction that in the past we have not done what we could have, then let us with new fervor and zeal meet our responsibility." 

THE MEN'S CHORUS 

The nucleus which developed into the "Deutscher Mennontischer Männer Chor," by which name the chorus was known for many years, was formed when four young men met regularly as a quartette to sing sacred songs. 

Soon this group was increased to eight men. In September, 1895, these men with several others began rehearsing from "Gebhart's Maenner Perlenchoere." These hymns were rendered at different occasions or meetings under the leadership of Eli A. Luginbill. 

On April 8, 1898, a number of young men met at "Arena Hall" located in the Public School Building in Berne to create a permanent organization. At this meeting E. A. Luginbill was elected as chairman and D. J. Sprunger as song director. 

These were to serve until July 1, 1898. Since no secretary had been elected, no minutes were recorded. From the date of the organization to January 1, 1899, rehearsals were held about once every week.

At a business meeting on June 24, 1898, conducted in the tailor's room of the Sprunger, Lehman and Company Store,

D. J. Sprunger was elected chairman, Amos Burkhalter, secretary, and E. A. Luginbill, song director.

At the semi-annual business meeting on January 4, 1899, the following were appointed to draw up a constitution for the chorus: Menno Burkhalter, E. A. Luginbill, D. J. Sprunger, Albert N. Sprunger, and Amos Burkhalter. 

This constitution was submitted and adopted at a meeting on January 17, 1899. Two new officers were provided for, namely vice-president and treasurer. An election was held at once, and D. J. Sprunger was elected vice-president and 

Noah Fox, treasurer.

The constitution was later revised and translated into the English language. The same constitution, with several amendments added, is still in force at this writing. 

The purpose of organizing this chorus, according to Article II of the constitution, was to "give an opportunity to develop and use the talent of singing entrusted to us by God, to His Honor."

The charter members that were instrumental in organizing the chorus were: Amos Burkhalter, Menno Burkhalter, Noah Fox, Edwin H. Gilliom, Simon F. Lehman, Eli A. Lugin bill, Albert Neuenschwander, Menno A. Neuenschwander, H. 

P. Ray, Albert N. Sprunger, Ben Sprunger, D. J. Sprunger, Nathan Sprunger, Reuben Sprunger, William J. Sprunger, C.

E. Stauffer, and D. C. Welty. 

In the early years of this organization rehearsals were held practically once every week. They met in the homes of different members, and quite frequently in the home of 

Isaac Lehman. The first report of Treasurer Noah Fox shows that he paid to Isaac Lehman the sum of fifty cents for a cord of wood. This was the only item of expense listed, and was for heating the room where the choir held many of their rehearsals. Later most of the practicing was done in the church. 

For several years the chorus has taken vacations for two or three months of the summer.

During the forty years that the chorus has been singing, the following have served as directors: E. A. Luginbill, D. J.

Sprunger, M. M. Baumgartner, M. E. Stauffer, and Freeman

Burkhalter. 

The accompanists that have assisted during this time were: 

Mrs. Mary Lehman Reiff, Mrs. Rose M. Lehman Baumgartner, Mrs. Rebecca Reusser Stucky, and Mrs. Elaine Baumgartner Lantz.

According to the records that have been kept, the chorus has up to this time rendered more than one thousand three. hundred hymns and special numbers of music. One hundred and six sacred concerts have been presented. These took place mostly in towns and cities in a radius of about one

For some time the chorus in giving concerts away from home would divide the offering received with those who spon-

sored the program on a fifty-fifty basis. In the later years the whole offering has been received. Practically all these offerings have been given for missionary purposes. A total of over three thousand dollars has thus been given to the cause of missions. In most cases the members of the chorus have paid their own transportation expenses. 

One of the outstanding events in the history of the choir was the Twenty-fifth anniversary and Home Coming, held on September 24, 1920, at Lehman's Grove, now known as Lehman Park. Invitations to attend this celebration were extended to all members and former members with their families. One hundred sixty-three were present. In the forenoon an interesting program was rendered, which included a song by all old and present members. Missionary Alfred 

Habegger, a former member of the chorus, delivered a timely address. After the basket dinner a picture was taken of the group. In the afternoon an informal meeting was held at which time the former members related the many blessings that had come to them while they were members of the chorus. 

Many old favorite hymns were sung. On the following Sunday evening, on September 26, at the home church, a combined choir of the present and former members rendered one of the most inspiring programs this organization ever presented.

The experiences, joys and blessings which have come to those who had the privilege to sing with this organization are many. The deep spiritual blessings received will live on and on.

There could be no more fitting close for this short history than the last stanza of the hymn, "Cast Thy Bread 

Upon the Waters," which has long been a favorite song, especially with the older members of the choir. "Cast thy bread upon the waters, Waft it on with praying breath, In some distant doubtful moment, It may save a soul from death; 

When you sleep in solemn silence, 'Neath the morn and evening dew, Stranger hands, which you have strengthened, May strew lilies over you."

THE LADIES' CHORUS The Mennonite Ladies' Choir was organized in December 

1912, with eighteen members, by Miss Helena Liechty. The purpose of the organization is to sing the gospel and thereby witness for the Lord. Eligible for membership in this organization are young women who have a voice to sing and whose daily walk is in accordance with their profession. 

The Lord wonderfully blessed this organization and soon calls came from many different places to give song services and assist in gospel services. Among the outstanding ones which proved more than usual in blessings to the members of the choir was a five-day service at Dayton, Ohio, under the auspices of Dr. Clippinger's church, and the Rescue Mission,

Milwaukee, Wisconsin, Mr. Silliway, Superintendent. While en route to Milwaukee the choir members were the guests of the Moody Bible Institute and after lunch rendered a short program at the Moody Memorial Church. Two trips were also made to Detroit, Michigan, and many other places closer home too numerous to mention. Many a member in the early days rendered exceptional service, leaving fond memories on the members and the directress. Among them we should like to mention Mrs. Ben Sprunger, now in glory, who was much appreciated for her voice support as well as prayer support. 

The members of the first choir were: 

Martha Burkhalter 

Kathryn Egly

Gertrude Lehman 

Mollie Lehman

Mrs. Ben Sprunger

Lehman, and Lydia Neuenschwander. 

The Ladies' Chorus (1913) 

First row: Rosa M. Lehman, Louise Habegger, Meta Mabegger, Hulda Gilliom, Kathryn

Mrs. M. M. Baumgartner (Rose Lehman) was pianist from the time of organization until 1920 and since that time Ruth Liechty has served. Helena Liechty served the choir as directress until 1923 when ill health no longer permitted the work. 

Her wholehearted services will never be forgotten. 

Mrs. Arthur Sprunger (Cordelia Riesen) filled the vacancy for the next two years. The choir continued in furnishing special music at various Sunday School and Christian Endeavor conventions, also giving entire concerts at many of the surrounding towns such as Portland, Bluffton, Decatur and Linn 

Grove. After the resignation of Cordelia Riesen Sprunger in 

1925, Menno Stauffer was elected to serve as director and he remained in the service until 1931. The choir continued to be richly blessed and sang at many of the church services here as well as in the surrounding community. On several occasions they sang with the Men's Chorus. Quartettes or groups from the choir rendered special services when it was impossible for the entire choir to take part. The homes of the sick were often visited and several times the choir caroled at Christmas time. A concert at the Irene Byron Sanatorium near Fort Wayne was a blessing both for those who heard and those who sang.

The choir has been a continued inspiration and help under the capable direction of Mrs. C. T. Habegger who has been director since 1932. Several programs by the combined Ladies' and Men's Choir have been given. The time of practicing which up to this time had been on Sunday afternoon, was changed to Wednesday night before Prayer Meeting. For several years the choir gave joint concerts with the Missionary Church Choir of Fort Wayne, also broadcasting from station WOWO over the Missionary Hour. As in the preceding years the choir was always glad to render services when called upon by other churches, such as Plymouth Congregational Church of Fort Wayne and the Grace Church in Pandora, Ohio.

In 1935 Mrs. Ralph Blosser (Elizabeth Habegger) substituted during the absence of Mrs. C. T. Habegger for about

half a term. Some memorable occasions during this year were the joint concerts with the Men's Chorus at the Shrine Auditorium at Fort Wayne for a Methodist Conference and also at the opening services of the Portland Centennial. The choir members decided on getting uniform gowns, adding much to the appearance of the group. Most of the members take part in the "Messiah," which has been presented for the past three years. The combined Ladies' and Men's Choir presented 

Stainer's "The Crucifixion" last year.

The choir usually disbands during the summer months, for a short vacation. This last term was cut rather short because all efforts were put forth on the rendition of Hadyn's "Creation" and so our practices were discontinued at an earlier date. THE INTERMEDIATE MISSION BAND 

In the year 1911 Rev. and Mrs. C. H. van der Smissen and family came to Berne to make this their future home. 

Mrs. van der Smissen was much interested in Missions. 

She had been in charge of a girls' sewing society in Summerfield, Illinois, before coming to Berne. She began the work of Mission Bands here by holding meetings at her home with her two small daughters and a few of their friends. This met with such success that the society grew too large for one person to supervise, so a few ladies were asked to assist her. 

The meetings began with devotions, then an hour was spent in sewing for Missions, and closed with a few songs. 

In 1918 Mrs. D. C. Sprunger was asked to help. She had many years before been in charge of a similar society in Wayne County, Ohio. She served faithfully for eleven years, remaining with the Junior Mission Band. Shortly after Mrs. Sprunger 

The Intermediate Mission Band began her work, Mrs. van der Smissen was forced, because of ill health, to give up the work and turned it over to her daughter Frieda who served a few years.

Sometime during this period it was decided to divide the

group into two divisions, the smaller ones to be the Junior Mission Band, and the girls from thirteen to eighteen forming the Intermediate Mission Band. 

In 1922 the societies had grown too large for the home, and began holding their meetings the first Saturday afternoon of each month in the church basement. 

Since that time many different leaders and committees have served, and a large number of girls have been blessed by having attended these meetings. In the last few years some of the committee members were former Intermediate 

Mission Band girls.

The aim of our Band is to acquaint the girls with the work of Missions and arouse interest in the cause of Missions, and to give of their time and money for Missions. 

Offerings are held at each meeting. Different methods have been used for our meetings. A short program, prepared by a committee of four girls, is given, either before or after the sewing hour. The average program consists of devotionals, special Music, a missionary story or contest, and as a closing number the girls sing a number of choruses. 

Contests in which the girls learn the names and places of our General Conference Missionaries have proved of great value.

During the winter some of the meetings were held in the homes of the girls or of the leaders. These meetings proved very popular, in that it gave more time for working and longer programs, also affording time for social fellowship and getting acquainted.

At present we have a membership of about forty girls. 

The girls have made quilt blocks, quilts, scrapbooks, towels, pillow cases, draw sheets, diapers, and torn and rolled bandages and made many other articles which were sent to our mission stations.

The quarter project proved very successful. 

Each girl was given a quarter donated by a mission friend. At the end of six months, the five dollars had multiplied to almost forty

dollars. Twenty-five dollars of this was sent to China to give hospital care to an orphan baby for one year. 

Eleven regular meetings are held each year. In the fall a Sunday evening program is given by the girls at which time an offering is taken. These offerings, as well as the monthly offerings, help to carry on the missionary cause. 

An orphan in India has been supported for many years. 

The one supported at this time is a little boy, the others having been girls.

Other projects in which the girls have helped are as follows: hospital instruments for China and India, building funds, war and flood relief for China, bicycles for China, support of 

Bible women, digging of well for Busby, Montana, gifts to missionaries, Dr. Dester's bungalow, and every year a certain amount is sent to the General Conference Home and Foreign 

Mission Boards. 

The Lord has richly blessed this Mission Band in the many years past and our prayer and aim is to serve our Master more and that all that is being done might be to His honor and glory. 

THE JUNIOR MISSION BAND 

In December 1911 Rev. C. H. A. van der Smissen and his family moved to Berne, Indiana from Summerfield, Illinois.

At that place Mrs. van der Smissen was the leader of a girls' 

Missionary Society. There was no such society in Berne, so she soon began with her own two youngest daughters, Edna and Ruth. She taught them to sew and knit for missions on 

Saturday afternoons and then told them mission stories. The meeting was closed with singing of missionary songs, a short Scripture reading and prayer by the leader. The girls enjoyed this very much and told other girls. Soon a few neighbor girls came and Mrs. van der Smissen asked Mrs. Edwin Lehman and a little later Mrs. Otto Neuenschwander to help her. 

Because of failing health Mrs. van der Smissen's daughter 

Frieda soon had to take her place as a leader, which position she held for two years.

In 1918 Mrs. D. C. Sprunger became one of the helpers and for eleven years she was a most devoted leader. So many 

The Junior Mission Band girls came and they were so different in age that it became necessary to divide the group. The younger girls were called the Junior Mission Band and the older ones the Intermediate Mission Band. Both bands were put under the supervision of the Sewing Society and in 1922 began to hold their meetings in the basement of the church where they still meet. 

The aim is: 

To create interest in Missions 

To raise money for Missions 

To work for Missions

To Pray for Missions The motto is: 

"For we are labourers together with God.-"I Corinthians 3, part of verse 9.

The Junior Mission band has paid for the support of two orphan girls through the mission school in India and is supporting a third, the little girl of a native pastor. Besides that, several hundred dollars have been sent to missions and much work such as quilts, comforters, dolls, doll blankets, towels, pillow slips, quilt blocks, scrapbooks, calendars, and pictures have been distributed. .

Some of the projects to which the Junior Mission Band

has contributed are: Russian Refugees in Canada; Indian orphan boy, Carl Johnson; a sterilizer for the hospital at Kai 

Chow, China; auto for Dr. Dester; Concordia Hospital, Canada; electric wiring, Busby, Montana; Detroit Rescue Mission; sterilizer, Basna hospital, India; printing of Cheyenne New Testaments; literature for camp visiting for Rev. Rodolphe Petter; stereopticon, Oraibi, Arizona; books for Mary Schirmer's library, Hotevilla, Arizona; funds for destitute patients in Kai 

Chow, Champa and Basna hospitals; portable phonograph for 

Martha Burkhalter; flood relief for China and India; repairs of buildings, hospital beds; Dr. Dester's bungalow; part support of a Chinese evangelist; traveling expenses for missionaries; bandages for the Champa and Basna hospitals; other medical equipment for China and India hospitals; the yearly Christmas fund and toys, etc., to our stations among the American Indians and Sunday School literature for Fred Johnson, Tuba City, Arizona.

Since 1935 systematic studies on the missionary work of the General Conference have been conducted and once a year a public program is rendered at the church. 

As far as can be learned no records are available of the work done or the money spent before the year 1923. Since then, until June 30, 1938, $1839.52 have been spent by this organization of little girls from six to thirteen years of age for the different mission projects mentioned above. 

THE INTERMEDIATE CHRISTIAN ENDEAVOR SOCIETY 

In January 1913 at the annual business meeting of the Senior Christian Endeavor Society, the question was brought before the meeting as to the advisability of organizing an Intermediate Christian Endeavor Society. After the matter was discussed a committee was appointed to work out a constitution. This committee was composed of Rev. P. R. Schroeder and O. F. Gilliom.

A constitution was formed and the age limit was set for the ages thirteen to seventeen. The meetings were held on

Sunday afternoons. The first meeting was called and eightyfive members signed for active membership and twenty-two for associate. It was soon learned that some changes would be necessary if the society should have a healthy growth. One of the changes was to set the age limit from thirteen to nineteen and the other to have the time for the meeting changed to Sunday evening in the basement of the church at the same time the senior society met in the church auditorium. These two changes proved very helpful to the society. 

Rev. Schroeder was the first suprintendent; however, due to heavy work in the church, he did not serve after 1913, and Menno Burkhalter served until 1917 when C. H. Sprunger was appointed. He served one year; then in 1918 Christine Habegger was appointed. She served three months. In April 1918 

Helena Liechty was appointed and she is still giving her time and untiring services in the office of superintendent. During 

1923 Frieda Lehman served for several months during Miss 

Liechty's illness. 

Missionary enterprises and missionaries were supported all these years. A Hindi girl, Grace Logan, was supported entirely by the society until she was out of school. 

Upon looking over the list of the first members we find that several have served as missionaries who did service in home and foreign fields. Howard Liechty, a charter member, has served seven years abroad under the Presbyterian Board. 

Helen Nickel, also one of the early members, is serving her second term in India. Twenty-eight of the original members have moved away, with a faithful remnant still working in the home church keeping the home fires burning. 

Hundreds have passed through this society and have received untold blessings from it. Many have since received training in Christian work.

The society today has an enrollment of two hundred and is enjoying a steady growth.

THE SUMMER BIBLE SCHOOL 

The Summer Bible School has been of greatest importance to the spiritual development and progress of the church. The first session was held in the summer of 1921. After conducting the Summer Bible School four years as a Mennonite school, the four churches of Berne decided to unite and make it a 

Union Summer Bible School. The Public School authorities have always been very obliging in permitting the use of the schoolhouse with all the necessary equipment free of any charges.

Affiliation with the Summer Bible School Association with headquarters at Chester, Pennsylvania, was effected and their prescribed course for a five-week term was adopted. "The Summer Bible School is a school for the definite, purposeful, practical study of the Bible, and is planned so as to systematically focus the Scriptures upon the Lord Jesus, whom they are intended to reveal. "It takes for granted that the most necessary and at the same time the most interesting book in the world is the Bible. "Therefore, there is no need of handcraft to stimulate interest-none is used, map-drawing excepted. "The course of study is comprehensive enough to create a soul thirst for the Word and to lay the basis for its intelligent and profitable study throughout life."

This course provides for ten grades including the Kindergarten. Pupils range from five to fifteen years. A staff of fourteen paid workers is employed. The average cost per pupil is now approximately $1.50.

There are others who contribute to the success of the school who receive no remuneration. Among them are the volunteer drivers of cars, who convey the children living in the country to and from the school each day. 

A commencement program is given each year at the Mennonite church to bring the school session to a fitting close. 

Students completing the ninth grade are awarded diplomas. 

On the day after commencement a picnic is held which is

looked upon as an opportunity to foster friendly feeling and Christian fellowship in the community. 

The school is enjoying the whole-hearted cooperation of the surrounding churches and of the community in general. 

The average attendance for the past ten years has been three hundred sixty-three pupils. The average attendance from the Mennonite church for the same period has been two hundred twenty-four.

THE MID-WEEK COMMITTEE 

The Mid-Week Committee had its beginning in 1927, and followed in the wake of the Rader Evangelistic Meetings. A large number of people, and especially young people, were converted during these meetings. Others re-dedicated or re-consecrated themselves to the Lord Jesus Christ. It then seemed both wise and necessary to provide some way whereby these converts and others might be helped in their spiritual growth and development. Consequently a committee was appointed, answering to the name of The Mid-Week Committee, to work out plans for this purpose. This committee, as have been all Mid-Week Committees hereto, was appointed by the Church Board and comprised the following members: 

Rev P. R. Schroeder-Chairman 

Freeman Burkhalter 

Sybilla G. Sprunger-Secre- Helena Liechty tary-Treasurer 

Inez Luginbill 

Jerry Liechty 

Rachel Burkhalter 

M. E. Stauffer

Before outlining any method or plans the members of this committee engaged in an earnest season of prayer.

Knowing that a knowledge of God's Word is vital and essential to the Christian's growth in grace, the committee felt that the best possible help that could be offered for that purpose was a course in Bible study.

After careful consideration as to the time for such a Bible

study it seemed most advisable to have it on a Wednesday evening, and to make it a part of the regular Mid-Week Prayer Meeting. Therefore the length of time for this meeting was extended one-half hour, making the service one and onehalf hour in length, with divisions into three periods. The following year it was considered best for various reasons to shorten the Mid-Week service 15 minutes. The following schedule was then arranged into three periods, which is still in effect at the present time: 

7:30-7:55 Inspirational service 

7:55-8:10 Prayer in groups 

8:10-8:45 Bible study in groups. 

Also, the first committee unanimously decided on a uniform course of Bible study. This was for two reasons. First, it was believed that there would be strength and power in having the entire congregation pursuing the same course of study. Secondly, they foresaw that if such study should be continued in the future the matter of arranging courses would thereby be less complicated. 

The first course chosen was a series of topical Bible studies, issued by The Union Bible Study Association of Dallas, Texas, which consisted of ten lessons.

In order to enable larger participation in study and discussion it was thought best to arrange for several classes in groups somewhat according to ages. Hence the following divisions were made: 

A German class for men and women 

A class for men above twenty years of age

A class for women above twenty years of age 

A class for boys below twenty years of age 

A class for girls below twenty years of age.

All the classes with the exception of the boys' and girls' classes were put in charge of a committee whose duty it was to provide for leaders for their class and make any division necessary according to the number enrolled in their group.

For the first course of study the following committees and leaders were appointed:

German Class: 

Rev. Joel Lehman 

J. F. Sprunger

Daniel Stucky Men's Class-ages above twenty: 

Ben Sprunger

Eli A. Luginbill 

Howard Lehman

Women's Class-ages above twenty: 

Mrs. P. R. Schroeder

Mrs. Menno Burkhalter 

Martha Depp 

Leader of Boys' Class-ages below twenty: 

Rev. P. R. Schroeder 

Leader of Girls' Class-ages below twenty: 

Sybilla G. Sprunger.

Owing to heavy enrollment in several classes and for making more suitable adjustments according to age, and for other reasons, these study groups have been changed and new divisions made at various times since then. At this writing (June 1938) there are eleven classes arranged in groups as follows: 

Children's Class-two groups, ages below thirteen

Girls' Class Young Women's Class Women's Class Small Boys' Class Boys' Class Young Men's Class Men's Class 

Young Married People's Class German Class-a mixed class. 

Mention should be made that also the Teacher's Training Class, since January 1931, is conducted during the regular

period of the Mid-Week service This class under the instruction of Edison Sprunger pursues courses of study providing training for Sunday School teachers and others who are interested in Christian work. 

The Mid-Week Committee in office during 1929 added another worth while feature to the Mid-Week service, namely the appointment of a Music Committee. This consisted of two members, Inez Luginbill, now Mrs. Milo Habegger, and Sybilla 

G. Sprunger. In the same year membership in this committee was increased to five. The duty of the Music Committee is to provide for one special number of music at each Mid-week service. At various times certain classes were made responsible for providing the special music. Besides adding interest and inspiration to the service itself, these special numbers of music give to the young people opportunity in employing their musical talents to honor God.

Another valuable undertaking at various times by Mid- Week Committees was that of securing speakers for Bible lectures. Obviously the purpose involved was to create greater interest in Bible study, to help gain a better knowledge and understanding of the Bible, plus the added stimulus to spiritual growth among Christians. 

In 1927 Dr. P. B. Fitzwater, Chicago, was secured who gave several messages on I Corinthians. 

Rev. John Mitchell, in 1930, gave a series of studies in 

Romans lasting several weeks. Other speakers secured under the auspices of Mid-Week Committees for mass Mid-Week services include, Rev. Luke Rader and Mr. Harold Mustaine; 

Rev. George W. Soerhide, City Mission Worker, Cleveland, Ohio; Rev. J. J. Esau, Lima, Ohio; Rev. D. C. Stucky, Detroit, Michigan, and the Spiritual Jubilee Singers of Chicago.

Since 1927 all books of the New Testament have been studied at least once, during the Mid-Week services, except Mark, Luke, I, II and III John. Several books have been studied twice. Ephesians has been covered three times. Of the Old Testament, ten books have been studied which include

the following: the Pentateuch, Joshua, Judges, Ruth, I Samuel and Daniel.

The book being studied at this writing is the Gospel according to St. John.

The "Golden Year" in attendance at the Mid-Week services, since 1927, was during 1931, when the average attendance totalled four hundred fifty-three. During 1928 the average attendance totalled four hundred, and in 1937 it was two hundred fifty-three.

While it is gratifying and causes much joy to see many young people come out to these Mid-Week Bible Classes, we hope that in the future many more will come, including both young and older people.

While preparing this historical survey and looking over the yearly attendance records we note with regret that there are occasional slumps in the attendance at these Mid-Week services. It is our sincere wish and prayer that these slumps may be eliminated in the years to come.

The Scripture motto for the Mid-Week Organzation is this: "Search the scriptures; for in them ye think ye have eternal life; and they are they which testify of me." John 5:39.

THE YOUNG MEN'S CHORUS 

Several times prior to 1934, a young men's chorus had been formed. With good intentions the group would organize and function for a while, then because of lack of interest would disband. Much blessing and benefit was derived through the organization both by chorus and audience. 

After there had been no such organization for a few years, the need for such a group again presented itself. Through the interest of some in the former chorus and others, a meeting was called for March 11, 1934. At this gathering the matter of again having a young men's chorus was presented. There being much favorable comment, a new organization was

Frances Burkhalter. Both are still serving the group faithfully. Twenty-three were present at the first practice. There are at present thirty-two members.

This new organization enjoyed God's richest blessing upon it for nearly two years and then suffered the same setback that the former choruses had experienced: spiritual decline and lack of interest. On the verge of disbanding, the young men, through prayer and the able leadership of the president, then Gerald Stucky, were kept together. 

Many have been the places where the group has gone to sing the gospel message, sometimes in neglected places and to small audiences. All that has been accomplished was through the help and strength of Him who loved us and gave Himself for us, our Saviour Jesus, to Him belongs all honor and glory.
