181 

DAVID BAUMGARTNER 

The subject of this sketch was the only child of Deacon 

David Baumgartner by his first wife, Katharina Imhof. According to the Civil Record of Langnau, Canton Bern, Switzerland, H. 5, page 144, he was born January 25, 1765, in Buederichgraben, Jura, Oberamt Courtelary, Canton Bern, Switzerland, and, according to a letter headed "Adams County, Indiana, January 25, 1853," which he addressed to his son David in Wayne County, Ohio, he begins by saying, "Today I begin my 89th year," it will be seen that eighty-eight years back brings the time to the cited date of his birth. Hence the date of his birth on his tomstone, January 15, 1763, and as recorded on the family tree, is evidently incorrect.

David's mother died when he was yet a small child. His father again married and with his second wife he had eight children.

At the above named place he grew to manhood. After his father moved to Langnau in 1791, he presumably inherited, or bought the old homestead, where he lived for a long time, and where all his children were born. The old house he lived in burned down after he sold the homestead. From here he moved to Langnau, in the Emmenthal of Basel, time not given. 

From Langnau he moved to La-Chauxde Fonds, and from there he, wife and children by his third wife emigrated in 1837 to 

America, Wayne County, Ohio, where his oldest son David already lived. Land already being quite expensive in Ohio, he concluded to move to Indiana, where land was yet much cheaper. In August 1839 he therefore moved with his younger children to Wells County, Indiana, as he states in a letter to his friends in the Fatherland.

Later he moved in with his youngest son Samuel (the writer's father), who lived in French township, Adams County, Indiana, two miles southeast of Vera Cruz. Here he died of old age, November 4, 1853. He was married three times and was the father of eleven children.

Mr. Baumgartner was a minister of the gospel in the Men-

nonite Church for about sixty-five years, having been made a preacher according to the Mennonite custom, at the age of twenty-three. He also preached in Ohio while he lived there, and was the first Mennonite preacher in Adams and Wells Counties in Indiana. He was a strong advocate of the Mennonite doctrines and had the confidence of his church. The 

Mennonites at this time had no paid ministry, hence he was necessitated to engage in secular work to make a livelihood for himself and his family. He was a book-binder by trade. 

He was described to the writer as a tall man somewhat slender and heavy boned. Samuel, his youngest son, resembled him more than any one of the other children. 

He possessed a very amiable and unselfish disposition. He was always courteous to ministers of other denominations. His convictions were clear, and his Christian characteristics strong. 

He had a hatred for all shams or pretensions in Church or State. He loved peace, disliked pride and loose morals, idleness, dishonesty and a cold and formal religion. He was tender-hearted and kind in his family, respectful to his superiors and inferiors, and was a lover of religious conversations. 

Below is part of a personal letter written to his friends in the Fatherland, when he was eighty-four years old, translated by Rev. Samuel H. Baumgartner, his grandson, who received it from his father Samuel Baumgartner, Sr., after his death. The Rev. David Baumgartner lived with Samuel Sr. at the time the letter was written. "I wish you all, with my whole heart, the grace and peace from God, and knowledge, light and life in Christ, and the sanctification of the Spirit, through Jesus Christ, Amen! "I have already waited a long time for a letter from you, but so far have not received one. I would have written before this, but since I have received no reply to my last letter, as 

I hopefully expected, I do not know whether I have offended you in anywise, which I did not mean to do. I wrote with upright sincerity. But since an opportunity presents itself to me, I concluded to write again. There is a dear friend here,

BIOGRAPHIES 

183 named Matthias Miller, from Canton Basel, who says that it is his intention to visit his Fatherland once more, and said if we have a letter to send along with him he would deliver it to Schampo where he is well acquainted, having often been there; and if the Lord gives success to him in his journey we hope to learn of your state and condition, if you have not forgotten us. We often think of you, especially in these times of disquietude in Europe. In my dreams I see this one and then another. That many of us think of you is undoubtedly caused by a letter Samuel's wife received in which it is stated that you cannot pay for military service and are necessitated to move out of the land. This causes me to wish that if only all who are in very limited circumstances would be here, where there are yet so many thousand acres of unsettled land and where one can live according to the dictates of one's own conscience, although the words, 'in sorrow and toil shalt thou earn thy living,' apply here as well as with you. But those who can and will work have food and raiment and what more do we want for the short pilgrimage, for we have here no abiding city. "I do not remember whether I have already written you of our beginning in America. When we arrived in Wayne County, Ohio we were $90.00 in debt; but thanks be to God, we were well and could work. Christian and Peter worked at the carpenter trade, building houses and barns. Samuel was with 

Abraham Zuercher and received good wages. (Wages were at that time two and three shillings per day. S. H. B.). I worked in the book-bindery and mother helped to make garments, so that in a little more than two years we were out of debt, but had no land. In Sonnenberg the land was quite high, in Indiana one could buy from the government one hundred sixty acres for $200. "Christian, with some others, came to Indiana and after him Peter; but the land had to be paid cash. Good friends loaned us enough money to buy two quarter sections. We followed Christian and Peter to Indiana in August, 1839. 

Twice we had to remain in the woods all night. Christian and

his wife came by way of water, (likely over Lake Erie to Toledo, and then per canal to Fort Wayne, Indiana and then overland to Wells County.-S. H. B.) and were a number of days ahead of us. (Christian evidently returned to Ohio to bring his family to Indiana. S. H. B.) Jeampier Meriliant who is married to Magdalena Kirchhofer, went with us and each family had one wagon with two oxen and two cows. When we came to our land we found Christian sick with a fever in a small log house in which there was no room for us. We had to live nine days in a 'Laubhuette,' a hut made of leafy branches. The first thing we did was to dig a well for water, for except the river, there are no running fountains. We found water, then we made a log house and lived together until we also had made one for Meriliants to move in. We were very glad when we had houses to live in, and water to drink, but we had little to live on. The potatoes were $1.00 per bushel. We only bought for planting, but the summer was very dry, and there were only a few planted. Flour per barrel is at present (1849) $4.50; we had to pay $10.80, and so far to get it. Around us is still all timber. Thus you can imagine the amount of work there is to do to get the land cleared. On the right side of the river (Wabash) where our land is located, (a mile northwest of Vera Cruz) there was no road cut out. 

When one had to seek the cows or cattle, one was in danger of becoming lost in the forest. It was necessary to make use of the compass. "We were also afflicted with sickness. The three brothers had the chill fever, also mother, who since then is often troubled with rheumatism; only Maria and I escaped. Barbara was sick for twenty-two months with consumption and died in October, 1847. Thus her suffering is over, and we thank 

God who helped us through. When we did not know what to do He assisted us, so that we could bear it. The God of mercy be praised for all His mercy and grace, Amen! "Now concerning our present condition in 1849. Christian has six sons and one daughter. He has a quarter section of land with eighty acres cleared. He has sixteen head of horses

BIOGRAPHIES 

185 and cattle, and he also has hogs and sheep. He sowed thirteen acres of wheat. "John has five sons and six daughters, and one hundred forty acres of land, with sixty of it cleared. He also sowed thirteen acres of wheat. He also has horses, cattle, sheep and hogs. "Peter also has a section of land and two horses and cows; he has one daughter and two sons. "Samuel has one hundred acres of land with some cleared. "Maria got married to Peter Bixler, son of Jacob. They have eighty acres of land which his father gave him. They have one horse, two cows, a yearling calf and some hogs. which Samuel gave her for keeping house for him. "Mother and I now live with Samuel who gives us our necessities of life, and we have plenty to eat. I am, thank 

God, quite well, excepting feebleness caused by old age, (now eighty-four years old). I can do no hard work. I work in the book-bindery. This is the third time I work at it, and will likely be the last time. Mother is still working on the land, and spins in the winter. Thus when you compare our present condition with the former you will notice quite a change. Of course the sons still have debts on their land, and even though the interests are high, six percent and more, what is this in comparison with the high interest of three to four In'doc, when one must yet buy bread. We now also have a flour-mill and a saw-mill two miles away. "At the present there are sixteen families that belong to our church. Every three weeks we hold divine worship. 

Christian and I alternate in giving exhortations. There are also an equal number of Amish people near here, also some Reformed church people and some Lutherans. There are also some German and French people here. I could yet write much that comes to my mind, but must close. What I have written in my last letter I do not remember; but one thing of which we often speak is a desire to have a school teacher, even if he is not so well educated, if he is only God-fearing and could teach the children to read and write well and do some figuring and

acquaint them with God's Word and the love of Christ. In our families are twenty children and there are others who desire that their children might receive instructions according to Swiss customs, for there is as yet no German school here. We hope the Lord will fulfil our desires. A schoolhouse is to be built between Samuel's and John's farms. (The schoolhouse was built on the northeast corner of my father's farm, and was known as the election schoolhouse of French Township. 

Later the place was changed a mile northwest, S. H. B.)" 

(Taken from "Brief Historical Sketches of Seven Gener ations" by Rev. S. H. Baumgartner.) (Still later, in January 1853, Rev. Baumgartner worte a letter to his son David. He was entering his eighty-ninth year and it, too, is a remarkable letter for a man in his eighties. 

His statements are clear and concise. In both letters he gave his views on religious experiences but they are rather lengthy and cannot be reproduced here. Editor)

CHRISTIAN BAUMGARTNER 

Christian was the first child of Rev. David Baumgartner, by his third wife. He began his journey of life in this unfriendly world, in Buderichgraben, Jura, Oberamt Courtelary, Canton Bern, Switzerland, January 31, 1809. His school privileges were meager, yet he achieved a fair German education. 

He came to America, settling in Wayne County, Ohio, in the spring of 1837. Believing the Scripture, that it is not good for man to be alone, he entered the marriage bond with Fanny 

Schrag, August 4, 1838. Rev. Peter Schneck, of the Mennonite

Church of the Sonnenberg society, in Wayne County, Ohio, solemnized the wedding ceremony. In the fall of 1838 he moved to Wells County, Indiana. Here he continued to live until his death, which occurred August 24, 1878, resulting from brain fever of only a few days' duration.

His first wife, Fanny Schrag, died October 24, 1850. This left him with seven small children. He looked about for a

BIOGRAPHIES 

187 second wife and mother to his children. Katharina Baumgartner (nee Lehman, who was the widow of John Baumgartner, and the mother of the well-known Dr. C. Cleophas Baumgartner, and sister of the Rev. Peter S. Lehman and of the wife of Rev. Christian Sprunger) became his second wife, De cember 2, 1852. The marriage was solemnized by his father. Christian became associate minister to his father, March 18, 1845, when the lot, according to the method pursued by the Mennonites, fell on him.

Later he was released from this obligation, but again made minister the second time, October 21, 1866. (This record was found in his Bible which became the property of his daughter, Maria Niederhauser, which is now the property of her son, Homer Niederhauser of Berne, 

Indiana.) He and his father alternated in preaching. They preached every three weeks, both in Adams and Wells counties according to his father's own statement. (See father's letter.)

He remained, after the second installation into the ministry, a minister until death. He was very loyal to his church, and strictly adhered to her polity, and to his religious convictions, and almost considered it a religious duty to shun those of his own household who held to other religious faiths. 

Mrs Katherina Lehman Baum gartner, second wife of Rev. 

Christian Baumgartner

In his younger days he frequently walked three miles to church on Sunday mornings, that he might be better able to meditate on the subject he wished to bring before the people. 

The writer of this sketch can see him yet with the mind's eye, walking along the public highway, in a deep contemplative

mood, or in bad weather riding with his family on a spring wagon, when Sunday morning came. He generally wore a long cutaway, swallow-tailed coat, without a lay down collar.

He was a man of medium height and weight, with round shoulders, and wore medium long whiskers. His life was not without trials and tribulations. He lived in an age when ignorance, lax morals and sectarianism often had high sway, and caused spirited disputations and vexation of spirit. But gradually out of this chaos came better things, holier belief and living, and denominational toleration emerged from the former chaotic conditions. In disposition he was peaceful, in work industrious, and in habits frugal, and thus he succeeded in making a comfortable living for his family. His children were all born in Wells County, Indiana.

The above was taken from "Brief Historical Sketches of 

Seven Generations" written by Rev. S. H. Baumgartner.

ULRICH KIPFER 

Ulrich Kipfer was the son of Ulrich and Anna Gerber 

Kipfer. He was born August 17, 1805 in Rheingut, Oberamt

Langnau, Canton Bern, Switzerland. He was married to Katharina, daughter of John and Katharina Thut Aeschleman on

July 7, 1827 in Langnau, by Peter Pfister. He came to America with his family May 3, 1851 and settled temporarily in Wayne County, Ohio. Undecided where he wanted to locate, he then, in company with Rev. Matthias Strahm, traveled through 

Indiana, Illinois, Iowa, Nebraska, Kansas, Missouri and Arkansas. The latter two were slave states, so they did not appeal, and there was also much yellow fever in Arkansas. 

Evidently Adams County, Indiana, was the most desirable place they saw for they came here in March, 1852 and settled in French township.

Mr. and Mrs. Kipfer had nine children. The eldest son, also named Ulrich, left the Mennonite Church in 1858 and helped Rev. Matthias Strahm organize the New Manist Church.

Some of the other children also joined the New Manists later

Very little is known of Mr. Kipfer as a preacher, but it is generally conceded that he was a likable, earnest, Christian man, devoted to his calling. He earned his living as a farmer.

MATTHIAS STRAHM 

Matthias Strahm was born in 1813, in Oberamt Langnau, Canton Bern, Switzerland. He left his native land in 1851 with his family and sailed on Easter Sunday with friends and relatives that numbered forty-two in all. They were on the water thirty-three days.

During the journey his five-year-old daughter fell ill and died. They had a short funeral service. They sang the German song, "Meine Lebenszeit verstreicht," after which Ulrich Kipfer led in prayer. After the service a stone was tied to her feet and she was lowered into the ocean. 

When they arrived in America they traveled by Lake and Canal to Cleveland, Ohio. They made temporary settlement in Wayne County, Ohio, close to Massillon. From there Matthias Strahm and Ulrich Kipfer went farther west to seek a place to make final settlement. They traveled through 

Indiana, Illinois, Nebraska, Kansas, Missouri and Arkansas.

The latter two were slave states, otherwise they would have been suitable. When they returned to Ohio it was then decided to settle in Indiana. So in March, 1852 they settled along the 

Wells and Adams County line.

In 1853 Mr. Strahm became one of the leaders of the 

Baumgartner Church in French Township, but he left it in 1858 to organize the New Manist Church. Later, in 1870, he moved to Kansas where he died in 1875.

BIOGRAPHIES 

191 

PETER S. LEHMAN 

Peter S. Lehman was born November 17, 1821, on the 

Buchwald, Canton Bern, Switzerland. He was the eighth of nine children born to Peter and Katherine Tschantz Lehman. When he was five years of age his mother died, leaving the brood of nine motherless. In due time the bereft husband brought home a stepmother to the motherless children, in the person of Barbara Sprunger, daughter of Rev. Peter Sprunger.

As a boy he tended his father's sheep on the Mung. At the age of thirteen, the year when his father was building a house, he was often called, sometimes dragged, from bed in the early morning and was sent with an older brother to fetch the horses from the pasture, and help get things in readiness so that immediately after breakfast the necessary trips to the stone quarry and sawmill could be made for building materials. 

Naturally to him this seemed a pretty rough way of handling a youngster.

The year 1835, when he was fourteen, he regarded as a year of special favor and blessing, for he was sent to school. 

The teacher was John Baumgartner, a very earnest Christian man, who later married his sister Katherine. The Christian instruction and training received at the hand of this teacher were of significant value to young Lehman. 

At the age of seventeen he was received into membership of the Muensterberg Mennonite Church through baptism. 

He found out later, however, that the old carnal nature did not drown in baptism, as it came very much into prominence at times, causing him to do things for which a guilty conscience afterward bitterly accused him. At the age of twenty-three he was stricken with severe illness, which kept him in bed eight weeks. Here, as he himself was wont to say, like 

Nebuchadnezzar he was brought to his senses. He cried to 

God for mercy and deliverance, and God heard his cry.

After his recovery he taught school, receiving the handsome "salary" of ten and one-half cents per day from the Church fathers, for they would not permit him to receive

Rev. Peter S. Lehman

Mrs. Peter S. Lehman

BIOGRAPHIES 

193 Government pay, since this would offer the Government a favorable opportunity to lay claim on him for military purposes.

In the year 1846, at the age of twenty-five, he was married to Verena Sprunger, daughter of Abraham Sprunger, and half sister to Rev. S. F. Sprunger. The young couple moved to their "honeymoon" home at the foot of a mountain on a piece of ground owned by his father. This however was not an ideal place to live, as often in winter for weeks at a time they never saw the sun, and for long stretches of time never saw another person. They felt isolated and lonely indeed. 

In July 1848 he was chosen by lot for the ministry. 

For a long time he was undecided whether or not to accept this call. According to his own confession, his new household cares, together with the almost impossible tax burden, and now this on top of it all, seemed almost too much for him to bear, for he had not yet learned to cast his burdens on the Lord and trust Him for help. But God works in wondrous ways. He sent a beloved brother, the Rev. Ulrich Steiner, to him, who with a gripping handclasp greeted him with the words, "God be gracious unto thee, my son." This made a deep impression on young Lehman, and after some further admonishing words he felt impelled to accept this as a call from God. He dedicated his life to the ministry and began his preaching career in the Muensterberg Mennonite Church.

The next year the young couple left their depressing valley home and moved to Domung, a brighter and much more attractive place. Their residence here, however, was shortlived, for after two years their faces were set toward America. 

On March 6, 1852, they with their two little girls, Anna and Marianna, together with a group of about eighty other "Muensterbergers," embarked at Havre, France, on the sailboat "Hanneman Norfolk." After forty-two days they landed on New York soil. From thence they went by steamboat up the Hudson to Albany, thence by rail to Buffalo, thence over Lake Erie to Cleveland, Ohio, again by rail to Massillon, and thence by wagon to Sonnenberg. They were warmly received 7

by the Mennonite brethren who had already settled there. 

The Lehmans, however, despaired of making Sonnenberg their home, for the price of land was beyond the purchasing power of their none too bulging purse. They went to Putnam County, from whence he with four others shank's mared it to Indiana to spy out the land for a possible future home. They came to the conclusion this would be a favorable location, as there was plenty of land available for a large Mennonite settlement. 

Already a few Mennonite families had settled in the vicinity of what is now Berne, but they had a distance of about eight miles to go to church, to what was then known as the "Baumgartner Church."

Lehman bought one hundred sixty acres of wooded land, only twenty acres of which was cleared. This was to be their future home. He hoofed it back to Putnam County, very soon after which some good Indiana friends moved the family by wagon to their Indiana home. It was no mansion that awaited them, but only a miserable little log hut, brutally pioneer, and to quote Lehman's own words, "one would now feel sorry for chickens if they had to spend a winter in so cold a place as that." The intention of course was very shortly to erect a more livable house, but due to sickness, a broken leg, and other unforeseen handicaps, it was not until five years later that this came to pass.

The first winter in this pioneer home was a terribly hard one for the young Lehman family. He was again compelled for many weeks to be on his back with sickness, of course totally unable to do any work whatsoever. The young mother, who already had her hands full with the care of the two little girls and the many duties of the home, now had this additional burden thrust upon her of caring for her sick husband. But to top it all off, she was called upon again to make a trip, mother-fashion, down into the Valley of Anguish, to return this time with her third little girl, Rachel (later Mrs. Peter

Gilliom). Her lot was indeed a hard one, and her cup was almost full to the brim. But she was made of stern stuff, courage and heroism not lacking. Do not think, dear reader,

BIOGRAPHIES 

195 that the only heroines are those whose names are blazoned in headlines across the pages of our daily papers or whose praises are sung from the housetops. Greater heroines than these are often found in obscure places, whose names do not appear in the limelight of publicity, but are hidden from the public eye, properly evaluated and truly known only by those who know them best. Such an one was this young mother Lehman. 

Fortunately, after some weeks the father recovered from his illness, which he largely attributed to the remedial properties of sassafras tea, which he drank copiously in the last stages of his illness. Once more he took up school teaching during the winter months, while the summer months were devoted to strenuous farming. 

When the first little Mennonite Church was built just across the highway, north of where the present Mennonite parsonage now stands, he became the pastor and continued as such for sixteen years, ministering to the spiritual needs of the growing community. One wishes it were possible to report only spiritual prosperity and growth in this pioneer church, but as the church was not made up of perfect people, church affairs did not always move in a smooth and perfect fashion either. Sad though it is to admit, the fact remains that Satan by subtle means was to a degree successful in bringing dissension, yea, even disruption, into this new and struggling church.

In the spring of 1868 Lehman felt it best to leave the church in other hands, so he and his family, together with a number of other families, migrated to the region of the Ozark mountains, in Hickory County, in southwestern Missouri. More families followed later, and a church was founded, with Lehman as its pastor. Here he was again farmerpreacher, for twenty-six years. He preached without salary, served the church faithfully, organized a Sunday school, and gave catechetical instruction. He lived a godly life, and was held in the highest esteem by his church constituency as well as by others. He was a man of sterling character, strong

personality, a man of his word. Though the spiritual welfare of others was always uppermost in his heart and mind, he was ever ready to render material help to those who were more needy and less fortunate than he. 

As time went on there were many crop failures in this region, people became discouraged and one by one the families moved away. Finally, after much coaxing on the part of relatives in Berne, the now aged Lehmans sold their farm and moved back to Indiana to spend their remaining years in Berne. Here, in 1896, through a surprise arrangement by their children and grandchildren their fiftieth wedding anniversary was celebrated, which brought much joy to their hearts and tears to their eyes. It was a most impressive occasion, never to be forgotten by those who were in attendance. The Rev. 

S. F. Sprunger was also present, making some most touching and appropriate remarks.

The total number of years they were permitted to enjoy their married life together was fifty-three. 

To this pair the following eight children were born at various stages of their wanderings from place to place; some in Switzerland, some in Indiana and some in Missouri: 

Anna (Mrs. Christian Luginbill)

Marianna (Mrs. Christian Gilliom) Rachel (Mrs. Peter Gilliom) 

Katherine (Mrs. D. C. Neuenschwander)

Japhet 

Dina (Mrs. Joel Welty) 

Emma (Mrs. James McCaslin) 

Lydia (Mrs. W. T. Lehman) Of these only two are living at the present time, Mrs. Dina 

Welty of Fort Wayne, Indiana, and Mrs. James McCaslin of 

Mullinsville, Kansas.

After returning from Missouri, Grandfather Lehman was permitted to live in Berne six years longer, when it pleased the Heavenly Father to call him suddenly, without sickness or suffering, by death, on the morning of February 3, 1899 (which was also the year of D. L. Moody's death). Grandmother

BIOGRAPHIES 

197 Lehman was permitted to live fourteen years longer, when she, too, was called after a period of intense suffering. They have gone "the way of all the earth," and their bodies now lie side by side in the M. R. E. Cemetery near Berne, where together they await the resurrection day. 

To the grandchildren the memories of these beloved grandparents are very precious indeed, and they count it a high honor to be lineal descendants of this noble pair. Truly may they say in Psalmist language, "The lines have fallen unto us in pleasant places, yea we have a goodly heritage.” 

CHRISTIAN SPRUNGER 

Christian Sprunger, one of the early pioneers who immigrated to America in 1852 with a company of relatives and friends, was born on April 10, 1828 at Latan Dachsfeldenberg, Switzerland. Miss Marianna Lehman, his friend, was also a member of this group. On June 3, 1852, some time after their arrival they were united in marriage in Wayne County, Ohio, where they stopped a few weeks before coming to Indiana. They purchased a tract of land in Adams County, one mile north from the present site of Berne where they established their home. It meant a life of isolation, for the forest surrounded them and no lights from neighbors were visible. 

Sometimes no other human being was seen for weeks with the exception of a lone hunter now and then. They endured many privations and hardships. Roads were almost unknown, mere trails answering their purpose. Fort Wayne was the nearest business center to which place they had to go for some of the bare necessities. A trip to the city required not less than three days, a day going, a day for the business transactions, and a day returning. The axe and the pick had to be taken along to clear away narrow places in the trail. 

On leaving for one of such trips, one of the children, a threeyear-old boy, was in perfectly good health when Mr. Sprunger left home, but the child took sick and was dead a number of hours before his return.

Five Brothers 

Seated: Peter E. Sprunger, Abraham J. Sprunger, teacher of the first public school in Berne.

Standing, Johannes Sprunger, first song leader in the Berne church, Rev. Christian 

Sprunger, David Sprunger.

On April 13, 1856 he was chosen as minister of the Mennonite settlers and in the spring of 1868 he was ordained as elder. He continued his farming along with his preaching,

BIOGRAPHIES 

199 retiring from both on account of failing health several years before his death which occurred October 18, 1903 on the Sprunger homestead which had changed from a log cabin to a lovely country home.

The following children born to this union are, living, Mrs. 

Peter A. Sprunger, Mrs. Samuel Steiner, Gideon, Rosina C. and Mrs. Fred Hanni of Berne, and Rev. Joel Sprunger of 

Stafford, Kansas. The deceased are Mrs. Anna Beer, Mrs. 

Elizabeth Burry, Ulrich, and Mrs. Christian Gilliom and Christian who died as a small child.

CHRISTIAN AUGSBURGER 

Christian Augsburger was born January 19, 1821, in Liberty Township, Tioga County, Pennsylvania, a son of John and 

Elizabeth Jacob Augsburger, both of Canton Bern, Switzerland. The Augsburger family came to America in 1817 and settled in the northern part of Pennsylvania. In 1829, when only eight years of age he moved with his parents to Wayne County, Ohio, where the family lived for thirteen years. Again in 1842 they moved on a farm in French Township, Adams County, Indiana, three and one-half miles west of Berne. 

Numerous times during his early manhood he visited in Wayne County, Ohio, and always walked the entire journey of nearly two hundred miles. On December 1, 1859, he was united in marriage with Miss Barbara Liechty of French Township, to which union were born sixteen children, and twelve grew to manhood and womanhood. They lived on a farm in French 

Township, which Mr. Augsburger had bought prior to their marriage. He had no thought of becoming a minister, but in 

1865 was chosen by lot to become the assistant pastor of the Baumgartner church in French Township. After the choice had been made he went alone into the woods and kneeling beside a log prayed that if this be God's work for him, with the help of God he would do the best he could. He was ordained to the ministry that same year and served the church until 1869, after which he preached in homes and schoolhouses.

He died at his home in French Township, January 19, 1903, at the age of eighty-two. He was a devout Christian father and a minister of the gospel for thirty-eight years.

PETER HABEGGER 

Peter Habegger's father was also named Peter; his mother was the former Elisabeth Sprunger (Bethli), oldest sister of S. F. Sprunger's father. Peter was born February 

21, 1801, in Trunda, Oberamt Münster, Canton Bern, Switzerland. He had four sisters, Barbara, Anna (who married David 

Moser, a hat maker. They lived in the Vera Cruz settlement), Elisabeth, mother of the late Jacob Wulliman, and

Verena, mother of Peter Burkhalter of Berne. 

Mr. Habegger was married to Elisabeth, daughter of John and Katherine Baumgartner Neuenschwander, on February 25, 1832, by Peter Sprunger, his grandfather. The wedding took place in the Trunda Church in an "open church service." 

The Peter Habegger's had four children, three daughters and a son: Anna (Mrs. Christian B. Steiner), Barbara, Marianna (first Mrs. Michael Neuenschwander, later Mrs. Jacob C. 

Liechty), and Jacob, who was the ancestor of a host of Habeggers in the Berne community.

Mr. Habegger came to America, to Adams County, with his wife and four children May 11, 1865 and settled one and one-half miles northwest of Berne. He then became assistant to Rev. Christian Sprunger and served the church faithfully until he died, May 15, 1872. Later, in February, 1875, his wife and daughter Barbara moved to Allen County, Ohio, where 

Mrs. Habegger died February 16, 1897, at the age of 84. She was a descendant of Rev. David Baumgartner's father.

It is not known when Mr. Habegger was elected minister, or by whom he was installed into the ministry. 

Mr. Habegger was buried in the church burial ground near the church which he had served. Some years later many of the graves were moved to the M. R. E. Cemetery, but Mr. 

Habegger still lies buried under the filling station, north, across the street from the parsonage.
