SAMUEL F. SPRUNGER 

If certain women had had their way on January 16, 1850, in far away Switzerland there would be no occasion to write this biography. On that day the mother of Samuel Ferdinand 

Sprunger passed away. She was the former Magdalena 

Rüffenacht of Eggiwil, and had been a good wife and stepmother to Abraham Sprunger and his children by a former marriage. One of the children described her as a "very liebe 

Stiefmutter." When she died some of the relatives whispered, "If it had only pleased the Lord that his mother might have 

Birthplace of S. F. Sprunger, Münsterberg, Canton Bern, Switzerland taken little Sammie with her, how well he would be cared for." 

Sammie was only fifteen months old, so one can appreciate their feelings.

Looking back now, almost ninety years after that incident, one can hardly fail to see what seems a divine purpose in leaving the baby behind. Men who do not take Christ into account may look on it as a chance happening of history, but Christians who recognize that God has specific plans for His children will feel otherwise. Surely it should at least suggest a divine hand reaching out to fashion out of many elements

a great result, for Sammie found a way to let Christ make history in his own life. At any rate he was left, a mere infant, the only child by the second marriage.

His eldest half-sister, Verena, was married two years before his birth, so after his mother's death his two half-sisters, Elizabeth and Anna, had to care for him. 

On November 29, 1851 there was a double wedding when his brother Peter (Pete) and his sister Elizabeth were married. On that day little Sammie stuck to Elizabeth "like glue," she had to hold him most of the time, but she did not seem to mind it much. Perhaps he sensed the fact that she was leaving him.

The following spring the family came to America in company with the large number of Sprungers elswhere mentioned in this history. Another brother, Abraham, better known as Läng Hämi, was fifteen years old when they arrived and since the family was very poor he had to work at different places in the community, and Anna, aged twelve, kept house for the father and little brother for almost ten years when she, too, was married. After that father and son had the housework to do as well as the farm work. They lived in a log house on what is now the Leon Neuenschwander farm. 

Sammie was baptized April 14, 1865 by Peter S. Lehman, his brother-in-law, in company with Marianne Sprunger, Anna Riff, J. C. Neuenschwander, Sam Riff and Stephen Sprunger. 

He was not happy to be elected to the ministry. It was not because he was not interested in religion, but he had not had a deep religious experience and realized he was not fit to be the shepherd of the flock. Neither had he had much opportunity to study, so he entered the Mennonite school in Wadsworth and was a member of the first class to be graduated from that institution in 1871. 

St. Francis De Sales once said, "If He calls you to a kind of service which is according to His Will but not according to your taste, you must not go to it with less, rather with more courage and energy than if your taste coincided with His Will." Sammie did just that and in his warmed heart a new

BIOGRAPHIES 

203 religious certainty was born. He had a new vision and he followed that vision with service, an essential of Christianity. 

Ever after his life was wholly surrendered to the Divine Will and Plan, always obedient unto the heavenly vision. No doubt, some of his religious fervor was passed down from his father. 

When a small boy he observed that his father considered Communion as a very holy matter; on Saturday afternoon before Communion services were observed on Sunday he did not do any work that absolutely did not have to be done, thereby preparing for the following day. 

With him religion was not an appendix to life, much less an escape from it; to him religion was life itself, everyday life, its work, its play, its sorrows, its joys. He had a Christian philosophy of life that determined his attitude toward God, himself, others and things.

At Wadsworth he came in close contact with Professor Carl Justus van der Smissen, and thus began one of the pleasantest friendships of his life. But more of his life at Wadsworth later on.

When he returned home he met opposition and fierce controversies in the church. He became discouraged and disappointed, and to make matters worse, rumors reached his ears that his fiancee, Katherine Luginbill, was turning against him because of his new ideas. This, however, was not true and after his school debts were paid they were married October 

2, 1872 by Rev. Christian Baumgartner. Sammie had borrowed Abraham A. Sprunger's horse and buggy and in the forenoon they drove to Rev. Baumgartner's home where they were married. Mrs. Baumgartner insisted on their staying there for their wedding dinner, "rösti" being part of the meal. 

The first year they lived with her parents, Mr. and Mrs. 

Peter Luginbill, who lived on what is now the Kuntz farm, west of Lehman Park. In the meantime the church built the home two miles northwest of Berne, (where Jacob B. Steiner's now live), on land donated by his brother Abraham. They lived there until August 22, 1881 when they moved to Berne into the house that is still occupied by his widow and two

Mrs. S. F. Sprunger 

Rev. S. F. Sprunger

BIOGRAPHIES 

205 daughters. Abraham Lehman (father of Isaac) donated the land for the new residence, and C. W. Baumgartner furnished the timber from his own forest free of charge, other church members contributed by cutting down the trees and getting the lumber ready for use.

Those were the days when the church did not pay the preacher, so "der Sam" as he was now called, taught school. 

They did not celebrate various holidays then as we do now so the children were delighted whenever they had a day off. 

About the only time this occurred was when their preacherteacher had to take time off for funerals. There were no telephones or newspapers so there was no way of notifying them beforehand, but the "Heute keine Schule" notice on the blackboard delighted them, the explanation that followed that he was conducting so and so's funeral probably didn't interest them.

Joel and Dan Welty had a book store which Joel had opened in 1882. They secured "der Sam" to assist them in the selection of books, so in 1884 his name was also incorporated in the firm name and it became known as Welty and Sprunger. They also did a furniture business in connection with the book store.

When the General Conference met in Berne in 1884 the book store became a church institution instead of a private concern as heretofore, and Welty and Sprunger became the publishing agents for the Mennonite Book Concern as it was later known, and S. F. Sprunger became the Business Manager for the Publication Board. He held that office from 1884 to 1890. 

When "Der Christliche Bundesbote" became the first official periodical of the General Conference in January of 1882, Rev. Sprunger was one of the assistant editors. 

In 1889 he began editing the German Sunday School Quarterly and he continued that work until 1920. He edited the Bundesbote Kalender from 1886 to 1922. 

After the Middle District Conference was organized in 1888, he was the first president and he continued in that office

until 1895 when he missed the conference because of illness in the home. In 1897 and 1898 he again served in that capacity. 

He preached the Conference sermons from the following texts: 

1889, John 1, 20-24; 1890, Psalm 122; 1891, I Peter 4, 10-11; 

1892, Ephesians 4, 15-16; 1893, II Chronicles 32, 7-8; 1894, Jeramiah 29, 7; 1897, I Peter 5, 1-6; 1898 (?). He was delegate every year up to 1906 except in 1895 and again in 1902 when he had eye trouble. From then on he attended only three sessions, the last one being in 1919. In 1890 he was appointed one of two evangelistic workers in the Conference, J. F. Lehman being the other one. He served on many committees, and it has been said that his remarks usually ended the debate on almost any question.

He had an absolutely unshakable conviction at times. 

He thought what convinced him must convince everybody. 

He was not afraid to express his feelings if he felt a thing should be preached or talked about. In fact this outspoken manner was embarrassing at times, not only to strangers and friends, but to members of his family.

He had a sense of humor but it was limited to certain aspects of life. If a remark deviated in the minutest detail from the truth, even if said jokingly, it would not do, and he did not hesitate to call one's attention to it. Because of this outspoken manner he did not have the faculty of making one feel at ease in his presence. 

He was very fortunate in his choice of a life companion. 

She made his work easier for him. They kept open house for all visiting preachers, missionaries and lecturers during his pastorate, and how he enjoyed those visits! He radiated his joy when one of them came and they spent much time swapping interesting stories. 

Rev. Sprunger was a thinker, an intellectual leader of men, but above all he was utterly devoted to God. He was a man of deep consecration, not self-centered. He made no claims upon greatness, but he had a spark of genius in him.

He had flashes of that rare and very precious gift of God, spiritual insight. He saw into things, through things. By

BIOGRAPHIES 

207 virtue of these gifts he was an authentically great preacher. 

Some of his sermons (simple but intense) still live in the hearts of many people, unfaded by time, bearing the very tones of his voice. He knew how to tell truths with an artless art of sincerity. As a preacher one can say of him, here he is at his greatest and best. As a pastor and comforter of the sick and troubled souls he did not rank high. The social contact was lacking in him, but he did shine in the pulpit as a great preacher and organizer.

To do justice to such an outstanding character means that one should have a personal knowledge of all his activities. 

This we do not have, so it is not in our power to write an adequate biography of him. Consequently we shall have to rely upon what others have written about him. 

Ten years ago there appeared in The Mennonite a symposium compiled by N. G. Fankhauser. The contributors were two of his contemporaries who were his childhood playmates, chums of his youth, and intimate associates during his pastorate, D. C. Sprunger, his nephew, and C. A. Neuenschwander, also Mr. Fankhauser, Rev. J. W. Kliewer, Rev. C. H. A. van der Smissen, Sister Hillegonda van der Smissen of Newton, Kansas and Rev. C. E. Krehbiel, also of Newton, Kansas. It follows in part: "This appreciation of the life and services of our departed 

Brother Rev. Samuel Ferdinand Sprunger, is not intended to be an exaltation, or what too often post mortem eulogies of prominent personages amount to, a veneration of a mortal human being. It is simply a due appreciation of his life and service while he sojourned among us. Neither is this effort a negation of a good and great man's limitations, failings or weaknesses, a condition common to the best of mortals even though it is not the purpose of this paper to enumerate them anymore than denying them. "Taking the above for granted, we herein confine ourselves as his fellow mortals, to mentioning some of the outstanding characteristics of this man, whom we all loved dearly

for his exemplary service and now fondly cherish his undying memory. "What has the life and work of Brother Sprunger meant to us as a community, as his parish for more than thirty years, and as the Conference, of which he was a very active member for nearly half a century. This is the task assigned to the writer, or rather compiler of this article. But, valuable as the years of acquaintance with Brother Sprunger have been to the writer, still that acquaintance falls far short of what is involved in this article to form a sufficient basis for a due appreciation of that extraordinarily useful life. Brother

Sprunger's life and service has been too big for any one single individual acquaintance to be able to evaluate adequately. 

So I have taken it upon myself to draw upon the more competent sources of personal acquaintances still surviving to furnish us a proper evaluation of Brother Sprunger's services among us, and I have arranged these contributions in the form of a symposium. "What is the true basis of evaluation of the life of a 

Christian servant? According to the controlling principles of Brother Sprunger's own life it is nothing short of the example of his Lord and Master, Jesus Christ. Like the apostle

Paul, the ruling principle of all of Brother Sprunger's decisions and activities in his parish and in conference work was always, How would my Lord and Master have me decide this question or solve this problem, or How would the Kingdom of God be best furthered? No matter, in Church or Conference, would be too small or too great to be decided on that basis. While a man of intensely practical views of life, and far from being given to mystical reveries, his extraordinary gift of common sense and perspicacity in all matters up for decision was always hallowed with a deep sense of submission to the will of God as revealed in His Word. This then, is the basis of evaluation of the services of our departed 

Brother's life. "Although Mr. Sprunger and Mr. Neuenschwander somewhat cover the same period of his life, nevertheless each

BIOGRAPHIES 

209 draws an interesting pen picture from his own individual point of view. "C. A. Neuenschwander, who has the earliest recollections of young 'Sprunger Sammie,' was within a few months the same age when they emigrated with their parents from Muensterberg in Switzerland as little three-year-old toddlers on the wobbly deck of a sail boat across the wild Atlantic. Here is 

Brother Neuenschwander's story of Sammie: 

""The statement is often made by public writers, and also in private conversations that great orators, or famous public speakers, are born and not made by education or practice, a claim that undoubtedly contains more truth than poetry, and the same claim holds good in regard to public leaders in the ministerial or religious field, as well as in the sphere of politics or statesmanship. All outstanding men, speakers and leaders, were born for their respective work, and not merely made so alone by study and using the tongue. ""The subject that I am requested to describe in this sketch is our dearly beloved Rev. Samuel F. Sprunger, and especially about his boyhood days. He was a born speaker and an excellent debater, and also a born leader, and he showed these qualifications in his early days. Being a fluent speaker, with a clear, strong voice, witty, humorous, jolly, friendly, a good entertainer, and absolutely honest, made him an admirable companion and also the main speaker in gatherings of young people. Whenever he spoke the others present were silent and diligent listeners. He was held in high esteem as leader of his companions and not without cause. While he was full of life, fond of clean fun, and ever ready to entertain his young followers, he would never lead them into any bad, rough or unclean doings. Many boys and girls had all reasons to be thankful for his guiding hand in his maturer service. 

He had at least one thing in common with the great London preacher, Charles H. Spurgeon: the latter once said that there were three D's that he always hated, namely: "Dirt, Debts, and the Devil," and so it was with Rev. Sprunger; he hated all dirty things, and debts, and was antagonistic to all of the

works of the devil, with all the power God had endowed him with. He was so highly esteemed and so well loved by all who were intimately acquainted with him, that when he was drawn by lot into the ministry at the age of about twenty years, more sympathetic tears flowed than on any other occasion in the history of our Mennonite church in Berne. "It is wonderful how God moulds the career of his future servants from early childhood. Born of poor parents on the 

19th day of October, 1848, on the Muensterberg in the Bernese

Jura, Switzerland, he lost his mother by death at the age of fifteen months. He crossed the Atlantic ocean with his father and others of his family in the early months of 1852, landing in the wilds of the virgin woods of Adams County, Indiana. 

After the age of eight he worked three years for his halfbrother Peter, and later at different places on farms until God guided his course to the ministry. "His schooling was necessarily meagre in those pioneer days. Many a youngster spends more time now in one school year on schoolhouse benches than little Sammie did in public school in all his life. Driving ox teams, cutting away timber, burning brush piles, rolling logs in piles and burning them. were about all the schooling he had received up to the time he was drafted into the ministry. We do not need to marvel that God led him on to enter the Mennonite School at Wadsworth, Ohio, to develop his inborn talents for the service in his Master's Vineyard. One main factor in upholding his decision to enter that school was Rev. Christian Baumgartner.

He advised Sprunger to attend school at least long enough to be able to speak the "Highgerman" language correctly. Said

Baumgartner had served as preacher many years northwest of Berne and he knew the handicap of being able only to speak in the Swiss tongue. The congregation at Berne opposed, with few exceptions, Sammie's desire for more education, some members even to the extent that they never would hold communication with him afterwards.' "The other restrospect of Sammie's youth follows from the

BIOGRAPHIES 

211 pen of D. C. Sprunger, a playmate of his boyhood, several years his junior. "Sammy entered his career under apparently unfortunate circumstances, as he lost his mother in early infancy. His father, on account of his occupation, was away from home a great part of the time. No wonder that at the funeral of his mother well-meaning relatives wished that he too could have been taken. But it was at that time as it was in the time of old when the Lord said to Samuel: "The Lord sees not as man seeth." I Samuel 16, 7. He knew why the mother was not permitted to take Sammy with her to glory, for in His all-wise plan Sammy was spared for a great mission of which the General Conference knows about to this day. He is a prominent character in the history of Mennonite foreign missions, home missions, and most of all, the First Mennonite Church of Berne, Indiana. God permitted him to be the instrument to build up and guide this church in a most blessed way. "When only three years old he showed quite a liking and ability for vocal music, and this stayed with him to the end. 

As the language spoken in his Swiss home was mostly French he had already learned a French song which he freely hummed and sang to his American admirers. "Another characteristic of Sammy was that in early youth he was generally able to squeeze himself out of difficulties. 

As a three-year-old boy he had already been taught manners at the table, so by penalty of punishment, he was never to say of food that was offered him: "It is not good," or "I don't like it."

But on coming to this new country certain foods were new and unpalatable to him. At one time on a visit such food was served and he ate his portion without complaint, but when the generous hostess wanted to give him another helping of the same he said, "It was good, but I don't want any more." His boyhood days were much as those of other boys of his age. 

He was not loud or boisterous in general, but he was humorous and even sarcastic, sometimes somewhat inconsiderate, as may be shown by an incident later. "Even as a boy his personality was attractive. The writer,

spite of such occaseveral years younger than Sammy, spent days and weeks and months in his company. Well do I remember what a tease he was, how often I would run to mother and complain that uncle Sammy teased or even abused me. Mother generally said, "Keep away from him." But no! In sional trouble the drawing power was too great to resist. This trait was noticeable in his character in the latter part of his life. He had not the gift of being very friendly or obliging, but he seemed to possess a certain drawing power. "As mentioned above, as a young man, he, like others, was sometimes not only humorous but sarcastic which he kept up in his later years. Once an incident occurred which showed him the folly of thoughtless expression. At an evening party a crowd of young men were seated in a room, young Sprunger entered the room with the remark, "Heh! the clover is blooming in here!" As a reply to this Sammy received a violent ear-box. In the room sat a young man with clover-blossomred hair, and the fellow made short process to remind Sammy that he had understood. "One effective weapon he used against his opposition in his early ministry was his calm and quiet disposition. When falsely accused he would not even engage in a word fight but keep on going according to his conviction. Even in the later years of his ministry he sometimes was very firm in his conviction and defended it to the very limit, but when the majority decided against his opinion he would at once give in and would work with the accepted plan and rarely ever mentioned his former plan. Thus his drawing disposition, as in former years, brought him the sympathy of the young people, and by them was built up his success in his ministry to the church.' "Young Samuel's school career at Wadsworth is intimately described by a member of the family of the leading spirit of the Wadsworth school, Sister Hillegonda, daughter of Samuel's most highly valued mentor, Professor C. J. van der Smissen, which follows: "Brother Samuel Ferdinand Sprunger came to Wadsworth in the fall of 1868, to study in the first Mennonite Academy.

BIOGRAPHIES 

213 Later he used to say that at the beginning he repeatedly felt like running away from the school, but as the teacher assigned to the task to teach the young ministers was expected to arrive in winter, he decided to hold out till he came. At the end of 

December 1868, Professor van der Smissen arrived at the Academy with his family and from then on Brother Sprunger said he had no more desire to run away. He studied with great interest and zeal, not only in classes but he daily sought out his teacher to converse with him on what concerned him most. 

But not only this teacher, but also the Spirit of God worked with him, and he was imbued with new spiritual life. Things changed altogether. He used his opportunity faithfully even though his studies were at one time interrupted with serious illness. 'During vacation he, with three of his fellow students, stayed at Wadsworth, as he had no parental home that expected him and had but little money to spare for travel. He stayed and worked in the harvest field. His services were sought as a good 'cradler,' and he was otherwise a very handy man on the farm. Evenings were spent in company with such companions as Father van der Smissen and William Galle, with whom he swapped life experiences. In working with various employers and living in their families, his wide-awake observance caught many a life lesson for his future work. 

He had a rare gift to understand other people's circumstances and viewpoints and the Lord trained His future servant also thereby for the service he was called to perform. ""The afternoons in school were to some extent used by the students in manual training work for the school and young Brother Sprunger came handy as a proficient carpenter. "Even as a student, Brother Sprunger had frequent occasions to develop his rare gift as preacher as well as in Sunday School. He thus became a blessing in this activity to many. 

On the occasion of a Christmas festival for the students, Brother Sprunger was the eloquent spokesman for the latter by delivering an address of thanks to Professor van der Smissen. On the occasion of a farewell service he composed a song

which the students sang. The five young graduating theologians gave short addresses, besides the German teacher, Brother Schultz, and Prof. van der Smissen blessed them while they were kneeling in a semi-circle around him in prayer. It was an impressive service that they remembered through life. Brother Sprunger's departure from school was touching. The seriousness of his calling lay heavily on him. 

His brother Abe (Läng Hämi), who visited him at Wadsworth, offered him a home to begin with. ""With his beloved teacher, the venerable Professor van der Smissen, he kept up regular correspondence. They both were instrumental in the organization of the Salem congregation on Sonnenberg. Upon the former's death, Rev. Sprunger preached his funeral sermon.' "Brother Sprunger's chief life service, as pastor of the 

Berne Mennonite Church, is here evaluated by Brother Neu enschwander, for many years associated with Brother Sprunger as member of the Board of Deacons: "No one can appreciate an unbiased, fairminded, careful and farseeing preacher more than a sincere member of his flock who was closely connected with him and served under his leadership for years as a deacon of his church. This was the privilege of the writer of this paragraph. Many difficult problems among church members presented themselves for adjustment, some cases demanding immediate action by the church board and of the utmost complicated order, involving high-standing members of the congregation, cases that required the most careful handling according to God's command, and to do justice to all concerned. To all these Rev. Sprunger seemed equal. I do not remember of one case that was decided under his leadership that was, or ought to have been recalled or reversed. He was practical in all his work and surely so in hearing complaint from one church member against another. 

A sister in the church once said, "I will never again go to Sam Sprunger with a complaint against another church member.' 

When asked, why not? She answered: "I thought I had a grievance against another church member and went and re-

BIOGRAPHIES 

215 lated the matter to Sprunger. He then asked me, 'have you spoken to the offending party?' I then said no, I do not like to face the party. He then said, 'O yes, you will; I will hitch my horse to the buggy and drive you over to see the person and settle the matter if we possibly can." They went and the difficulty was settled then and there and the sister never brought another complaint. A number of similar proceedings could be cited. He was a most tolerant leader in handling an erring member; his sole aim was to get an erring one to see his, or her, mistake, to truly repent, make peace with God and the congregation, and then go on and sin no more. He was also fair-minded to other ministers; he often said that he never heard a sermon preached but that he could and did learn something. If there was nothing elevating in the sermon he could at least learn how not to preach.' "As a climax of the evaluation of Rev. Sprunger's services we let Rev. J. W. Kliewer's estimate follow here: "One of the first things I noticed about Rev. S. F. Sprunger when I became his successor and co-worker was his sense of justice. That to me was the key to many of his actions and attitudes. Justice is usually objective in its applications and not subjective. Because it can show no favoritism it will at times seem stern. Rev. Sprunger could, because of his pronounced sense of justice, warm up more readily over a cause than over a person. Two illustrations must suffice to throw light upon this. After his successor in the pastorate had been in the church but a comparatively short time, Rev. Sprunger announced publicly that from now on the members of the church should go to the new pastor for marriages, funerals and so on. He evidently did not want personal feelings to decide in these services, but that which would most consistently work out for the good of the church. As a member of the Foreign Mission Board he repeatedly insisted upon it that gifts for missions should not be given because of friendship for this or the other worker but because of the love for the cause. He argued that otherwise ill feeling might arise between the missionaries because some would be more lib-

erally supported than others, that support would be sporadic and therefore no far-reaching plans could be made, and that the church would be compelled to scatter its forces into too many fields by each congregation following its pet missionary with its support. "Rev. Sprunger possessed a wonderful organizing and executive ability. Although he visited his large membership but rarely, he was well informed about what was happening in the everyday affairs of his members. A member whose steps needed watching, complained that the preacher had an uncanny way of finding out. He had his finger on every pulsebeat of the church. ""He was a man of positive convictions, and he had a forceful way of stating these convictions. He was for this reason a debater of unusual ability. Anyone not knowing Rev. 

Sprunger might interpret his advice as a demand, but he did. not seem to admire a man who would uncritically take his advice without first giving it the tests of his own mental reactions. He would defend his point tenaciously, but if need be, could also yield it gracefully. When the present church was to be erected, Rev. Sprunger at first opposed the plan to build. for reasons which seemed legitimate to him. When, however, a small majority carried the day for building, he with almost every other in the minority group stood by the decision of the small majority, and he lent every help within his possibility to bring the building to completion. "Brother Sprunger had a prodigious memory. But with this strength of memory there was not coupled the usual weakness of good memories, lack of analysis of thought, for Rev. 

Sprunger's mind was keenly analytical. While hearing an address or reading an article he would not only take it up into his memory but while doing so would analyze it and register his mental reactions to it. At one time he heard a sermon in the English language. He took no notes. A short time afterwards he gave the same sermon in German, giving full acknowledgment to the preacher who had first preached it. 

Some who heard both the original and the repeated sermon,

BIOGRAPHIES 

217 pronounced the repeated one the more effective, because Rev. 

Sprunger had put his own soul slant to it and had thrown his own dynamic personality into it. "In his preaching Rev. Sprunger leaned towards the expository type with ample and apt illustrations. He had no sympathy for sermons that did not have a definite Biblical foundation. His vocabulary and sentence structure were so simple that even unlettered persons could easily follow him. 

His logic was so convincing, and his analysis of human character so piquant that it won the admiration of the keenest of intellects. His delivery was forceful with more of explosive intensity to it than warmth. His hearers went away not congratulating themselves for having heard a "fine" sermon, but with their conscience pricked and a challenge to better living. 'Rev. Sprunger was an example of the finest type of selfforgetfulness. His last years were years of severe and uninterrupted physical suffering. Yet very few aside of his most intimate friends ever heard him say much about his suffering. 

He said he did not know what good he could do others by burdening them with his troubles.' 66 "Rev. S. F. Sprunger became a familiar name in the entire conference of the denomination early in his pastoral career. 

Just what proportions his labors assumed during his almost fifty years of connection with it is graphically shown by the record Rev. C. E. Krehbiel, secretary of the General Conference, has kindly furnished for this symposium. The record follows: 

""The first time Brother Sprunger is mentioned is in 1872, when he represented Muensterberg, Indiana, with one vote.

In 1875 he represented Berne with three votes and from then on he seems to have attended every session to and including 

1911, his last one. 66 'He served on his first committee in 1872 examining the records of Monthly Meetings of the Board of Managers of the Wadsworth school. In 1875 he was elected into a similar committee; he was also made a member of an investigating committee of seven to adjust difficulties in the school.

"From 1876 to 1890 he was a member of the Business or Program committee of the Conference. In 1876 he was on a committee that was to present a plan under which Wadsworth could be operated without incurring further debt. At that session he also pledged $22 for the Berne church for two years (two votes) to support Wadsworth. "In 1878 he was chosen into the committee to prepare a Katechimus and Glaubensbekenntnis. He was made Reiseprediger of the Conference and was on the Wadsworth school committee. "In 1881 he was on the committee that presented a plan for merging the Friedensbote, Zur Heimat, and Nachrichten aus der Heidenwelt into Der Christliche Bundesbote. He was also a member of the committee that prepared the Formularbuch. "In 1884 he was a member of a committee that drew up the plan for the publication work to be entrusted to the Board of Publication. He served on the Foreign Mission Board from 

1884 to 1887 and again from 1896 to 1917. In 1902 he requested the Conference to elect or name a proxy to serve on the Foreign Mission Board for him on account of illness; and in 

1908, when he and Brother J. W. Kliewer, then both of Berne, were elected into the Foreign Mission Board, he requested that another be chosen in his stead because they two were from the same church; but the conference desired that he continue on the Board. He was also a member of the Publication 

Board from 1884 to 1893. (At that time there was no rule against being a member of two or more Boards at the same time. He was a member of every standing Board and of the program committee in 1884.) In 1884 he prepared a Church 

Record which the Conference recommended. ""In 1887 he was elected a Trustee. "In 1890 he was on the Nominating committee and again elected editor of the German Sunday School Quarterlies and the Bundesbote Kalender. It is not clear from the records how long he served in this editorial capacity. This year he

BIOGRAPHIES 

219 was also chosen in the committee to draft the Conference 

Constitution. 

"He served again as Trustee from 1893 to 1914. From 

1893 to 1896 he again served on the Home Mission Board. "In 1896 he was a member of the Resolution committee. ""In 1911 he was a member of the Nominating committee and of the committee to revise the Katechismus. This was the last Conference he attended. "In 1917 poor health kept him at home and the Foreign 

Mission Board met with him in Berne.' "A similar record of work that Brother Krehbiel furnished from the General Conference records could be given of Rev. 

Sprunger's labors in District Conferences. He was prominently involved in the organization of the Middle District Conference and in its activities, particularly in its home mission work. 

A picture of the kind of his activities is furnished us by 

Brother C. H. A. van der Smissen, son of Rev. Sprunger's venerable teacher, and his constant co-worker in the conferences. Here follows Brother van der Smissen's pen picture of Rev. Sprunger's conference activities: "In conference he was deliberate, but when he said anything it counted, for it was to the point. "An experience which we had at the conference in Trenton, Ohio in 1903 characterizes Brother Sprunger better than words could do it. When he arrived, he hobbled painfully with crutches, for he had fallen and broken his hip, which ever afterward pained him. He said to us: "Brethren, this is the last time I come to the conference." After he had been taken to his place during conference, the writer said, "Who believes that Brother Sprunger is here for the last time?" 

No one believed it. ""During conference a song was missing on the program. 

Sprunger with his crutches came to the front as a member of a quartette. In the evening, a few minutes before the time of service, news came that the speaker of the evening could not be there in time. What was to be done? Who can take the place? "Brother Sprunger, can you help us out?" "I

can, if you want me to do so." He preached a German sermon, which he had heard in English in Winona. After the service 

Brother H. J. Krehbiel said to the writer: "I heard that sermon at the same time Brother Sprunger heard it, but Brother Sprunger's sermon was by far better than the original!" ""When we were at the station ready to leave, the writer said: "Who did the most work in the conference?" The unanimous answer was, "S. F. Sprunger!" "Do you believe that this was the last time that Brother Sprunger will attend conference?" "No! certainly not!" And eight years later 1911, he was in Wadsworth, and spoke in English, which was not easy for him, but what he thought necessary he did to the best of his ability and he succeeded. From Wadsworth he went to the General Conference in Bluffton, Ohio, which was the last conference he attended.'"

After he resigned from the Board he was made an honorary member. "Much more material might be gathered and many anecdotes collected to illustrate Rev. Sprunger's activities in congregation and conferences; from personal reminiscences of his older parishioners recalling personal dealings with them in solving personal and church difficulties; in conferences often in heated debates where forensic sparks fairly flew between 

Sprunger, Goerz and Krehbiel, and so on and on; but all this would only accentuate what has already been told herein and would unduly lengthen this discourse without adding essentially new laurels to Brother Sprunger's crown for our appreciation of his eminent service. ""The teachers shall shine as the brightness of the firmament and they that turn many to righteousness as the stars for ever and ever.' These words of the prophet furnished the most suitable text possible to the funeral sermon for Rev. 

Sprunger and his obituary. The concourse of people that on that occasion crowded the great church to its doors and the tears that flowed at that funeral were an eloquent indication of how all his flock and his many friends from outside that at-

BIOGRAPHIES 

221 tended it valued the life of exalted service of this apostle of the Christ." "His contributions to the church may be summarized as follows: implanting of the missionary spirit, promotion of temperance, morality, and Bible knowledge. Especially was the church music elevated and the interest of the young people aroused through his leadership in song and choral work. "He was a well read man, nobility of character distinguished him; Punctuality, Faith, good Judgment, inborn Leadership, fearless correction of erring brethren-these are the indelible marks of his consecrated personality." 

He was always interested in new inventions. We well remember the time when airplanes first made their appearance. A visitor in the home remarked that he did not believe in the possibility of airplanes "coming into their own." Rev. 

Sprunger, on the other hand thought it not only possible but highly probable and added that he hoped he could ride in one before he died. This wish, however, was not fulfilled. 

Rev. Sprunger was not well the last twenty years of his life. He suffered an injury to an eye which necessitated an operation in 1902, and early in 1903 he suffered another injury, when late one Wednesday afternoon he fell, while chasing some chickens, and fractured a hip. He never fully recovered his health and ever after had to use a cane.

He preached his last sermon September 14, 1919 from the text John 10, 28. His last marriage ceremony was performed on August 29, 1920, though he was often asked to officiate after that. He suffered long and intensely and he wished to die. 

At last, after many years of weary waiting the end came, on November 16, 1923. The immediate cause of his death was pneumonia.

He was the last to go out of his father's household and the first to go out of his own. 

Rev. and Mrs. Sprunger had ten children, Wilhelmina, Oswin, Bernard, Theodora (Baumgartner), Gustav, Hulda (Yoder), Metta (Talle), Dorcas, Eva, Oda (Staley). The widow and children still survive.

PETER M. NEUENSCHWANDER 

Peter M. Neuenschwander was another one of the early preachers. He was born September 15, 1854 on "der Geiss

Gemeinde Subo," Oberamt Münster, Canton Bern, Switzerland. 

He was elected as "Prediger zum Buch" at the early age of seventeen, in September, 1871, when lot was held at Morung, Canton Bern. He was install ed by Elder David Nussbaum. 

The other candidates were 

John Baumgartner, Christian 

Moser, Jacob Amstutz, and two young men by the name of John Amstutz. 

Mr. Neuenschwander came to America with other members of his family in 1876, arriving in Wayne County, Ohio, on May 24. Shortly after, on 

August 3, some of them came on to Berne, Peter M. among them, and he became assistant to Rev. Christian Sprunger. 

Peter M. Neuenschwander 

When the 1879 church was built Peter would have nothing to do with it, for it was much too stylish, so he left the congregation and organized one of his own, comprised of his own household and relatives and a few others, and they held their meetings in a small building on what was long known as the Schwiezer Schwander farm one half mile west of Berne. He was ordained as elder in the Neuenschwander church in May, 1883, by Rev. Christian Sommer of Wayne County, Ohio.

He has not changed much as far as ideas are concerned; he is still adamant, still of the opinion that S. F. Sprunger

BIOGRAPHIES 

223 did wrong in adopting a more liberal church policy. He told the writer only recently that S. F. Sprunger should never have done what he did. 

Mr. Neuenschwander was married on November 9, 1882 to Katherine C., daughter of Mr. and Mrs. John C. Nussbaum.

For three years they lived with his parents west of Berne, then they settled on a farm of their own a few miles southeast of 

Berne. They had eleven children, four of whom died as infants or at an early age. Mrs. Neuenschwander passed away March 2, 1900. 

He still lives on the home place with a son and two daughters, who now own the farm, and continues to preach to about five families every other Sunday, in the homes of the members. 

JOHN W. KLIEWER 

John Walter Kliewer was born June 8, 1869 in Michalin, near Berdichieff, Russia, the son of John P. and Aganetha Foth 

Kliewer. On November 12, 1874 he came to America with his parents, settling in Harvey County, Kansas. He received his education in the rural schools, the Newton, Kansas high school and the Mennonite Seminary at Halstead, Kansas. After teaching a few years he attended Bethel College, and, having finished the courses then given there he again taught, this time two years in the grades at Halstead. Then he was elected to the high school principalship there, but resigned to take up a position in the Bethel Academy. 

In the year 1898 he entered Garrett Biblical Institute at Evanston, Illinois. While there he was asked to help in the 

Middle District Conference work. He supplied in Trenton, Ohio while Rev. H. J. Krehbiel travelled in behalf of Bluffton

College. For a while he also made bi-weekly trips to Woodburn, Indiana to serve the congregation there. In the summer of 1901 he came to Berne and taught "German School." He was graduated from Garrett in 1901. At that time there was a scarcity of workers in the denomination and three positions were offered him: the editorship of The Mennonite, the posi-

tion as evangelist of the General Conference to succeed Rev. 

J. B. Baer, and the pastorate of the Wadsworth, Ohio church. 

He accepted the last named offer, and later, on October 6, 1901, was ordained as elder in Wadsworth by Rev. Ephraim 

Hunsberger, in the presence of Rev. A. A. Sommer and Rev. 

N. C. Hirschy. 

In 1903 Rev. Kliewer accepted the call to the Mennonite Church in Berne. He was very reserved by nature, he was not one to make friends easily, and at first his manner 

Iwas not understood. It was he as much as any one who urged the building of the new church, and it was during his pastorate that English services. were begun. There were also other noteworthy changes during his pastorate from 1903-1911. 

On October 30, 1902 he was married to Emma, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. John W. 

Ruth, in the Mennonite 

Church at Halstead, Kansas, by Rev. Christian Krehbiel. 

Rev. and Mrs. Kliewer had three children, Karl, Ruth and 

Paul, all born in Berne. 

Rev. John W. Kliewer

He left Berne August 3, 1911 to take up the presidency of Bethel College at Newton, Kansas. He served in that capacity for nine years, also as pastor of the College Church. In 1920 he resigned as president of the school to be sent as inspector to the Mennonite foreign fields. This work and the subsequent visits among the churches to report his findings occupied a little over two years.

After returning from his missionary trip he again took

BIOGRAPHIES 

225 up the active work as pastor of the Bethel College Church, but resigned in July of 1925 to resume the presidency of the College. In 1925 he received the honorary degree of Doctor of Divinity, both from Garrett Biblical Institute and from Witmarsum Seminary. In 1932 he retired from the presidency of the College because of ill health.

Dr. Kliewer, until his illness, was considered one of the foremost leaders in the Mennonite Church. He served as secretary of the General Conference from 1905-1908, as president of the Board for Foreign Missions from 1908-1935. He was president of the Board of Trustees of Bluffton College in 1908. He served for a number of years before and after that as a member of the Board. He also served as a trustee of 

Bethel College, as president of the Western District Conference, and as vice-president of the Federal Council of Churches. 

Dr. Kliewer was the author of "Letters on a Trip Around the World" and "Memoirs of J. W. Kliewer or, From Herdboy to College President."

He passed away February 9, 1938 after having suffered several strokes of paralysis. Mrs. Kliewer passed away from pneumonia, January 2, 1935.

The following tribute to Dr. Kliewer as it appeared in the 

Bethel College Bulletin of September 1, 1936 was written by 

Walter E. Ewert. No doubt, few of us have been familiar with this interesting picture of Dr. Kliewer, so we are happy to present it here. "In the quaint little country where the wooden shoes clunked and the squat windmills turned lazily in the breeze the Kliewers lived, planned and dug canals that kept the heavy waves of the sea away. "Being experts in draining land, even though the surname denotes it is derived from the book-binder or 'Kleber' trade, the Kliewer forefathers went to Northeastern Prussia to keep the waters of the bulging Vistula River from flooding the free city of Danzig. "Many leagues to the east in Moscow lived the Empress of Russia, Katherine, with her regal entourage. She had

heard of the hard working Mennonites living in Germany and invited them to settle in Southern Russia and till the fertile fields there. A group of Danzig Mennonites, including the 

Kliewer family, left in the latter part of the eighteenth century and migrated to the locality of the Holy City of Kiev in 

Southern Russia. 

"Years of prosperity passed in review and then rumors and threats came from Moscow in the seventies that the military exemption under which the Mennonites had lived would be withdrawn. The Kliewers left their thatch-roofed homes in 1874 and came to America on the 'Vaterland.' Among them a five-year-old youngster watched the ocean waves starry-eyed, and proved himself a good sailor. The young Kliewer boy was immune to seasickness. And it was an omen that when he should be full-grown the ocean voyages he was to take would hold no qualms for him. "Arriving in Kansas by rail the Kliewers, with some other 

Mennonite families, settled near Peabody. "The open plains of the Sunflower State in that time were not enclosed and farmers would chase their cattle together and have some one herd them through the summer. At eleven years the young immigrant lad became a cowherd. As soon as winter came the cows had to be separated and the individual farmers would get the cattle they owned. With the cattle having grown wild during the summer months the corraling of the cattle was a tricky task and often times it irked the patience of the farmers astride their stock horses. But young cowboy Kliewer with his light and quick-footed pony would dash among the herd and easily single out the cattle the respective farmers owned. "Even during winter our cowhand looked after cattle. 

Today Dr. Kliewer remembers he worked one winter for a 

Mr. Melvin Haas, herding cattle while snow lay among the corn stalks. As he grew older, in summer he worked on threshing rigs and watched Turkey red wheat, the seed of

BIOGRAPHIES 

227 which the Mennonites had brought to Kansas for the first time, flow in golden streams into sacks. "He recalls that in pioneer days the grasshoppers were such a scourge in Kansas that they ravaged the corn fields so badly they devoured the corn to the cob. "Dr. Kliewer's mother died when he was eight years old.

In speaking of his mother he says that in his early youth she emphasized the importance of truth to him and says of her, 'I have often been grateful for this advice of mother's when I was forced to get along with people of quite different viewpoints.' "Wanderlust,' that malady that creeps upon youth so often, had fixed itself upon the youth and with a cousin he made up his mind to travel to the Pacific Coast. But when he informed his pastor of his intentions the pastor persuaded him to drop the adventure and enroll instead in Halstead 

Academy at Halstead, Kansas. "He attended the Halstead school, little dreaming that some day this wooden frame building would develop into a college of which he would act as president for many years. 

Later he enrolled at the Garrett Biblical Institute at Evanston, Illinois, where he graduated with the degree of Bachelor of Sacred Theology. Some years later this school also gave him the honorary degree of Doctor of Divinity." "In 1911 Dr. Kliewer came to Bethel College at Newton, Kansas, as president, also taking over the duties of pastor of the Bethel College Church. 

"War was wreaking havoc in Europe in 1917 and in that year Dr. Kliewer was elected president of the Mennonite General Conference. The Mennonite General Conference feared that the Federal Council of Churches to which it belonged would begin to sway with the spreading of war propaganda and preach the cause of war from the pulpit. Due to this

fear the Conference instructed Dr. Kliewer to announce to the 

Federal Council the withdrawal of the Mennonite Church; but 

Dr. Kliewer resolutely refused to convey the decision and thereupon also refused the presidency of the Conference. He strongly urged that the Mennonites at this crucial moment should remain in the Federal Council and concentrate their efforts to swing the Council members in favor of peace, which was in harmony with Mennonite principles and tenets. "Dr. Kliewer also served as a member of the Peace Committee of the General Conference during the World War. "Respected highly for his sane and uncompromising stand on peace Dr. Kliewer, after the Armistice, was asked to speak at a Harvey County (county in which Bethel College and Newton are located) memorial service for those who had died in the war. It was a ticklish situation. A friend admonished him not to make the speech pointing out that it was a trap set to catch him with his peace policy. Another advised him to speak. Dr. Kliewer says, 'I spoke according to my convictions, realizing that there were many parents in my audience who wished they could have stopped the war before their sons had fallen victims of it.' The audience was sympathetic. Later the address appeared in the local newspaper." "After the World War Dr. Kliewer toured the world as president of the Mission Board in the interest of foreign missions." "While in India he attended baptismal services and witnessed ninety-six converts baptized. As a souvenir of that event he today carries a cane which one of the natives carved from the jungle wood where the baptism took place. "On the same trip Dr. Kliewer also visited the Island of

Java, and attended the largest Mennonite Church in the world in Holland. "Dr. Kliewer tells of the time he met that fiery feminine

BIOGRAPHIES 

229 fighter of fire water-Carrie Nation-in the Union Station in 

Kansas City. Carrie Nation not only had a habit of crushing whisky bottles in saloons with an ax, but she heaped anathema upon tobacco as well. While in the Union Station, Dr. Kliewer clearly recalls, Carrie glanced about and noticing several men smoking she reached over to one and with gusto snatched his pipe from between his teeth and pitched it away with a look of haughty contempt. "Dr. Kliewer's health began to fail in the last few years and he was forced to resign as president of Bethel College, pastor of the Bethel College Church, and the presidency of the Mennonite Mission Board. In these various capacities his analytical thinking, quick decisions and forcefulness of expression won the admiration and respect of thousands. "As a vigorous exponent of the truths of Christianity and his inspiring leadership and service to the Mennonite Church he has left his indelible imprint in the memories of countless persons. Bethel College under his devoted presidency grew and flourished. In 1911 when he became its president there were only fourteen students enrolled in the college department. By 1920 there were one hundred eleven in the college branch. During his second term as president from 1925 to 

1932 the college enrollment ranged from two hundred eightynine to three hundred sixty-seven. In 1926 the total enrollment including the academy was four hundred eighty-three. "Recently he donated his entire private library of two thousand volumes to the college. The collection represents an accumulation of careful gleanings of a life time from the best literature in the field of religious and ethical thought. 

Of its inspiring values Dr. Kliewer's lectures and sermons have constantly given striking evidence. For this generous gift of volumes the two storage rooms immediately below the stone steps of the entrance to the main building have beer. completely white-finished, decorated, and equipped with shelves and lights. Friends are invited to see this treasure room when visiting the campus."

PETER R. SCHROEDER 

Pastor of the Berne Church 1912 to 1928, the subject of this sketch was born at Mountain Lake, Minnesota, August 

22, 1888. Fishing, skating, chopping wood for mother, herding cattle on the school section for Grandfather Fries en, and later serving at the local switchboard in the first telephone office of his home town, were boyhood activities. 

He remembers staying at the switchboard one Christmas day when the parents with the rest of the family made their annual bob sleigh trip to "Grandma's" for peanuts and presents. His reward was a train trip to St. Paul on father's next visit to the twin cities. He "hired out" to farmers several summers to help make hay, plow, and thresh. Fifteen blisters on the hands at the end of his first day's threshing is a stinging memory. 

Rev. Peter R. Schroeder

He made the eight grades in regulation time. He still wears his thirty-seven-year-old Elgin watch-graduation present from his father and preserves the graduation photograph, the whole group with diplomas in hand and the floor covered with congratulatory flower baskets. 

Next to the boyhood influence of a godly mother and the life-long attendance at the Bethel Sunday School and church, the two years spent at the Mountain Lake German School under Teacher J. J. Balzer were the determining factors in moulding his viewpoint and faith. Two more years at the newly built local public school made him a high school grad-

BIOGRAPHIES 

231 uate with a rural school teacher's certificate for good measure. 

While serving District Number 32 as school master in 1906-7, he made the seven miles daily with a rented horse and buggy through snow and mud and sleet. Miss Anna Hiebert, school mistress teaching three miles nearer town, took advantage of this means of conveyance for her daily trips. 

When J. A. Funk decided to leave Mountain Lake in 1904 in order to make Waldheim, Saskatchewan, his future home, he cast about for someone to take his Sunday School class of boys in the Bethel Church. He lit upon young Schroeder, then only about fifteen years old, freshly converted and baptized by Elder H. H. Regier. This was one of the footprints of the living God in the life of the exceedingly young prospective Sunday School teacher. His decision for Christ, brought about by a conversation with Teacher Balzer that spring on the German School stairway, was to be strengthened by definite work in the Lord's harvest field right at home. 

The inner conflicts coming in the later teen age years were closely related to the practices of the community in which the youth was at home. Questions of honesty and truthfulness, attitudes toward the dance, the saloon, the cigarette, moral purity, cards, and the like, had to be faced and would have ruined the lad, had it not been for a marvelous overruling Providence that thwarted the enemy's plans at every turn in answer to the mother's fervent prayers and Peter's own hunger and thirst after righteousness.

A most trying temptation came when the offer of a position as clerk in a local bank promised a possibility of money making. However, the deep impression of a call to become a foreign missionary, made upon Schroeder by the vivid addresses of visiting missionaries in his early boyhood years, outweighed all arguments in favor of the bank position. 

After trying the teaching profession during the winter of 1906-7, the young man packed his trunk for college. Bethel 

College near Newton, Kansas, was home to him for three years, 1907-10. Besides studying he helped organize a men's glee club, and became its amateur conductor until the music

department saw fit to fill this important position with a capable faculty member. He earned part of his expenses by conducting a weekly singing school in the Truesdale school (now Zimmerdale) with a large group of eager Old Mennonite lads and lassies. This, too, was continued until the college music department saw fit to protect the reputation of the college against too amateurish representation in the surrounding territory. 

On Christmas day 1907, young Schroeder preached his first sermon in the Bruderthal Church, near Hillsboro, Kansas. But the high point in those first three college years was his appointment as representative of the College to the sixth International 

Student Volunteer Convention at Rochester, N. Y., in the

New Year week of 1910. For weeks after his return reports on Convention messages were laid before the student body. 

That summer a call from the Evangelization Committee of the Northern District Conference to Driscoll, North Dakota, made several changes in the young college student's plans. He felt the need of earning something before attempting his last College year, and this call would be a splendid opportunity of doing definite Christian work while earning the necessary money. Moreover, he had an intimate friend with whom he had spent almost all of his school years. With her he had worked his arithmetic problems, in company with her he had wheeled a number of his younger brothers and sisters in the family go-cart along the wooden sidewalks of old Mountain 

Lake. It was Susie Nickel, daughter of Theodore Nickel the clothier. She was the oldest of a family of nine while young 

Schroeder was the third of a family of ten. So there often were two go-carts on the sidewalk. The friendship of the two deepened early into an old-fashioned courtship. She had been. at Bethel 1908-10, and so he asked her to go to North Dakota with him. The wedding day was September 1, 1910. Eight months were spent in a quaint sod house in North Dakota, fifteen miles north of Driscoll. The young minister's time was occupied with teaching a rural school, preaching and pastoral work. The next summer was spent at Langham and Wald-

BIOGRAPHIES 

233 heim, Saskatchewan, under the inspiring leadership of the pioneer home missionary N. F. Toews.

When the General Conference convened in Beatrice, Nebraska in September, 1908, a call was given for volunteers for the foreign field.-Many young people responded to that callamong whom were Susie Nickel and P. R. Schroeder. (Editor's Note)

August 1911 found the two at the Bluffton, Ohio, General 

Conference of the Mennonite Church of North America. Being volunteers for the foreign mission field field they were asked to sing a duet. Its theme was, "Christian, are you up and doing?" According to a statement made later by the veteran minister of the Berne Mennonite Church, Elder Samuel F. 

Sprunger, it was the singing of this song that suggested to him the idea of inviting young Schroeder to assist in the Berne Church until a pastor could be found. J. W. Kliewer had resigned, and the aged Sprunger had stepped in at the request of the church pending the permanent filling of the vacancy. 

Schroeder and his wife in the meantime had returned to Bethel College, where he was to finish the college course and she the five year High School Bible course. The call came in the midst of their school work. The Board of Foreign Missions by whom they had been accepted as candidates, possibly for India, consented to the temporary change of plans. So on the 8th of June, 1912 the two stepped from the train at 

Berne to assist Elder Sprunger for a short while. Several times the congregation asked them to extend their stay, finally at the 1913 annual business meeting voting upon recommendation of Elder Sprunger to have Schroeder ordained as Elder and pastor without a definite time limit to his term of service. 

He was ordained January 25, 1914 by the aged shepherd of the flock and remained until July 31, 1928.

The ten years since leaving Berne have been the busiest in Schroeder's life. For two years he served as president of 

Freeman College. He retired from that office in 1930 and within a few weeks came a call from the South Church, seven and one-half miles from Freeman, to take the place vacated by

Pastor W. S. Gottshall. Like the call to Berne eighteen years earlier, this also was to continue only until the church should find a permanent minister. 

Within three months Schroeder was requested to reconsider his resignation when the Board desired to reinstate him in the College, at least as instructor. With this recall to the school Schroeder carried double work. He taught in the 

Bible department of the School from 1930 to 1936. He served as acting pastor of the South or Salem Church until 1932 when he was called as regular pastor for a term of three years, with the commodious parsonage of the church offered to the family for a home. He has since then been re-elected twice for three-year terms, the last to expire in 1941. 

In 1933 he was elected president of the General Conference at Bluffton, Ohio, and re-elected at Upland, California, in 1935. With his chairmanship at the twenty-eighth session of the Conference at Saskatoon July 31 to August 7, 1938, his service in this office terminated, being ineligible for re-election by provision of the constitution. By virtue of this office he was sent to Amsterdam, Netherlands, in 1936 as representative at the Third World Mennonite Congress. His relation to the

General Conference has been an intimate one for many years. 

Beginning with the 1905 session at Mountain Lake when he was still attending high school there, he has been present at every intervening session. One term he was secretary of the 

Conference. For thirteen years he was on the Board of Education, six years he was a member of the Committee on Doctrine and Conduct. He has preached a total of about 3500 sermons in the churches of which he has been pastor and in many other churches and communities in seventeen states of the United States, in Canada, and in Europe. 

In recent years with the work of conference and evangelism and pastorate increasing, the teaching work at Freeman College had to be relinquished. On a number of occasions his assistance was requested in the settlement of internal difficulties in struggling congregations. In numerous offices in the Middle District Conference in former years and in the

BIOGRAPHIES 

235 

Northern District Conference in the last ten years Schroeder has asserted his loyalty to the Living and the Written Word and he is now praying for and looking forward to a deep spiritual awakening in the Church where he is pastor. 

The home life of the Schroeder family has been a delightful one, with many experiences peculiar to the parsonage and the pastor's life. In the twenty-eight years of their married life the Schroeders have lived in North Dakota, Saskatchewan, Kansas, Indiana, and South Dakota, in fourteen houses. The first was a sod house in North Dakota, two were their own homes, and two were lovely parsonages provided by the congregations they served. They have four daughters, Celeste, Vernelle, Louise, and Esther Ruth, all born at Berne. Their parents love them dearly and have entrusted them to the Lord.

They have attended school a total of fifty-six years. The two oldest have graduated from Wheaton College, Louise has graduated from Freeman Junior College and Esther Ruth from the High School at Freeman College. The girls have been helping each other through college by teaching school. This program is to continue the next few years. Vernelle has been accepted as a missionary candidate for India. Their mother belongs to the class of Proverbs 31. It is estimated that the 

Standard sewing machine has saved the family a thousand dollars in a quarter of a century. But the house wife has also been a loyal matron of the parsonage. She has left the imprint of a faithful missionary vision upon the women's societies in whose service she has been active. For over twenty years she has filled her place in the Women's Missionary organization of General Conference. Her work as Sunday School teacher has been accepted by the Holy Spirit in supplying spiritual nurture to those attending. The three older girls have taught in half a dozen or more Summer Bible Schools. 

The family is deeply interested in Music. The girls have all had a part in musical organizations that sang the gospel story. 

Celeste and Vernelle have each made two trips with the 

Wheaton girls' glee club. Louise made a two-thousand mile trip with the Freeman College chorus. Esther Ruth sang with

the sextette on the WNAX Sunday afternoon broadcasts for Freeman College. At family prayers every member takes part. 

The testimony of the whole Schroeder family echoes the double blessedness of the high calling of the minister. The family is unanimous in its conviction that the gospel is that story and doctrine which is recorded in the Word of God, and that another gospel is no gospel. "Jesus Christ is the same yesterday and today, yea and forever." Hebrews 13:8.

CORNELIUS H. SUCKAU 

John Suckau, father of Cornelius, left West Prussia in 1876, came over to this country, and settled on a farm near Newton, Kansas. Here he was united in marriage to Marie 

Andres, who had preceded him to this country. To this union were born four children: John, Cornelius, Marie and Anna.

Cornelius, the subject of this sketch, was born January 23, 1881.

In 1888 John Suckau, accompanied by his wife and family, returned to Germany, at the request of his father, to take charge of the parental estate, Thörigthof. During their stay in Germany Cornelius attended die Volks-Schule with his brother John. In 1890 John Suckau decided to return to

America for he realized that his democratic method of work in the fields was displeasing to his father and misunderstood by a community in which it was not customary for land owners, themselves, to do manual labor.

On their return the family settled in Newton, where Cornelius entered the German Lutheran Parochial School. 

After three years of German schooling he entered the public school to complete his elementary education. He took his high school work at the Bethel College Academy at Newton, Kansas.

After graduation he apprenticed himself to the Rev. H. P. 

Krehbiel, to learn the trade of printing. Then he turned to teaching and taught German in several Mennonite localities. 

It was while teaching school that the definite call came to him

BIOGRAPHIES 

237 

Rev. Cornelius H. Suckau to enter mission work. He began immediately to prepare himself for this work by taking the Bible Course then offered in Bethel College, Newton, Kansas, and later by taking three years of training in Bible and medicine at the Union Mis sionary Training Institute of Brooklyn, New York. 

On June 24, 1909 he was married to Lulu Johnson who was a graduate of the same school. 

In August both he and his wife were ordained as mis-

sionaries. They entered the mission field on November 6, 1909. For nearly a year they were in charge of the Government Leper Asylum at Chandkuri and also of the Leper Asylum of the Evangelical Mission of that place. In 1914 they were appointed to open the mission station at Korba, C. P., India. 

While in India two children were born to them: Theodore on August 21, 1910 and Edna on April 17, 1914.

In 1920 they returned to America for their first furlough, having labored in the field for ten and a half years. During this furlough Mr. Suckau continued his studies at Bethel College, Newton, Kansas, and was graduated with the A.B. degree in 1921. 

In the fall of the same year the family returned to India for another term of service at Korba. 

During his ministry in India Mr. Suckau served for two terms as president of the Indian Worker's Conference, and as president of the Missionary Conference.

Weakened in health because of malaria to which he had been subject throughout his stay in India, he returned with his family to America in 1928. Upon his arrival he received a call to become the supply pastor of the First Mennonite Church of Berne, Indiana. After having consulted the Mission Board and a physician he accepted the call and began his duties on September 7, 1928. In January 1930 the congregation elected him as their permanent pastor in which capacity he has been serving since that time.

Throughout his ministry Rev. Suckau has been ably assisted by his wife. His son, Theodore, is at present teaching in the field of mathematics at the Ohio State University, Columbus, Ohio. His daughter, Edna, is teacher of English at the Monroe High School, Monroe, Indiana. "The lines are fallen unto me in pleasant places; yea, I have a goodly heritage. I will bless the Lord, who hath given me counsel." Psalm 16:6, 7a.

239 

ISAAC A. SOMMER 

Isaac A. Sommer, the son of Abraham and Elisabeth Lehman Sommer was born January 17, 1851 in the Sonnenberg Settlement near Dalton, Ohio. His early years were spent at home on the farm, getting what education he could in the short sessions of the parochial and public schools. He joined the Sonnenberg Mennonite Church as a young man. When he was eighteen years old he went to Wadsworth where he attended the Mennonite College for a year. After teaching school for several years he again attended the college at Wadsworth for three years, after which he resumed teaching. He taught in the communities of 

Wayne and Allen 

Counties, Ohio, in 

Kansas and

Berne. 

and in 

In 1878 he toured Europe, no doubt during the summer months. In 1883, when Missionary 

Haury was sick, he, upon request of the 

Board, temporarily assisted in the mission work at Cantonment, Oklahoma.

Rev. I. A. Sommer 

In the spring of 1884 he accepted the editorship of the "Jugendfreund," published by the Mennonite Publishing Company of Elkhart, Indiana. In the fall of the same year, at the session of the Central Conference held in Berne, he received

and accepted the call to become editor of the "Bundesbote." 

About two years later he also began editing the "Kinderbote." 

Later, in 1905, when Rev. H. G. Allebach was compelled to give up the editorship of "The Mennonite," on account of poor health, Mr. Sommer also accepted the editorship of that paper. 

He came to Berne at the age of thirty-three and the greater part of his remaining years were spent here. During the years of his editorial work he made his home with the S. F. Sprunger family where he and his close friend, S. F. Sprunger, shared the same study. It was during those years that he established a wide reputation in his services among the Mennonite churches throughout America. He also served on committees in both the General and Middle District Conferences. 

On January 3, 1887 the congregation here gave him a call to the ministry. This was not done by lot as in former years, but all those who approved of the call signified by standing vote. He was ordained on October 2, 1887 by S. F. Sprunger, and served faithfully as his assistant for many years. It was usually the case that when Rev. Sommer was scheduled to preach that everyone could and did expect a special sermon for the children, for he seemed to have a special love for them and enjoyed talking to them and they in turn enjoyed hearing him. He always had interesting stories to tell. 

By 1911 he discovered that the burden of three papers was too much for his strength and he was compelled to give up all editorial work. After he had improved in health, he spent some time traveling for the Mennonite Book Concern, canvassing for subscriptions for the religious papers of which he was formerly the editor. He also sold religious books. While in 

Canada he suffered a severe breakdown from which he never fully recovered. Later, he went to Glendale, California, where he spent two years with his brother, Rev. A. A. Sommer, for the benefit of his health. From there he often sent interesting contributions to The Mennonite.

Rev. Sommer was probably the first to bring forth the idea of an All-Mennonite Conference. In preparation for this he once prepared for The Mennonite a diagram showing the inter-

BIOGRAPHIES 

241 relation of the various branches of the churches to each other. 

He was active in the church in many ways. He was one of the pioneers in the church in working for Temperance. His work in connection with the Sunday school and library was also of great value. He taught the Normal Class, was a member of the Library Committee and for many years served as librarian. He shared S. F. Sprunger's enthusiasm for missions and contributed liberally to the work. His contributions to the Publications, and the Colleges of the Conference were also always liberal.

During his late years in Berne he made his home with Mr. and Mrs. Isaac Lehman and shared a study with Rev. C. H. 

A. van der Smissen, his successor as editor of the "Bundesbote." His great interest in publications endured to the end. 

His last contribution for publication was an appeal for assistance for distressed Russian Mennonites. He often preached from the pulpit here and elsewhere, being one of several who served the Woodburn congregation from time to time from 1898-1901, but his greatest work was wrought as editor of the church publications, his greatest work was accomplished with his pen.

After the death of his close friend and co-worker, S. F. 

Sprunger (in November 1923), Rev. Sommer remarked, "His death has the effect of making me lonesome and sick." He seemed to decline rapidly in health in the months following, but his condition was not serious until two weeks before he died. His age at death March 19, 1924 was seventy-three years, two months and two days. 

Mr. Sommer was never married.

C. H. A. VAN DER SMISSEN 

Carl Heinrich Anton, the only living son of Rev. and Mrs. 

Carl Justus van der Smissen, was born December 4, 1851, in

Friederichstadt, Schleswig Holstein, Germany. When his par ents and two sisters, Wilhelmine and Hillegonda, (the oldest sister was married and stayed in Germany), came to America in 1868, Carl Heinrich had not completed his studies, so he

did not come until June 19, 1874. Several months later, on 

October 15, he was ordained into the ministry, in Ashland, Ohio, by his father, assisted by Rev. Ephraim Hunsberger, (fatherin-law of N. C. Hirschy).

He was married October 12, 1881 to Mary Elizabeth Knight. 

Two children, Carl and Alieda, were born to this union. His wife died February 13, 1892. The following year, on June 18, he married Elizabeth, daughter of Daniel B. and Maria Berger 

Ruth. There are seven children by the second marriage: Hillegonda Cornelia, Wilhelmine Frederica, Theodore Alwin, Elizabeth 

Katherine, Henrich Roland, Edna Bertha and Ruth Margaret. 

Rev. van der Smissen continued actively in the ministry until he assumed the editorship of the Bundesbote and Kinderbote in 

1911. It was in December of that year that he and his family moved to Berne. 

During his residence in 

Berne, Rev. van der Smissen took a keen interest in the work of the church. He served as a Sunday School 

Rev. C. H. A. van der Smissen teacher from 1912 to 1932 and supplied in the pulpit on various occasions.

In 1920 he also accepted the editorship of the German Sunday School Quarterly which S. F. Sprunger had edited up to that time. He still edits the Quarterly which is printed in 

10,000 copies. In 1931 he relinquished the editorship of the 

Bundesbote and in 1936 that of the Kinderbote.
